	REFERENCE TYPE
	[] === []; -> false
	[1] === [1]; -> false
	var object1 = { value: 10 };
	var object2 = object1;
	var object3 = { value: 10 };
	object1 === object2; -> true
	object1 === object3; -> false
	object1.value = 15;
	object2.value; -> 15;
	object3.value; -> 10;
 
	Up until this point, all the oher JavaScript types that 	we've learned, are defined by the programming 	language. The person who wrote the language, tells you 	what they are, they are a primitive type. Now reference 	type, which are non primitive types, are not defined by 	the language. They are created by the programmer.
	When we say var number1 = 1; JavaScript knows what one 	is, it's a number, it's a primitive type, it's always the same 	thing, it's immutable, and every time it uses the one, 	thats the same one JavaScript knows about. 
	When we created the three different objects, those are 	reference types, they are created by the programmer. 	JavaScript doesn't know what object1 is until we tell it 	what to create. We say create a box and call it object1, 	and put value: 10 in the box. Then we say, any time we 	say object2, we reference object1, the box, that holds the 	value: 10 inside. When object3 gets created, it's a new 	object and creates a new box object3, with value: 10 	inside it as well. The object name, or variable, is simply a 	reference to the address of the object, which inside of it 	has the value: 10. It's like the object is the house, and the 	name of the object is the address, and the house within 	it, has all these properties and values.
	Arrays are just objects at the end of the day.
	CONTEXT
	Context vs Scope
	function b() {
		let a = 4;
	}
	console.log(a); -> Uncaught ReferenceError: a is not 	defined. 
	This is scope. The function and only the function is the 	scope of the a variable, which means only inside the 	function knows what a is referencing.
	Context is different in that it tells you where you are 	within the object. If we say:
	console.log(this); -> window - window object
	console.log(this === window); -> true
	this.alert("hello"); -> alerts "hello"
	"THIS" MEANS WHAT IS THE OBJECT ENVIRONMENT 	THAT WE'RE IN RIGHT NOW.
	What is to the left of the dot.
	alert(); is possible because we're inside the window{} 	object.
	Like window.alert();
	This, just refers to what object it's inside of.
	function a() {
		console.log(this);
	}
	a(); -> window - window object
	The function does not change the object that "this" is 	invoked in, therefore it is still referencing the window 	object that the function is inside of. Functions have their 	own scope for variables, but "this" references objects. Object 	scope if you will.
	const object4 ={
		a: function() {
			console.log(this);
		}
	}
	object4.a(); -> {a: f()} - "this" now refers to the object4 	object which contains the a key and function value.
	Why is that important to us?
	It's really important when we do...
	INStANTIATION
	Instantiation, is when you make a copy of an object, to 	reuse the code.
	Imagine you're making a big MMO game and this game 	is going to have many many players. You can have 	wizards and trolls and warlocks and elves. If you had to 	create an object for every single player, that's a lot of 	repeated code, that's a lot of hassle, and that's a lot of 	your time. 
	To solve that, you can use Instantiation. You're making 	instances, or multiple copies of an object.
	class Player
	It is common practice to capitalize the class, i.e. class 	Player.
	A class is something that you want to make a copy of.
	If I ever want to make a copy of an object, it's a good idea 	to do something like this.
	class Player {
		constructor(name, type) {
			this.name = name;
			this.type = type;
		}
	}
	Constructor, when you create a class, the Player class, 	says, every time I'm making a copy of Player, the first 	thing that gets run is the constructor function, and this 	constructor function, is going to create these properties 	on the Player object. Once this is executed, you can create 	anything that we want for the Player. E.g.
		introduce() {
			console.log(`Hi I am $(this.name), I'm a 				$(this.type)`)
		}
	Now by adding the introduce() method, the full class 	says any Player that is created will have these properties, 	and be able to introduce themselves with said properties.
	The reason we use "this" here is that when we create a 	"Player" we can access the name and type property, by 	referencing "this" object for said properties.
	In order to access the "Player" and make copies of it, 	you'll have to run a constructor which is all the properties 	and methods that you want the player to have. And you 	can alsocreate methods that Players can have. And any 	time you want to access a property within the constructor, 	use this.name or this.introduce();
	class Wizard extends Player {
		constructor(name, type) {
			super(name, type)
		}
		play() {
			console.log(`Weee, I'm a $(this.type)!`);
		}
	}
	I want Wizard to extend whatever Player has. I want to 	add on top of whatever "Player" has. 
	ANY time we extend something, we need to also call the 	constructor of the extendee, "Player". We have to do 	something called "super" to pass the properties that we 	want to pass to the constructor of the extender, Wizard. 	Super is used to essentially give you access to the 	properties of the extends Player object. Think SUPERUSER 	access.
	Now we can do this, instead of constantly creating 	Players and saying they have their Wizards and they can 	play, now we can say:
	const wizard1 = new Wizard("Shelly", "Healer");
	const wizard1 = new Wizard("Sean", "Dark Magic");
	class Player {
		constructor(name, type) {
			console.log(this);
			this.name = name;
			this.type = type;
		}
		introduce() {
			console.log(`Hi I am $(this.name), I'm a 				$(this.type)`)
		}
	}
	class Wizard extends Player {
		constructor(name, type) {
			super(name, type)
		}
		play() {
			console.log(`Weee, I'm a $(this.type)!`);
		}
	}
	const wizard1 = new Wizard("Shelly", "Healer"); ->
	Wizard {}
	const wizard2 = new Wizard("Sean", "Dark Magic"); ->
	Wizard{}
	We say, we have a "class", "Player", then a class "Wizard", 	that "extends", or uses as reference, Player. Then we have 	two variables that we create wizard1 and wizard2. The 	programs going to see the "new" keyword and any time it 	sees a "new" keyword, it says, we're creating a "new" 	Wizard, and this Wizard is getting passed two 	arguments, "Shelly" and "Healer". So it's going to go to the 	Wizard class, again, because it has this "new" word, it 	goes to this Wizard class and it says hey Wizard, I want 	you to extend Player, so it's going to go to this constructor 	and it's going to see "super" and what this does is it takes 	us up to the constructor of the Player and it runs the 	constructor execution block. Now it attaches "this" as the 	Wizard. Then it adds the introduce method and then 	adds the play method.
	wizard1.play(); -> Weee I'm a Healer!
	wizard1.introduce(); -> Hi I am Shelly, I'm a Healer
	wizard2.introduce(); -> Hi I am Sean, I'm a Dark Magic
	class Player {
		constructor(name, type) {
			console.log("Player", this);
			this.name = name;
			this.type = type;
		}
		introduce() {
			console.log(`Hi I am $(this.name), I'm a 				$(this.type)`)
		}
	}
	class Wizard extends Player {
		constructor(name, type) {
			console.log("Wizard", this)
			super(name, type)
		}
		play() {
			console.log(`Weee, I'm a $(this.type)!`);
		}
	}
	const wizard1 = new Wizard("Shelly", "Healer"); 
	Uncaught ReferenceError: Must call super constructor in 	derived class before accessing "this" or returning from 	derived constructor.
	This basically means that we have to run super in our 	constructor first, to then have access to "this" object.
		class Wizard extends Player {
		constructor(name, type) {
			super(name, type)									console.log("Wizard", this)
		}
		play() {
			console.log(`Weee, I'm a $(this.type)!`);
		}
	}
	const wizard1 = new Wizard("Shelly", "Healer"); ->
	Player > Wizard {}
	Wizard > Wizard {name: "Shelly", type: "Healer"}
	We log Player > Wizard {} first because when we call the 	Wizard constructor, it says super, which invokes the 	Player constructor, which runs it's console.log(), and 	then it goes to the Wizard console.log().
	This is new syntax, that you'll see in React and was a 	part of ES6.
	OLD WAY | CLASSICAL INHERITANCE
	var player = function(name, type) {
		this.name = name;
		this.type = type;
	}
	Player.prototype.introduce = function() {
		console.log(`Hi I am a $(this.name), I'm a 	$(this.type)`);
	}
	var wizard1 = new Player("Shelly",  "Healer");
	var wizard2 = new Player("Sean", "Dark Magi");
	wizard1.play = function() {
		console.log(`WEEE I'm a $(this.type)`);
	}
	wizard2.play = function() {
		console.log(`WEEE I'm a $(this.type)`);
	}
	When we use class, we're saying we want to make copies of 	something, and that's called Instantiation.
	When I use the "new" keyword, I'm saying make an 	instance of Wizard and use some functionality from 	Player, and adds it's own function as well.
166. Pass By Value vs Pass By Reference
	Primitive Types are immutable. We can't change them. In 	order to, we would need to remove the primitive type.
	Let's say var a = 5 well to change what that five value is I 	have to literally move it from memory and create 	something new, like a = 10. I can't really modify 5, just 	something new gets created. If we do var b = 10, same 	thing. They don't really know of each other's existence. 
	This is what we call Pass By Value.
	Objects on the other hand, are, Pass By Reference.
	var a = 5;
	var b = 10;
	A now has an address iif where this primitive value 5 sits 	in memory. B has the same for 10.
	var b = a;
	Primitive types are passed by value.
	b++;
	console.log(a); -> 5;
	console.log(b); -> 6;
	All the JavaScript engine did, was copy the primitive type 	value 5, as if I was doing b = 5, so now b has a reference 	to the value primitive type 5. All we did was copy the 	value.
	Memory Heap, or Stack, is where we store information, we 	just simply made a copy, they don't have any connect, 	which is what Pass By Value means. 
	Pass By Value means we copy that value, and create that 	value somewhere else in memory.
	let object1 = { name: 'Yao', password: '123' }
	let object2 = object1;
	object2.password = 'easypeasy'
	console.log(object1); -> { name: 'Yao', password: 	'easypeasy' }
	console.log(object2); -> { name: 'Yao', password: 	'easypeasy' }
	This is Pass By Reference. 
	Objects in JavaScript are stored in memory, and passed 	by reference, which means we don't copy the values like 	we do with primitive types.
	When we assigned object1 to object2 we simply said, hey 	this is where the object is in memory. 
	Object1 and object2 are both pointing somewhere in 	memory, we don't know where, to a box that contains this 	information.
	So when we do object2.password we are targeting the 	actual object at the address 'object2' which we have 	previously assigned as 'object1'. Its referencing this 	address. That's where Pass By Reference comes from.
	This is good because by having one object, we are saving 	space in memory.
	But this can also be bad because, unlike a primitive type, 	we might have this issue where somebody else changes a 	property on that referenced object. We need to be careful.
	var c = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
	var d = c;
	d.push(187628761);
	console.log(d); -> [ 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 187628761 ]
	console.log(c); -> [ 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 187628761 ]
	If we wanted to do a copy of this array instead of 	changing the actual array, we would do:
	var d = [].concat(c); 
	This will create a new array object, and add, or 	concatenate, the array c to it.
	Objects are a little bit more difficult.
	let obj = { a: 'a', b: 'b', c: 'c' }
	I want to copy this object, I want to have this piece of 	code, this object, in a different location in memory.
	let clone = Object.assign({}, obj);
	obj.c = 5;
	console.log(clone); -> { a: 'a', b: 'b', c: 'c' }
	let clone = obj; -> { a: 'a', b: 'b', c: '5' }
	Object declares the clone variable as an object and 	assigns it two parameters, the first being the value we 	want to store in clone itself, which is an empty object {}, 	the second being the value we want to pass into the 	object, obj here.
	SPREAD OPERATOR
	let obj = { 
		a: 'a', 
		b: 'b', 
		c: { 
			deep: 'Try and copy me' 
		}
	}
	let clone = Object.assign({}, obj);
	let clone2 = {...obj}; 
	obj.c.deep = 'hahaha';
	console.log(obj); -> { a: 'a', b: 'b', c: { deep: 'hahaha' }}
	console.log(clone); -> { a: 'a', b: 'b', c: { deep: 'hahaha' }}
	console.log(clone2); -> { a: 'a', b: 'b', c: { deep: 'hahaha' }}
	We cloned everything, but it got overwritten. What 	happened? EACH OBJECT gets passed by reference. EVEN 	the nested object.
	Although we cloned the initial object, it was a SHALLOW 	CLONE. So that is the memory address of the outer object, 	but within the shallow object is another object, a whole 	separate house at a whole new address if you will.
	So how do we clone more than just the first layer? Deep 	cloning. It's a little funky but we can use JSON.
	let superClone = JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(obj));
	What this essentially does, is it takes the object, obj, and 	it stringifies it, so that it maintains scope, depth, and 	state, and then parses it out into a variable, or another 	object, so that the new object, superClone, is now filled 	with the same properties, scope, depth, and state as the 	other object, obj.
	console.log(superClone); -> { a: 'a', b: 'b', c: { deep: 'Try 	and copy me' }}
	It should be noted that one should take caution with 	deep cloning as it can have large speed implication 	depending on the depth of the object being cloned.
167. Type Coercion
	This is the one thing that will make you pull out all of 	your hair in frustration.
	1 == '1'; -> true
	Type Coercion means the language converting a certain 	type to another type.
	ALL LANGUAGES HAVE TYPE COERCION
	In JavaScript, type coercion hapens, when you use the 	double equals sign. == means compare the two values, 	and if they have two different tpyes, try to coerce one to 	the same type.
	Three equals means compare the values but don't try to 	coerce.
	There isn't really ever a usecase for the double equals 	sign (==). Always use three equals (===).
	Type coercion doesnt just happen with equals signs, it also 	happens in if statements.
	if (1) {
		console.log(5);
	}; -> 5
	JavaScript coerces 1 to be true, which then executes the 	statement.
	if (0) {
		console.log(5);
	}; -> undefined
	JavaScript wants a boolean, so it coerces the value, and 	turns 0 into false.
	In JavaScript there is a concept of -0 and +0.
	-0 === +0; -> true - but they are technically different
	Object.is(-0,+0); -> false
	NaN === NaN; -> false
	Object.is(NaN,NaN); -> true

	Object.entries
	The successor of Object.keys.
	Object.keys allows us to interact with an object like we 	would an array. E.g.
	let obj = {
		username0: 'Santa'
		username1: 'Rudolph'
		username2: 'Mr. Grinch'
	}
	Object.keys(obj) now allows us to interact with the obj as 	if it were an array with property/value pairs.
	Object.keys(obj).forEach((key, index) => {
		console.log(key, obj[key]);
	}); ->
	username0 Santa
	username1 Rudolph
	username2 Mr. Grinch
	This is one way for us to loop through, or iterate over 	objects. Another is Object.entries. I.e.
	Object.values(obj).forEach(value => {
		console.log(value);
	}); ->
	Santa
	Rudolph
	Mr. Grinch
	Object.entries(obj).forEach(value => {
		console.log(value);
	}
	["username0", "Santa"]
	["username1", "Rudolph"]
	["username2", "Mr. Grinch"]
	Object.entries allows us a lot of control by giving us both 	the key and the value, in an array object, together.
	Object.entries(obj).map(value => {
		return value[1] + value[0].replace('username', ' ');
	}); -> ["Santa0", "Rudolph1", "Mr. Grinch2"]

	for (item of basket) {
		console.log(item);
	}; -> apples oranges grapes
	Iterating - able to go one by one.
	Iterating is able to be done in JavaScript on something 	called iterables. In JavaScript you can iterate over 	Arrays, and also Strings.
	for(item of 'basket') {
		console.log(item);
	}; -> b a s k e t
	For in - enumerating - properties - objects
	const detailedBasket = {
		apples: 5,
		oranges: 10,
		grapes: 1000
	}
	for (item in detailedBasket) {
		console.log(item);
	}; -> apples oranges grapes
	The for in loops allows us to loop over and see the object's 	properties, or keys.
	Properties of objects are Enumerable. An object in 	JavaScript is enumerable if it allows us to see the 	properties. 
	Apples, oranges, and grapes, are enumerables. That is, we 	can use a for in loop to check them out.
	for(item of detailedBasket) {
		console.log(item);
	}; -> Uncaught TypeError: detailedBasket is not iterable.
	for(item in basket) {
		console.log(item);
	}; -> 0 1 2 - This is because underneath the hood, 	JavaScript arrays, are objects.
	An array has an index of 0 1 and 2, so we can essentially 	see the basket array as an object like this:
	basket = {
		0: 'apples',
		1: 'oranges',
		2: 'grapes'
	};
	The for in loop is enumerating over the properties 0, 1, 	and 2.

	BigInt - new type (new in ES2020)
	typeof 1n; -> bigint
	JavaScript has something called MaxSafeInteger, which 	represents the maximum safe integer in JavaScript.
	(2^53 - 1) or 9007199254740991.
	Anything over this number is considered a bigint.
	The reasoning behind that number is that JavaScript 	uses double-precision floating-point format number as 	specficied in IEEE 754 and can only safely represent 	numbers between -(2^53 - 1) and (2^53 - 1).
	Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER; -> 9007199254740991
	9007199254740991 + 1; -> 9007199254740992
	9007199254740991 + 10; -> 9007199254741000
	900719925474100 - 1; -> 9007199254741000
	Bigint is used when we want to use a number larger than 	the max safe integer and do some calculations with it.
	9007199254741000n - 1n; -> 9007199254740999n
	9007199254740991 + 10; -> 9007199254741000
	9007199254740991n + 10n; -> 9007199254741001n

			height: 0.4,
			weight: 6
		}
	}

	let andrei_pokemon = {
		raichu: {
			species: 'Mouse Pokemon',
			height: 0.8,
			weight: 30
		}
	}
	let weight2 = andrei_pokemon.raichu.weight;

	if (andrei_pokemon && andrei_pokemon.pikachu && 	andrei_pokemon.pikachu.weight) {
		let weight2 = andrei_pokemon.pokemon.weight
	} else {
		let weight2 = undefined;
	}
	With optional chaining we can avoid this complication of 	chaining if conditions.
	weight3 = andrei_pokemon?.pikachu?.weight
	console.log(weight3); -> undefined
	weight3 = andrei_pokemon?.raichu
	console.log(weight3); -> {raichu...}
	weight3 = andrei_pokemon?.raichu?.weight;
	console.log(weight3); -> 30
180. ES2020 Part 3
	NULLISH COALESCING OPERATOR
	While the Optional Chaining Operator allows us to chain 	conditional checks similar to an && AND chain, the 	Nullish Coalescing Operator is used as || or OR.
	let andrei_pokemon = {
		raichu: {
			species: 'Mouse Pokemon',
			height: 0.8,
			weight: 30,
			power: 'lightning'
		}
	}
	let power = andrei_pokemon?.raichu?.power || 'No Power'
	console.log(power); - > lightning
		let andrei_pokemon = {
		raichu: {
			species: 'Mouse Pokemon',
			height: 0.8,
			weight: 30,
			power: ''
		}
	}
	console.log(power); -> No Power - '' evaluates as false
	|| works by checking if it is truthy.
	let andrei_pokemon = {
		raichu: {
			species: 'Mouse Pokemon',
			height: 0.8,
			weight: 30,
			power: ''
		}
	}
	let power = andrei_pokemon?.raichu?.power ?? 'No Power'
	console.log(power); -> undefined
	The Nullish Coalescing Operator checks if it is null or 	undefined, not whether it is truthy or not, like the or || 	operator.
	let andrei_pokemon = {
		raichu: {
			species: 'Mouse Pokemon',
			height: 0.8,
			weight: 30,
			power: '0'
		}
	}
	let power = andrei_pokemon?.raichu?.power ?? 'No Power'
	console.log(power); -> 0
	let andrei_pokemon = {
		raichu: {
			species: 'Mouse Pokemon',
			height: 0.8,
			weight: 30,
			power: '0'
		}
	}
	let power = andrei_pokemon?.raichu?.power || 'No Power'
	console.log(power); No Power
	Nullish Coalescing is really useful for most situations that 	you would use the OR operator.

	When the JavaScript engine in the browser runs into the 	word debugger, it stops. It takes you to the debugging 	window which allows you to literally go into the function 	and see what happens step by step.

187. How JavaScript Works
	What is a program?
	It has to
	-allocate memory - variables, files
	-parse and execute scripts - read and run commands
	The Chrome Browser uses the V8 Engine. An engine 	consists of two parts, a memory heap, and a call stack.
	The memory heap is where the memory allocation 	happens, and the code stack is where your code is read 	and executed.
	Memory Leak
	let a = 1;
	let b = 10;
	let c = 100;
	With all memory, theres a limited amount that we can 	have, so by assigning and declaring many variables to 	the memory heap, you are utilizing that amount 	extensively.
	When you have unused variables that are just taking up 	space on the memory heap, this is a Memory Leak. That's 	why people say global variables are bad. If we don't clean 	up after ourselves, we get memory leaks.
	Call Stack
	console.log('1');
	console.log('2');
	console.log('3');
	What the call stack does is, reads the first line, 	console.log, and it says oop, console.log has been added 	to the callstack. Then it runs, creates '1' and then says, 	okay, I'm removing the first console.log, because I've just 	finished running it, and I'm going to place the second 	console.log into my call stack. And so on and so forth. 	Add, execute, pop.
	const one = () => {
		const two = () => {
			console.log('4');
		}
		two();
	}
	//Call Stack
	Add one() -> add two() -> console.log('4') -> Nothing 	else inside of this -> Execute console.log('4') to print 4 -> 	remove console.log('4') from the call stack -> remove the 	two() -> remove the one()	
	JavaScript is a single threaded language that can be 	nonblocking.
	Single Threaded means that it has only one Call Stack.
	One call stack, one thing at a time. First in, Last out.
	Multithreaded languages have multiple call stacks.
	Why is JavaScript single threaded? Running code on a 	single thread can be quite easy since you don't have to 	deal with complicated scenarios that arise in 	multithreaded environments. Things like, Deadlocks.
	Synchronous programming means line 1 gets executed, 	then line 2 gets executed, then line 3 and so on and so 	forth. The latter can't start before the first finishes.
	Stack Overflow is when your call stack overflows. E.g.
	function foo() {
		foo()
	}
	foo();
	Recursion - a function that calls itself. Loops itself over 	and over.
	We keep adding foo to our call stack over, and over, and 	over.
	console.log(1);
	console.log(2);
	console.log(3);
	JavaScript runs single statements at a time, so what if 	our second console.log is something a bit more complex, 	like say looping over an array with a million objects. Well 	our console.log(3) would take a while to execute as the 	program would hang at the execution of the second 	statement before continuing.
	With Synchronous task if we have one function that takes 	up a lot of time it's going to hold up the stack. 
	This is why we need something nonblocking.
	Ideally we don't wait around for things that take time.
	How do we do this? Asynchronous.
	Think of asynchronous like a behavior. Synchronous 	execution is great because it's predictable, we know what 	happens first, second, and third, but it can get slow. 	When we have to do things like image processing or 	making requests over the network like API calls, we need 	something more.
	We can do Asynchronous programming like this:
	console.log(1);
	setTimeout(() => {
		console.log(2);
	}, 2000)
	console.log(3);
	For JavaScript, as we know it, to run, for the JavaScript 	Engine with memory heap and call stack to run, we need 	a JavaScript Run-Time Environment. 
	JavaScript Run-Time Environments are part of the 	browser. They have extra things. On top of the engine, 	they have something called Web APIs, callback queue 	and an event loop. setTimeout() is part of the Web API.
	//CALL StACK
	console.log(1);
	setTimeout() ->
	console.log(3);
	console.log(2);
	//WEB API
	setTimeout(), 2000
	//CALLBACK QUEUE
	after 2000, we get a callback() which says our time is up, 	we have something and it's ready to run
	//EVENT LOOP
	The event loop checks if the call stack is empty, if it's 	empty, it will check the callback queue. 
	It checks and sees our callback() and sends it to the call 	stack. Which then runs the console.log(2).
	console.log(1);
	setTimeout(() => {
		console.log(2);
	}, 0)
	console.log(3); -> 1 3 2
	element.addEventListener('click', () => {
		console.log('click')
	});
	console.log('click') is part of the callback function.
188. Modules 
	"To understand where we are, we first have to 	understand where we came from."
	Module Mountain:
	Webpack + ES6
	Browserify
	IIFE
	Script Tags
	Inline Script
	When we first started writing JavaScript we had an HTML 	file, and the very first JS was written inline inside the 	<script> tag. 
	<script type="text/javascript">
		function a() {
			alert('a')
		}
	</script>
	The issue is the scalability, the lack of code 	reusability, and the pollution of the global namespace.
	Once we declare "a" on the windows object, we can't use 	"a" for anything else within the window object. This can 	cause collisions, different team members working on 	different pieces of code and using the same name.
	Then came script tags. The ability to link to a separate 	<script> file. 
	<script type="text/javascript" src="./1.js></script>
	<script type="text/javascript" src="./1.js></script>
	The issue here is, if we need another page, we still have to 	copy and paste the script. Another issue is, the lack of 	Dependency Resolution. this means that we are 	responsible for making sure the scripts are in the proper 	order. And lastly, it doesn't solve the global namespace 	issue because all variables declared in the tags will still 	be declared on the main window object.
	Then there is IIFE, which stands for Immediately Invoked 	Function Expression. Iffy.
	var myApp = {}
	(function(){
		myApp.add = function(a, b) {
			return a + b;
		}
	})();
	jQuery uses this; it allows us to use $.
	An IIFE wraps a function in brackets () so that JavaScript 	reads it and says "Oh I have to evaluate this and execute 	this" and the brackets at the end say run this function.
	By doing this you don't pollute the global namespace, as 	everything that's inside the function, creates it's own 	scope.
	However the order of the files can still pose an issue.
	Next we have Browserify, which made use of CommonJS, 	which allows us to use a certain syntax.
	//js1
	module.exports = function add(a, b){
		return a + b;
	}
	//js2
	var add = require("./add");	//assuming js1 is "add.js"
	Browserify used the CommonJS syntax, but it's actually a 	Module Bundler. It runs before the website is put online, 	or deployed. It reads through all the JavaScript files, 	reads through all the syntax, and bundles everything 	into a single file. Once you run all the files through 	Browserify, it bundles them all into one file:
	<script src="bundle.js><script>
	All of these problems were because JavaScript didn't have 	a module system built into the language. Think of 	modules as building blocks, different pieces of code, so 	intead of having one giant thing, you have modules. 	Each piece is really really good at doing one thing. This 	changed wth ES6.
	ES6 added import and export.
	ES6 + Webpack2
	//js1
	export const add = (a, b) => a + b;	// export multiple
	//or
	export default function add() {	// export single
		return a + b;
	}
	//js2
	import { add } from "./add";
	//or
	import add from "./add";
	Webpack is a module bundler. Just like Browserify, it 	traverses the dependency tree, which is the import and 	export tags, and bundles them into a single file, or, it 	can even have multiple files based on your needs.
	With Webpack, we can use ES6 with all browsers.
	Webpack is very simple, it just uses a config file, like this:
	//webpack
	module.exports = {
		entry: './app/main/js',
		output: {
			path: './dist',
			filename: 'bundle.js'
		}
	}
	Usually when a group or team works on a project, there is 	one person that configures the webpack config file, and 	it's done, and the rest just build on top.
189. More Modules to Come
	There are more modules and a lot more JavaScript to 	come. Things like Asynchronous JS, Node modules, and 	ES6 Modules, where we will learn modern ways to use 	modules in the JS ecosystem.

	This idea of you not being good enough simply means 	that while you're just not there yet. Things take time. If 	you want to learn a valuable skill, it's hard work, that's 	why it's valuable.

195. Using The Terminal
	The Command Line, Terminal, or Command Prompt, is 	how humans originally communicated with computers 	up until the 1980's. It was the main way humans gave 	instructions to computers and computers gave output to 	the humans.
	We didn't have a GUI, Graphical User Interface, such as 	opening up a new file, or renaming a file, or opening up 	a web browser. We don't need to know how all that works 	behind the scenes, we just need to click a button or 	image.
	Command Lines are useful, because you can do the exact 	same thing you do visually on your GUI, in your 	Command Line, which is usually much faster.
	There are also common ways to access different computers 	around the world, like if you need to access a server in 	another country where your web app is hosted, you would 	use the Command Line.
	You'll be using your terminal a lot in your regular flow 	because you have access to servers, the database, your 	front end app, and more, from 	one terminal. You can 	even download scripts from the internet that other people 	have written.
	ls/dir lists everything in the current directory.
	pwd/(cd for windows) shows the present working 	directory, or current directory.
	cd changes the current directory. e.g. cd Desktop
	cd .. moves you up one directory level.
	clear/(cls for windows) clears the slate.
	cd / takes you to the root directory.
	cd ~ takes you to the user directory.
	open ./start . opens the current folder.
	mkdir makes a directory/ creates a folder in pwd.
	touch/echo > creates a file inside cd/pwd.
	start ".exe name" will launch the application, and 
	start ".exe name" "filename" will launch the file inside 	the pwd/cd in the application. E.G. 
	start code index.html
	mv will move a file, however you can use it as a trick to 	rename files as well. E.g.
	mv index.html about.html
	rm/(del for windows) will remove a file.
	rm -r/ rmdir will remove a directory. E.g
	rm about.html, cd .., rm -r webapp, cd .., rmdir test
	say (mac only) will voice the text input after "say"

Section 16: Git + GitHub + Open Source Projects
204. Installing Git
205. Git + Github Part 1
	Source Control - allows us to make sure that multiple 	people can work on the same file from different locations.
	When we are updating files, we want them to be updated 	that way for everyone working on the project, this is what 	GitHub allows us. Source Control has an ultimate copy of 	the project, that each idvidual working on the project can 	pull and push from. When there are two versions of the 	same file, with conflicting data sets, this is a merge 	conflict. E.g.
	<title>Super Background</title>
	<title>Cool Background</title>
	GitHub notices this merge conflict, and brings it to the 	front door of the users involved, to resolve the conflict.
	Start a project, named whatever you want. Leave it 	public, and initialize this project with a readme.
	Company X wants Marcy and Andrei to work on the same 	project. So Marcy goes to GitHub, creates the project, and 	then goes to clone or download. Make sure it says use 	HTTPS and click the copy link button.
	Then Marcy goes to the terminal/bash, navigates to the 	cd, pwd, Marcy's Project, and uses git clone followed by 	the link. This will add a .git folder to your project 	directory.
	If you try to use Git commands in the Marcy folder, as 	opposed to the Marcy's Project folder, it will not work, 	because that is not a git linked directory, or project, with 	the .git folder. 
	Now we want to sync the files that we modified/added in 	the Marcy's Project folder to our git project. We do this by 	using git status which tells us if we have untracked files.
	Then to add the files to git we use git add index.html.
	Now if we do git status, it will show us the branch is up to 	date, and there are changes to be committed: a new file: 	index.html is being added..
	Then to fully "accept", or verify, or commit the changes, 	you use git commit. Git commit allows you to pass a 	message and on GitHub it will show the message so that 	when users see the commit, they can see extra context. 	You do this with git commit -m "adding start"
	So we've done 'git add' to add our changes, then 'git 	commit' to say yeah, we're locking in those changes, 	committing to them, and then finally, we need to let 	github.com know that these are the changes with 'git 	push'.
	Now our github repository is updated and populated with 	some files thanks to Marcy.
	Now Andrei has joined the company and would like to 	work on the project. He can do so via the command line 	by doing the same thing as before. First, select the 	directory, 'cd Andrei' then
	'git clone git@github.com:aneagoie/background-	generator.git'
	This will clone the project to Andrei's directory.
	Now both Marcy and Andrei can work on Background-	Generator together, but separately, without changing or 	altering each others files.
	Say Andrei wanted to change the <h1> from 'Background 	Generator' to 'Cool Generator' in the index.html file.
	If we traverse to the background-generator directory 'cd 	background-generator' we can use git commands, since 	this directory has a .git folder inside it.
	'git status' shows us that there is a modification to 	index.html.
	If we want to add that change to our repository we can 	do 'git add index.html' for a single file, or if we want to 	add all of the files in the directory, we can use 'git .'
	Once we have added our changes, we can use 'git 	commit -m"Title Change" to commit the change to our 	repository with a message Title Change.
	Now that we have our change added, and we have 	committed to this change, we can push it to the repository 	so that it reflects the change, with 'git push'.
	Nothing should change on the git repo page, aside from 	the timestamp in which the changed file was updated, 	and the commit count. If we click the commits, which is 	now at 3, we will see each of the changes that have been 	made to the project, with respective messages. 
	We can also select each of these commits and it will show 	us what changes were made via that commit.
	If we go to Marcy's computer now, and view the project, we 	will see that the index.html still has the original title. 	This is because her computer doesn't know GitHub has 	been updated. 
	The command to use in a situation like this, is 'git pull'.
	'git pull' receives the latest updates from the repository, in 	opposition to push, which pushes updates.
	When we come into work, the first thing we want to do if 	we are continuing to work on this porject, is to 'git pull' so 	that we know we are working with the latest and most 	updated version of the project.
206. Github Update: Master --> Main
	Github has decided to rename the original branch from 	master' to 'main'. Main is less offensive than master. For 	this reason, any new Github repos created will have to use 	'main' instead of 'master'. Replace 'master' with 'main' 	e.g. 'git push origin main'
207. Git + Github Part 2
	 
	 
	The master branch, or 'main' branch, can be seen as the 	authority. The 'right way' that our app or website should 	look. 
	If we want to work on the project without altering what 	other people are working on, we can create a branch. In 	this example we will name it 'Little Feature'. When we 	work on Little Feature, our changes are made to Little 	Feature, not the main branch. So we can hone our 	project, problem solve, debug, iterate, and eventually 	when we are ready to add, commit, and push it to the 	'main' project, we can do so, all while limiting the extra 	alterations on the 'main' branch, and all while allowing 	our peers or authorities to review, and manage, the 	request.
	Now that we want to work on our own part of the 'main' 	code, it is best practice to make a branch to utilize.
	If we do 'git branch' it will show us the branches that are 	active in the current directory's project.
	* master/main is all we will see for now.
	To add a branch we simply give it a name. E.g. 'git 	branch littlefeature'.
	Now if we do 'git branch' it will show us the littlefeature 	branch as well as the master/main branch. However, 	main will be highlighted, because that is the current 	active branch. If we want to switch the branch we are 	utilizing, we do 'git checkout littlefeature'.
	This has created a new branch off of the main branch, 	named littlefeature, and switched us to this branch.
	In littlefeature, we are going to change our <h2>Current 	CSS Background</h2> to 
	<h2>This is the 	background</h2>.
	Then we do the same workflow, git status to verify the file 	was modified. Then git add . to add the modified file to 	our list of commits. Then git commit -m"Changing Text" 	to commit the changes we made. And lastly, git push to 	push the changes we have added and committed to, 	which will push the new index.html file into our 	littlefeature branch.
	On Github we will see a yellow bar which shows "your 	recently pushed branches". If you click on the button 	"Compare & Pull Request" it will take you to the open pull 	request page. 
	Here you can see the changes made in the branch, and 	you can add comments to further annotate your PR. E.g.
	"Marcy I've changed the tet, what do you think?" 
	Then you create the pull request.
	Now you have a pull request, or PR. You can see that it 	now allows you to "Merge pull request". It also now shows 	your PR under the pull requests tab, which is where Marcy 	can see in the Github repo, that a merge has been 	requested or that her attention is needed on a change.
	Marcy can then look through the changes, and if she 	wants, can select lines of code, or specific changes, and 	comment on them (e.g. "Looks good!), and then select 	"Start a Review".
	If Marcy is in charge and really likes this branch, she 	can just approve it by selecting the "Review Changes" 	dropdown, entering a Review Summary (e.g. "Everything 	is good.", and selecting approve. If just commenting the 	Review SUmmary, you can merge the pull request with 	the "Merge Pull Request" button, alluded to earlier. Select 	"confirm merge" and you're good to go. The "littlefeature" 	branch has now successfully been merged into the "main" 	branch.
	Now Marcy can do a git pull, and she will have the latest 	'main' update.
	Let's eplore conflicts. Say Marcy wants to make a new 	feature, called bigfeature.
	git branch bigfeature
	git checkout bigfeature
	git branch - shows the new branch (selected) with the 	'main'.
	Marcy changes the <h1> back to 'Background Generator'
	git add .
	git commit -m"Changed to original title"
	git push
	fatal: The current branch bigfeature has no upstream 	branch. To push the current branch and set the remote as 	upstream, use
	git push --set-upstream origin bigfeature
	Adding multiple changes. Add exclamation mark to 	<h1>. git add . , git commit -m 'Add ! to title", git push.
	Now if we go to Github and we look at the compare & pull 	request, we will see both of the commits. Say Marcy 	reviews, and leaves a comment, "Needs changes, Andrei" 	then creates the pull request.
	WHILE this pull request active, we make a new branch.
	git branch conflict
	git checkout conflict
	Remove <h1>
	git status - modified file
	WHILE this is happening, Marcy merges the pull request.
	git add .
	git commit -m"Check"
	git checkout main
	git pull
	git checkout conflict
	git merge main - merges everything from main to 	conflict.
	Marcy has been working on the big feature and she's 	merged it so now main has the bigfeature branch, but 	we've also created another branch, conflict, that we've 	been working on and it doesn't know anything about 	main, so we're saying whatever is in main right now, 	which includes bigfeature, pull it in, and make sure that 	there's no conflict.
	CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in index.html
	Automatic merge failed; fix conflicts and then commit 	the result.
	If we look at the index.html now, 
	<<<<<HEAD
	=====
		<h1>Background Generator!</h1>
	>>>>> main
	Head is where my branch is at, and it's saying we don't 	have anything here, but in main there is the h1 tag.
	Now you can talk to Marcy and figure out which change 	has the go ahead.
	git add . 
	git commit -m"Fixed"
	git push - fatal:
	git push origin conflict - success
	Github will now show an empty pull request, because we 	reverted all of the changes that were made.
	Every time you're working on something you always 	create a branch, title it with whatever feature you're 	working on, and intermittently pull from the 'main' and 	merge it into your branch, so you're not missing any 	updates other developers or teammates may have done.
	Once you're done with your feature, you make a pull 	request, by doing git push, and that pull request, you'll 	add comments, and people will review your files chanes, 	and if they want you to make changes you can simply git 	add git commit git push into the request, and then 	finally, you can merge the pull request.
	Now that you've merged, there is now a new main 	branch, and everyone should git pullto get the update.

209. Contributing To Open Source
	Open Source Software, is software with source code that 	anyone can inspect, modify, and enhance.
	The most famous Open Source Project, is Linux, an 	operating system.
	Most of the cloud that we know of, the servers usually run 	Linux. AWS, Google Cloud, Microsoft Azure, all us Linux.
	If you're looking for job experience but you don't have a 	job, contributing to open source is the perfect way for you 	to learn how to work in teams, and mimic a work like 	scenario.
	https://www.github.com/zero-to-mastery
	Make your first pull request, by adding your name to the 	contributors.md, md stands for markdown, file in the 	start-here-guidelines project.
	To do this, you can start by forking the project. Fork 	essentially means copy over the entire project and put it 	on your own GitHub profile. Click Fork in the top left, and 	select the git profile you would like to fork the project 	into.
	Once we fork the project, we need to clone it. It's like we 	own this repo now with our own version of the start-here-	guidelines.
	You can use SSH or HTTPS, we'll use SSH so we only have to 	enter our password once.
	git clone <git repo link here>
	cd start-here-guidelines
	"code ." open it up in our fav text editor.
	Now we want to create a new branch to work on so that 	we are not making changes to our "main" branch.
	git checkout -b test | -b makes a new branch and test is 	the declared name of that branch.
	Markdown has it's own syntax, which is followed in 	contributor.md file, to make the names of the profiles 	clickable links. 
	We do this with a - first, and then [] brackets around the 	GitHub username, and then in () parentheses 	afterwards, will be the link to the GitHub profile.
	git status - contributors.md modified
	git diff - shows that we added a new line
	git add .
	git commit -m "Added my name to contributor list"
	git push origin master - this means we want to push to 	origin. Origin would be the start-here-guidelines fork 	that we made. We don't want to do that because we want 	to be able to also change and add or name to the 	original ZTM start-here-guideline.
	git push origin test - this will push it to the test branch 	instead.
	Now we want to go to our project to see that we have two 	branches. We will now create a pull request on the test 	branch, where we can add additional comments, and 	even tag mentors, instructors, management, etc. Then we 	click create pull request and it creates. We can then see 	this pull request from our main project. Someone with the 	authority to do so, can then accept, and merge your pull 	request. Your branch can then be deleted if wanted, as 	well as your fork even.
210. Contributing to Open Source Part 2
	Go to Hacktoberfest in October if you want tons a fun 	projects and repos to play around with.
	https://www.github.com/zero-to-mastery/hacktoberfest-2019
	You can contribute to any of these. If we look at 	Animation Nation, we can see a bunch of community 	created CSS animation projects.
	Go to the project repository, and follow the steps we've 	done before.
	Fork the repo, clone the repo, git clone <repo link>
	Now let's look at the instructions on how to contribute.
	cd Animation-Nation
	code .
	Navigate to the Art folder, and create a folder with your 	name. This is where we will add our index.html and 	index.css files, as well as our .gif.
	Move this folder into the Art folder.
	Navigate to incude.js and copy a project object, paste it on 	the end, and replace the info with your own.
	We have done all these changes while on the 'main' 	branch, while optimally we would want to do them on a 	new branch.
	Let's do that now before the adds and commits.
	git checkout -b <project name here> "animatron"
	git add .
	git commit -m "Adding my artsyness to the project"
	git push origin animatron
	Instructions say
	1. create directory with name
	2. create two files, HTML and CSS
	3. Link CSS file to HtmL file
	4. Using only HtmL and CSS create a work of art
	5. Screen record your finished work and make a gif. Save 	this in the directory as well.
	6. Go to the root include.js. You will see an array of 	objects, each one represents someone's work of art. Copy 	the object and populate it with your projects details.
	7. Save everything and commit your repo.	
	We now have an icon for Compare & Pull Request, and if 	we go to the original Animation-Nation project, you will 	also see Compare & Pull Request.
	We can then add a comment "Please add me to this list" 	and tag a manager "@manager" and select Create pull 	request.
	You can view the file changes and see the code that was 	changed.
	The manager will merge your pull request, and delete 	the branch.
	Animatron is now added to Animation-Nation!!

212. Keeping Your Fork Up To Date
	git remote -v
	This will show youre current configured remote repository 	for our fork. E.g
	git remote -v
	origin https://github.com/YOUR-USERNAME/YOUR-	FORK.git (fetch)
	origin https://github.com/YOUR-USERNAME/YOUR-	FORK.git (push)
	git remote add upstream origin 	https://github.com/zero-to-mastery/PROJECT_NAME.git
	This has now added the original repo as the upstream to 	your current repo.
	To verify
	git remote -v
	origin https://github.com/YOUR-USERNAME/YOUR-	FORK.git (fetch)
	origin https://github.com/YOUR-USERNAME/YOUR-	FORK.git (push)
	upstream 	https://github.com/ORIGINAL_OWNER/ORIGINAL_REPOSIT	ORY.git (fetch)
	upstream 	https://github.com/ORIGINAL_OWNER/ORIGINAL_REPOSIT	ORY.git (push)
	Now you can keep your fork synced with the upstream 	(original repository) with a few Git commands.
	The simplest way is to do this command from the master 	of your forked repository.
	git pull upstream master
	Or you can follow a guide: https://docs.github.com/en/pull-requests/collaborating-with-pull-requests/working-with-forks/syncing-a-fork

Section 17: A Day In The Life Of A Developer
214. A Typical Day
	The day normally starts at 9am, where you would want 	to git checkout to your master branch and git pull so 	that everything is up to date from the time that you were 	gone. Then you could check the Trello board which will 	have the To Do's, the doing's, and the done's on it. E.g 	conflict in doing, we could go check the Pull Request, if 	it's an empty one, we simply close it, delete the branch, 	and the conflict is resolved. Then we would move conflict 	from "doing" to "done".
	10am there is normally a stand up where team/project 	members get everybody's updates and everyone stands 	around. There is usually an SM or Scrum Master, or a 	manager, or director, that organizes the meeting and 	takes the lead. This is the timefor communications about 	business with the team. There is also usually a PO, or 	Product Owner, or client, or CEO.
	10:30 back to work, working on bugs and features. You 	would go over to the Trello board To Do list and usually 	there are small chunks that you can chip away at. Say 	you want to update the ReadMe. Drag that into doing, so 	hat everyone knows it is active. Then:
	git branch update-readme
	git checkout update-readme
	Work, work, work, work, feeling happy, got a lot of work 	done, actually finished the readme task, had lunch, 2:00 	pm lets say, time to submit a PR, or Pull Request, to 	merge the readme changes.
	Team members will then review active Pull Requests.
	This includes yourself, and you can review different PRs, 	comment, and approve even.
	Afterwards, around 3pm, some companies will do 	something called QA, which is Quality Analysis. 	Essentially making sure that thte changes, we've 	implemented, hasn't impacted the webapp and it still 	works on all browsers, mobile, etc.
	If everything passed, the PR is approved, and your work is 	finally merged into master. Update readme goes into the 	done tab on Trello, and onto the next task!
	Being a web developer is all about breaking up big tasks 	like building Netflix, into small manageable chuncks 	and each day you're just working one chunk at a time. 	Baby steps. Digestible chunks. Write some JS one day, 	some CSS another, and HTML the next, to build up this 	one great big product.
215. Exercise: Helping A Developer
	As a developer, most the time, you are working on teams 	with other developers. Take this opportunity to help a 	fellow developer in the #helpme channel is Discord.
	You may not feel like you know everything yet, but you 	are at a stage where you can actually help other 	developers. Helping others is one of the best ways to 	improve your skills and learn, and you will get some 	good karma out of it!
216. A Developer's Morning Routine
	Check in with new letters and updates on the industry. 
	Andrei reads the top articles on 	https://news.ycombinator.com
	Then https://producthunt.com tops from the previous day.
	And lastly, sign up for the newsletter 	https://zerotomastery.io/blog/#monthly
	You don't HAVE to do all these things, have fun, but these 	are great resources to stay up to date on current 	technologies.
Section 18: NPM + NPM Scripts
217. Introduction To NPM
	NPM was created for developers to be able to share 	JavaScript code that they have written. This way any 	developer around the world can grab someone else's code 	that may solve his or her problem.
	This creates a great knowledge base of scripts that people 	can constantly update or improve and build on top of.
	It is very rare that an app or website these days is built 	purely from scratch.
	By using the collective intelligence we can grab pieces of 	code that are really good and build on top of it, to create 	our product.
	For exampe, using JS, you can write virtual reality 	applications, but  you don't have to figure out everything 	yourself. someone out there already worked on being able 	to use JSVR so you use that persons code to start your 	project.
	https://npmjs.com/
	Here you can search for what we call Packages. These 	packages are located in the NPM registry. This registry 	keeps track of these files that are submitted. Anyone can 	submit these files that are called packages, and they 	contain two things; A JavaScript file, and a package.json 	file. The package.json is the meta file that describes this 	package.
	the idea is to have small JS files that do one thing really 	really well so that other people can use it.
	You can then compose or combine different pieces of code 	and different packages on your project to add 	functionality and not have to rewrite something that has 	already been written by another person.
	NOT ALL PACKAGES ARE GOOD CODE. And you are adding 	more bytes to your project. There is an art to finding good 	NPM packages and you will get used to it after a bit of 	experience.
	Yarn still uses the NPM registry but you might see it every 	once in a while. Just like NPM, it's a tool to grab packages 	from the registry.
	There are usually three types of packages. 
	One is packages that you can use on the browser (front-	end). E.g. You can download jQuery from NPM, and all 	sorts of scripts that help you with front-end workflow.
	Second is ones that give you new commands that you can 	use on the command line. 
	The third and final type of NPM package, is one that can 	use on Node.js on the back-end.
	NPM - Node Package Manager. It was initially built for 	node but was so good and so popular that it expanded to 	front-end as well.
	There are three must do things now when starting a new 	project, and you'll see it in every project.
	One, they have HTML, CSS, or JS.
	Two, they have a Github Repo + Git for source control. 	Nobody stores their own project on their own computer.
	Third and final, they have a package.json file.
	NPM to download files, package.json to manage them.
218. Setting Up NPM and package.json
	First, let's say we want to learn React. Well React is a 	package created by Facebook. So to get packages, we need 	NPM. To get NPM, we need Node.
	Download Node.js. Latest LTS Version (Long Term Support)
	https://nodejs.org/en/download/
	Node allows us to run JavaScript outside of the browser. 	Up until this point, if we wanted to use JavaScript we 	would write it in the console, or attached a script file to 	HTML and loaded the HTML file in the browser. Each 	browser has a JS engine, like V8 that Chrome has. That 	was the only thing that knew how to read JavaScript. We 	need something to read it and understand it, and 	Node.js was created using the V8 engine.
	Go to your terminal, and type
	npm -v -> 5.5.1
	node -v -> v8.9.1
	npm install npm@latest -g -> installs the latest version of 	npm
	Now that we have everything installed, how can we start 	using NPM and get that package.json file.
	npm init -> any time you start a project, you want to do 	this, and it will create a package.json file in your 	directory.
	You can name it and do other things at this point, but 	unless we are publishing an NPM package, we can just 	Enter through it.
	JSON is just a file format. Text editors will recognize it as 	a JSON file. It's format looks very similar to JavaScript 	objects except for the fact that the properties have double 	quotes around them. 
	JSON - JavaScript Object Notation

220. Troubleshoot: Installing NPM and Node.js
221. Installing And Using Packages
	live-server - a little development server with live reload 	capability. Use it for hacking your HTML/CSS/JS files, but 	not for deploying the final site.
	npm install -g live-server
	Lodash
	npm i --save lodash
	npm allows you to install things one of two ways. One is 	globally, and the other is locally.
	Installing locally means that it will only be installed 	and accessible in the directory npm was installed in.
	Installing globally (-g) means you can use it anywhere 	on the pc it is installed on. Also meaning you can run it 	on your terminal.
	If we install live-server with npm install -g live-server, 	this will install it globally, which means it resides on our 	PC and not inside our project directory. If we then go into 	our background-generator directory and run live-server, 	it will open up our app in the browser, and the terminal 	will show that live-server has created a live server for the 	project at http:// xxx.x.x.x:xxxx and shows "Ready for 	changes".
	We can now edit a file or piece of code, and live-server 	will show us x change detected. E.g. text-align: left 	instead of text-align: center will show CSS change 	detected 	/Users/carme/Documents/GitPlayground/Background-	Generator/style.css
	live-server creates a server that keeps watching your 	files 	an every time a change is made it sees it and 	updates 	the project.
	You will see as the url 127.0.0.1:8080.
	The 8080 is the port. 127.0.0.1 is what we call a local 	host.
	This is why NPM is awesome.
	We were able to install something really quickly a now we 	have a server, which is our computer, which is watching 	these files and serving up the project right here on our 	computer.
	We didn't need to buy a server, we didn't need to put it 	on GitHub. Everything is here already.
	That was global.
	Local we can do something like, Lodash. Lodash is lie an 	extension of JavaScript. With JS we have all these methods 	that come with it, getElementById, querySelector, etc, 	and lodash gives us more methods or functions that we 	can use/run.
	Things like differenceBy, and flatten, and fromPairs. Any 	time you have an issue, lodash is really good instead of 	maybe writing your own.
	We don't want lodash on our computer though, we just 	want it for this project. That is why we would install it 	locally.
	npm install lodash
	When we install something locally, and we already have 	a package.json file, since we did npm init, it will add it 	to ou	r dependencies, which means packages that our 	website needs to work.
	Now if we want to actually check if we have lodash 	working, we can do a few things. 
	import { without } from 'lodash'
	console.log(without);
	live-server
	Open console, and we get Unexpected token import
	browsers may not have implemented this yet.
	We can use the require syntax here. We used require back 	with ES6 and webpack.
	However we need to use browserify to allow us to use 	require.
	npm install -g browserify
	In our script file, var _ = require('lodash')
	Browserify takes script files, and outputs a script file that 	we can use in the browser.
	browserify script.js > bundle.js
	We now have a bundle.js file. This bundle.js has your 	script as well as all of lodash, and it's in a bundle file 	that the browser understands. 
	Now we simply go to index.html and replace our link in 	our <script> element from script.js to bundle.js
	Now if we run live-server, lodash is working!
	In our script file, with lodash now in my project, if I had 	an array I could use the without lodash method.
	var array = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8];
	console.log('answer:', _.without(array, 3));
	But since I just changed the script.js file and not the 	bundle.js it won't work yet.
	browserify script.js > bundle.js
	live-server
	Open up console, and we have an array without number 	3.
	Adding packages is a big commitment and often brings a 	lot of overhead to your project. It is something that 	should be discussed with the team, if it saves you time, if 	it's really well written, if it has a big community around 	it.
	Now we have something called dependencies. We need to 	make sure now that our website or our app always has 	lodash in a certain version so that it works with our 	website. And it adds an extra layer of complexity.
	Most big projects can have close to 100 dependencies and 	having just one location, package.json that shows all of 	those files, is really really nice.
	{..
	"scripts": {..
		"dependencies": {
			"lodash": "^4.17.4"
		}
	}
	semver: Semantic Versioning. 
	The right most number is the patch release, that is, if 	theres any bugfixes, they will update it here.
	The middle number is the minor release, which means 	there are some new features added but there won't be 	any changes that are major.
	And the left number is the major release, that might be 	quite different from the previous version and it might 	break some websites depending on a different version.
	The biggest benefit of NPM is that it is much easier to 	share with other developers.
	I never have to put the node modules in my GitHub repo. 	All I need is the package.json file. For example: if you 	delete the node packages. Then let's say someone new 	started at the company and they want to have the latest 	version of the priject before starting on it. Even though 	there are no node modules, because we have a 	package.json file, NPM is smart enough , that as long as 	the user does npm install on the project, and their is a 	package.json file, it will automatically install the 	dependencies for the project.
	These dependencies are what background-generator 	depends on, but there's another type of dependency called 	'dev dependency' and dev dependency are packages only 	needed for development and testing.
	npm run test
	Error: no test specified. -> which is what our test property 	in our scripts object had defined "echo \"Error: no test 	specified\" && exit 1"
	NPM scripts allow you to do commands from package.json 	file.
	In our scripts object, we can change our test property to 	"build" and change the value to 
	"build": "browserify script.js > bundle.js && live-server"
	npm run build
	browserify script.js > bundle.js updated 
	and live-server runs your project.
	WE NOW HAVE A COMPLETE VIEW OF THE FRONT END
222. Why Update Packages?
	Because a library is constantly evolving, new fixes are 	being made, new features are being added, new security 	issues are being solved or fixed, so the version number is 	constantly changing/increasing.
	The security tab on GitHub will show you how many 	security issues you have in the project.
	You also might see a warning in your repos, that says "we 	found potential security vulnerabilities in your 	dependencies. Your dependencies which are in your 	package.json file.
	If you click one one of the security alerts, e.g. 	handlenbars, it will tell you that handlebars needs to be 	upgraded to x specific version or later.
	GitHub actually has automated security fixes, so if you 	click that, it will create a pull request with all of the fixes.
	When we build our app in React, we are going to be 	upgrading a bunch of the things later on at the end of 	the project, just to show the process.
	https://semver.npmjs.com
	This will show all of the versioning of most technologies.
	>=1 - greater than or equal than 1
	^1 all that are within 1
