224. Exercise: Creating A Portfolio
	https://github.com/cobidev/simplefolio
	Simplefolio - A project made by a student of ZTM as a 	thanks to the community.
	It is licensed by M.I.T which means that we can use it 	freely for ourselves and customize it to our liking.
	Be sure to star the project repo!
	Fork and clone the repo.
	cd simplefolio
	code .
	Follow the documentation to get started.
	After cloning and cd'ing to the repo directory you can 	remove the current origin repository if you forked it.
	git remote remove origin
	Then we can install the dependencies
	npm install
	And then we can start the development server.
	npm start
	Just like that, we have a template for our portfolio. We 	also have template instructions so that we can customize 	it to our liking. We have enough knowledge now to 	understand this.
	In your code editor, go to the source folder, into 	templates.html, and change the title to you preference, as 	well as the name to your own name. Then change the H1 	from I'm the Unknow Developter to whatever you would 	like (I am the developer you are looking for) and change 	the CTA as well (hire me).
	Just by saving this, it will detect the changes and if we go 	to the project it will be updated with the changes we've 	done.
	Now we follow the next set of instructions, the about 	section, where we can edit the src property of the img tag 	with our image in the assets/ folder (replace profile.png). 	On the p tag we can include a bio, and on the <a> tag 	we can include our resume url in the href property.
	Now we can follow the step by step guide in the Projects 	section.
	Head to the project-wrapper section, go to the template, 	scroll down, past about, to project, and here, each row is 	a project. Change the project title, to your project title. 	You can also change the description, and then link it to 	the live version. To do this you will grab the live github 	website link e.g. https://aneagoie.github.io/robofriends/
	For the source code you will replace that href with the url 	to the github repository e.g. 	https://github.com/aneagoie/robofriends
	The last thing we need is an image of our project, which 	we can use the tool Hatchful for. It is a free logo making 	website. 
	https://hatchful.shopify.com
	You will need an account to download your images.
	Now you can replace the banner.png with your own 	image named banner.png.
	All of the projects will have the same banner image, but 	you can see in the code that it is pointing to banner.png. 	If you simply change the href to other logos, you will be 	able to have multiple projects on your profile.
	The documentation will have different things, like the 	contact section, the footer section, and other things that 	you can update.
	The last thing we will do, is the styles. You can adjust the 	styles by going to the src/styles/abstracts/_variables.scss 	file. This is a SASS file format. It's kind of like CSS but 	gives you extra features. 
	For example you can create a CSS variable simply by 	using $ in front of the variable name declaration. E.g.
	$main-color: #034fe5;
	To find colors for a nice gradient you can use 
	https://uigradients.com/#BrightVault
	Now we can go to the src/ folder, then styles/ , then 	abstract/ and then _variable.scss, and this will be the file 	that all of our CSS variables are saved in. We can then 	replace the $primary-color: #02aab0 with our own #	034fe5 and this will change all colors using the 	$primary-color variable.
	We've done it! We've customized our portfolio page. 
	There is even a method that we can follow at the end of 	the docs, that allows us to deploy our website!
Section 19: React.js, React Hooks, Redux (Hooks and Redux Videos Moved To End Of Course)
225. Introduction To React.js
	Things are going to get more and more fun starting in 	this section because we're now learning real tools that 	real companies use and will get you hired. Development 	also becomes a lot faster because we're using these tools to 	help us build websites faster and "better".
	DOM manipulation is really, really difficult, constatly 	keeping track of what changed and what to change is a 	huge mess and it creates for very slow development. As 	your website becomes more and more complex, it becomes 	very, very hard to scale, which means it becomes very 	hard for teams of devlelopers to manage it.
	React was created by Facebook. It was created as a tool 	that allows them to build websites in a nice, predictable 	way that you can have multiple developers working on it 	at the same time.
	It "does all this magic underneath the hood" and puts 	out a website. It is very reliable and consistent. 
	It does one thing really, really well, and that is, the view. 	It manages to make our websites, the DOM, change in 	predictable ways, that is easy to scale, and easy to 	manage.
	Because it's only the view, it doesn't necessarilt mean it's 	only for browsers. React can be used with mobile devices 	to build native apps, it's used in virtual reality apps, and 	you can build desktop apps with React. Anywhere that 	has a view. 
	Netflix uses React. Any time you are just scrolling, 	scrolling, through movies or shows, that is React.
	In order to understand React, there's a few things we 	want to go over. Principles that are very valuable and 	have beem learned over the years by developers.
	One is the idea of thinking in components.
	Up to this point we've just built websites. One HTML file, 	one CSS file, one JS file. But wouldn't it be nice if the web 	was a network of these small components. think of them 	as LEGO blocks. Everybody builds a specific LEGO block and 	you just combine these LEGO blocks to create your website. 
	That is the idea that React established.
	Example:
	COMPONENTS
	Atoms - icons, little small, indivisible elements on your 	websites such as images, maybe text. Everything is 	divided into single atoms. 
	From there you form Molecules - or combine atoms that 	have similar functionality. Such as a navigation bar 	(multiple icons (atoms)). You combine individual atoms 	to add some functionality. Then
	Organisms - A little bit bigger than molecules that 	combines to a bigger scale functionality. Then
	Templates - the combination of the organisms, to give you 	an idea of how your app or website should look.
	Finally, you have Pages - the page of your app.
	If you learn to think in components, you're able to build 	small little components that you can re-use and put on 	different sites and parts of the web.
	For example, a back button. If i build a React component 	that is the back button, I can re-use it on multiple pages 	and don't have to rewrite the code. If I wanted the nav 	bar to be used in different areas of the app, well again, I 	don't need to rewrite the code. Write it once, and reuse it. 	You can even share it with the world.
	You can download React components like Bootstrap. They 	are not just HtML and CSS, they are fully functional too, 	such as nav bars, and even forms. And because of the way 	react is structured, it doesn't really need to know about 	the rest of the web page. It's just it's own LEGO block that 	works by itself. By reducing dependencies on other parts 	of the website, I can just plug this anywhere I want.
	Another important concept with React is the idea of one 	way data flow.
 
	In React, data flows from top to bottom and never the 	other way around.
	If these red, let's call them components, change, in that 	case, only their children know about the chage. The 	parents don't really care about the change, because the 	direction of data flow is downstream.
	So if we go back to the idea of components, if we had a 	website built with components and the whole tree is the 	page, and the first two tiers are organisms, and the third 	tier is molecules and the fourth tier is atoms. In that 	case, the red components change, and only the ones 	below it know, depicted by the image on the right in blue.
	The next big thing in React is the Virtual DOM.
 
	Beforehand, we were the painters. We told the DOM 	exactly what to do. With React, we have this React-bot. 	React-bot creates something called a Virtual DOM. The 	Virtual DOM is just a JavaScript object. This object 	describes our website, the current state. We just give the 	object to React, and the React-bot will automatically 	make changes to the DOM and paint the picture in the 	most optimum way possible.
	We tell React-bot, "Hey, this is the object, this is what we 	want in the DOM, and it takes care of DOM manipulation 	for you.
	Remember how annoying it was to do querySelectorAll() 	and getElementsByID and doing all the event handlers, 	well, React takes care of that. 
	The Virtual Dom is just a copy of the DOM that's in a 	JavaScript object, and then Reaact underneath the hood, 	changes the view. That's also why it's able to work outside 	of browsers. You can use it for things like VR and mobile 	devices because React is the machine, and what the 	canvas is, is whatever we want.
	FINALLY, React has, a great ecosystem. It is probably one 	of the biggest ecosystems in JavaScript right now. There 	are so many tools that work with React. the developer 	community is so big, that likely any problem you have, 	someone else has already had it and mad a post on the 	internet and you can find solutions. 
	You can find React packages in NPM. You can use it with 	Node. there's Webpack and Babel that you can use with it.
	It's just a really good ecosystem that uses the latest 	JavaScript technology to build websites in a really nice, 	elegant way.
226. React Guide
	https://zerotomastery.io/cheatsheets/react-cheat-sheet/?utm_source=udemy
227. Create React App
	The recommended way to start with React.
	Getting your app up and running is as simple as:
	npx create-react-app {appName}
	cd {appName}
	npm start
	This will configure and compile a complete web app 	inside a directory named by you, with a package.json 	file, package-lock.json, a README.md, your node-modules 	folder, a src/ folder and a public/ folder.
	npm start will get it up and running on a localhost:3000 	server.
	The best of the best are creating these tools for React 	developers so that out of the box you have linting and 	testing already set up.
228. React + Vite
	Alternatives to Create-React-App. There are many build 	tools out there you can use to build React applications. 	One of the more popular ones is Vite. Vite is the French 	word for "quick" (pron. "veet").
	This is what we call a build tool, that allows us to build 	up a project, essentially scaffold a project.
	Vite is popular because it is super, super fast and 	performant.
	To create an app using Vite, go to the API guide, go to the 	get started, and here we have the instructions. 
	npm create vite@latest
	Need to install the following packages: create-vite@4.4.1
	Ok to process? (y) y
	? Project name: > vite-project
	? Select a framework: > - Use arrow-keys. Return to 	submit.
	Vanilla, Vue, React, Preact, Lit, Svelte, Solid, Qwik, Others
	React
	Select a variant: > - Use arrow-keys. Return to submit.
	Typescript, Typscript + SWC, JavaScript, JavaScript + SWC
	JavaScript
	Scaffolding project in /Users/carme/...
	Done. Now run:
		cd vite-project
		npm install
		npm run dev
229. React App Folder Structure
	If we take a look into what the create-react-app, or 	create-vite@latest, does, it automatically created a 	folder structure populated with some files. Amongst those 	files is a package.json file. If we explore what has been 	created within the package.json, we can see the 	dependencies, scripts and much more. 
	For now we are going to focus on the "scripts" in 	package.json.
	Here we have a few commands, "start", "build", "test", and 	"eject".
	These each run the respective command, "react-scripts x".
	This is what create-react-app actually is. It is react-	scripts.
	If we scroll all the way down our /node-modules folder, 	we will see /react-scripts, which is where the magic 	happens.
	npm test
	> robofriends@0.1.0 test 	/Users/carme/Documents/GitPlayground/robofriends
	> react-scripts test
	Determining test suites to run...
	No tests found related to files changed since last commit.
	Press 'a' to runn all tests, or run Jest with '--watchAll'.
	Watch Usage
	>Press a to run all tests.
	>Press f to run only failed tests.
	>Press q to quit watch mode.
	>Press p to filter by a filename regex pattern.
	>Press t to filter by a test name rege pattern.
	>Press Enter to trigger a test run.
	a
	RUNS src/App.test.js
	PASS src/App.test.js
		renders learn react link (24ms)
	Test Suites: 1 passed, 1 total
	Tests: 1 passed, 1 total
	Snapshots: 0 total
	Time: 1.487 s
	Ran all test suites.
	Watch Usage: Press w to show more.
	If we go to our App.js and change 'return' to 'rturn' and 	then run 
	npm start
	The site will launch with:
	Failed to compile
	src/App.js
		Line 5:3: 'rturn' is not defined no -undef
	Search for the keywords to learn more about each error.
	This error occurred during the build time and connot be 	dismissed.
	As long as we run npm start, it is constantly Linting and 		checking in the background for me.
	NOW if we go back to the package.json, and scroll up a 	little bit, we will see "dependencies".
	These are what was installed when we ran the npx 	command. 
	We have "react", "react-dom", "react scripts", and "web-	vitals".
	We also have package-lock.json. This is automatically 	generated by the package.json file. It makes sure that the 	versions we're using right now of our dependencies, every 	single thing that is in node-modules, is locked to the 	version number.
	If I sent this project anywhere across the globe, when it is 	opened, it is going to work the exact same way, because 	the version numbers are locked.
	Package-lock.json essentially makes sure that we can run 	this project anywhere in the world, and it's 	automatically update every time we update package.json.
	We have something called .gitignore, which has a dot in 	front of it, which means its a dot file. A dot file is a 	configuration file, and .gitignore tells us, whenever we 	run a git command, that whatever is in the .gitignore 	file, will be ignored by the git add or git commit, and so 	on. We would want to .gitignore our /node-modules 	folder.
	We now have two things left, the /public folder and the 	/src folder.
	/public is where we have things like the favicon, the little 	icon on the tab of the browser. We also have things like 	index.html, which is the html page that actually renders, 	logos, a manifest.json, which is a special file that allows 	uses  to download or bookmark your website and use it as 	a desktop/homescreen app, and a random robots.txt file.
	The biggest thing that we care about within this file, is 	the index.html, and more specifically the <div id="root">	</div>
	<div id="root"></div> is where React grabs onto the HTML 	page. Where is this happening?
	The /src folder. The /src folder contains the logic of our 	app. As programmers, this is where we're going to work 	with our React app.
	Inside the /src folder, there is something called index.js
	This index.js has something familiar in it. A 	document.getElementById('root'). Once we grab this we 	want to do something with it. Find out what soon.
230. React 18 Update
	React 18 changes some things. In order for us to work 	with the same code base, we need to make sure we are 	running the same versions of the framework we are 	using. If we check our package-lock.json, and look at our 	react dependency, we can see whether or not it is over the 	18th version (e.g. 18.0.2).
	Before version 18, we had reactDOM.render, now we will 	use createRoot instead.
	In React 18, the major change that we really care about 	is the Updates to Client Rendering APIs.
	We can no longer use reactDOM.render.
	REACT < 18
	ReactDOM.render(
		<React.StrictMode>
			<App />
		</React.StrictMode>
	,	document.getElementById('root')
	);
	We must now use createRoot.
	REACT > 18
	const root = ReactDOM.createRoot(
		document.getElementById('root')
	);
	root.render(
		<React.StrictMode>
			<App />
		</React.StrictMode>
	);
	or
	REACT 18+ Shorthand
	ReactDOM.createRoot(
		document.getElementById('root')
	).render(
		<React.StrictMode>
			<App />
		</React.StrictMode>
	);
231. React Fundamentals
	We see that React essentially takes the <div id="root">	</div> and creates an entire page out of that, so let's 	break that down and see how it all works under the 	hood. 
	We have our index.js file, this is the entry point of our 	app. This is the first thing that is read by the computer 	"supposedly". In here we see that we import React from 	'react', this is the React package that we need to write 	React.
	We also have import ReactDOM from 'react-dom', which 	connects the React package to the DOM. The reason 	they're separated into two, is because React can be used 	with anything, it doesn't have to be just for apps on the 	browser. It could be for mobile, that's what React Native 	is for, it could be for VR. 
	Based on where you are using React, you need one to two 	packages for the main React library, and whatever the 	connector is.
	Next we have import './index.css', which is where we add 	all our niceness, and all our CSS and styles for the entire 	page.
	Then we have import App from './App'. This is used in our 	render method.
	ReactDOM.render(
		<React.StrictMode>
			<App />
		</React.StrictMode>
	,	document.getElementById('root')
	);
	What we are doing is saying, Hey ReactDOM, the thing 	that interacts with Document Object Model on the 	browser, use this method that comes with ReactDOM, this 	is pretty much all we use from ReactDOM, render. 
	In here I want you to render 
	<React.StrictMode>
			<App />
	</React.StrictMode>
	Put this on the page, 
	and the next line
	, document.getElementById('root')
	Put the above page, in here.
	We're saying this 
	<React.StrictMode>
			<App />
	</React.StrictMode>
	I want you to render inside of, 	document.getElementById('root')
	Well what is it that we are rendering? What is <App />?
	If we go to App.js, this is what it is. This is what we see on 	the screen.
	What we see on the screen is contained in this App 	component.
232. React Fundamentals 2
	In the /src folder, we have some other things, like 	setupTests.js or reportWebVitals.js. These are just 	configuration files which are nice to have, but aren't 	necessary to help us build the app. If you want you can 	read through the React documentation to learn more. 	The key files that we want to focus on are index.js and 	App.js.
	These are the main things that allow us to build 	components.
	As a developer, always test your understanding. How can 	we verify that the React App is actually being rendered 	inside the <div id="'root'></div>?

	ReactDOM.render(

			<App />

	,	document.getElementById('root')

	We're importing App from App.js, but we're not using it, 	we've replaced it with an h1.
	If we go to our app, and refresh, we will get our <h1>Hello 	World</h1>.
	With React, we are essentially using HTML like syntax, 	like h1, plus creating our own HTML like synta, using 	something called JSX.
233. Hooks vs Classes
	Throughout the React course we will learn that there are 	many ways to write components. One of these ways is 	using hooks.
	The way the course is structure is the way that is 	recommended to learn how to write proper applications.
	Hooks are fairly new and they're exciting A lot of people 	talk about them, but they are also very specific to React. 	What you learn about hooks doesn't really apply 	anywhere else in the programming world, whereas 	classes, that's part of JavaScript. 
	Learning classes is an important concept, not only in 	JavaScript, but a lot of other programming languages. 	Because hooks are fairly new a lot of codebases still use 		classes because there is nothing wrong with classes. The 	way the course is structured, we're going to learn classes 	first, so we know how they work and we can apply that 	knowledge to any of our future projects. then we will 	convert the app, using hooks, so we get to learn both ways.
	We're also starting with classes because there's been a 	little bit of move lately in the industry where people are 	shying away from hooks because the added complexity. 	This is a hot topic of discussion. It doesn't really matter 	which you prefer, the goal is to learn both, so that we can 	decide which one to use best.
234. Class vs Functional App.js
	import logo from './logo.svg';
	import './App.css';
	function App() {
		return (
			<div className="App">
				<header className="App-header'>
					<img src={logo} className="App-						logo" 
					<p>
					Edit <code>src/App.js</code> and 						save
					</p>
					<a
						className="App-link"
						href=""https://reactjs.org"
						target="_blank"
						rel="noopener noreferrer"
					>
						Learning React
					</a>
				</header>
			</div>
		);
	}
	export default App;
	This is a functional component.
	We want to change this into a class, and the way we do 	that is we say class app extends and then return in a 	render method:
	class App extends React.Component {
		render() {
			return (
				<div className="App">
					<header className="App-header'>
						<img src={logo} 									className="App-logo" 
						<p>
						Edit <code>src/App.js</code> 							and save
						</p>
						<a
							className="App-link"
							href=""https://reactjs.org"
							target="_blank"
							rel="noopener noreferrer"
						>
							Learning React
						</a>
					</header>
				</div>
			);
		}
	}
	We then get an error, React is not defined. So we need to 	import that.
	import React from 'react'
	We have now created a class component. We didn't 	change anything, we just converted the component into a 	class.
	Back in the day, this is how the default App.js was like. We 	are using the react package that we installed, using . 	component, this extends, or gives us the power, that comes 	in the react package.
	React.component automatically detects the render 	method, usually you want to add a render method 	because automatically part of the functionality is that it 	is going to run the render and return something, some 	sort of view.
	React.StrictMode just gives us extra protection that we 	don't need to worry about here in the next videos. We will 	focus on the /src folder and building out our components.

237. Your First React Component
	import React from 'react';

	// import App from './App';

	We see that we are 'import'ing React. How come that's 	working, when it didn't before? Before, we had to use 	'require' and browserify, well, React has a webpack 	underneath the hood that does the bundling for us. 
	As long as we keep our imports at the top of the file, we 	can import whatever other files or whatever packages we 	want.
	React and ReactDOM are separate imports. React is the 	view library, the little robot, and it can be used in a lot 	of places, like mobile, vr, and so on.
	ReactDOM is specifically for browsers. It is the glue of 	React. But React Native renders to mobile phones.
	We also have import './index.css'.
	React allows us to add CSS not just for one file, but for 	each different component.
	If we go to App.js, we have App.css that describes the app. If 	we go to index.js, we have index.css. These will only apply 	to whatever components the .js file renders.
	Unlike react packages which we just have to indicate the 	name of the folder, we have to tell it the exact path where 	index.css (for example) lives. The './' syntax just means 	the same directory as the current file. 
	If an import file doesn't have a filetype after it, React will 	automatically assume it is a JavaScript file. E.g:
	import App from './App'; - is - import App from './App.js';
	ReactDOM.render(<App />, 	document.getElementById('root'));
	If we go to App.js 
	import React, { Component } from 'react';

	We can see that we're importing something. We're doing 	something called destructuring here and getting 	component. Along with a couple files logo.svg and App.css.
	We can also see that our syntax is off in our editor so 	make sure that we change the language to JSX or Babel-	JavaScript (Babel).
	Component MUST render something.

		render(){
			return

				<h1>Hello World</h1>

			</div>

	}

	./src/Hello.js

	line 6: Unreachable code
	This is because we are giving the return multiple lines 	here, and return is expecting a single thing. If we enclose 	our expression in parentheses, it will evaluate and return 	a single value.
	class Hello extends Component{
		render(){
			return (
				<div>
					<h1>Hello World</h1>
					<p>Welcome to React</p>
				</div>
			);
		}
	}
	Now it's all working!

		background: red;

	Since we are familiar with CSS, we are going to use 	something a little more advanced, called Tachyons. 	Tachyons allows us to have, similar to Bootsrap, 	predefined class names that we can just add to 	components to make things look really really nice.
	npm install tachyons
	Now we can check if it worked through our package.json 	file. And we can use this very easily by going to our 	index.js file and saying:
	import 'tachyons';
	Now we have access to this package. We can use the 	tachyons site to find some classes that can be useful.
	https://tachyons.io/docs/typography/text-align/
	class Hello extends Component{
		render(){
			return (
				<div class="f1 tc"> - font 1 and text-center
					<h1>Hello World</h1>
					<p>Welcome to React</p>
				</div>
			);
		}
	}
	Everything is working perfect, however if we go ot our 	page and open the console, it will give us a warning, 	asking did we mean className instead of class.
	Up until this point, we've just kind of written HTML in 	JavaScript files, this is actually something called JSX.
	This is part of React. 
	This is not necessarily breaking separation of concerns, 	because, React works in the idea of components. That is, 	it's better to have functionality and styles per component 	so that each component is it's own separate universe. The 	idea of sepaation of concerns with React, is that 	components, well they're concerned about themselves, 	nothing else.
	JSX is used to create the React virtual DOM and that's 	how we are able to use HTML, because to React, it's all 	getting put into the VDOM and matched with the DOM. As 	long as you wrap it in a bracket, you can use multiple 	lines of HTML.
	Class is a keyword in JavaScript, class Hello extends and 	<div class="f1 tc"> both use class, this actually broke 	React at one point, but now just gives the nice error from 	earlier, so that we use <div className="f1 tc'>.
	Use className in JavaScript instead of class.
	Within the ReactDOM.render(<Hello greeting={'Hello' + 	'React Ninja'}/>, document.getElementById('root')) we 	can add something called props, or properties.
	This would be called a greeting prop. Think of them as an 	html attribute. This will give you:
	Compiled with warnings.
	./src/index.js
	   Line 8: Unepected string concatenation of literals no-	useless-concat
	This is for adding the two strings unnecessarily. It even 	gives nice suggestion errors. 
	Now in Hello.js we have access to props.
	class Hello extends Component{
		render(){
			return (
				<div class="f1 tc"> - font 1 and text-center
					<h1>Hello World</h1>
					<p>{this.props.greeting}</p>
				</div>
			);
		}
	}
	The site will now say, HelloReact Ninja.
	The code is saying this object Hello has properties, props 	that is greeting. Now we have access to whatever 	property, and whatever property value, we get from Hello.
	This is just a function.
	const Hello = (props) => {
		return (
			<div class="f1 tc"> - font 1 and text-center
					<h1>Hello World</h1>
					<p>{props.greeting}</p>
				</div>
			);
	}
	Think of React as that: we're just creating these functions 	that take parameters and these parameters, which are 	given by attributes and values, are just being rendered.

	const card = (name, email, id) => {
	This might be confusing if you're new to destructuring 	but as tou saw step by step, we're just receiving props, and 	we're destructuring the props, right isnide of the brackets 	{}.

239. React.Fragment and Semantic HTML
	React implemented something so you're not creating 	extraneous divs when returning multiple tags in JSX. 	Instead of
	<div>
		<p></p>
		<p></p>
	</div>
	We can now use:
	<React.Fragment>
		<p></p>
		<p></p>
	</React.Fragment>
	OR
	import React, { fragment } from 'react';
	<Fragment>
		<p></p>
		<p></p>
	</Fragment
	OR
	<>
		<p></p>
		<p></p>
	</>

But without 	having something called a key prop, if some of these 	cards got deleted, React won't know which one's which 	and will have to change the entire DOM, whereas if it 	had a key prop, it can know exactly which one was 	removed and remove that specific one from the DOM 	without rerendering all of the rest. 	Good to go and lookin great! But how can we make this 	interactive? Let's think about this logically. We have an 	App, we have a SerachBox, and we have a CardList. The 	SearchBox component needs to comunicate with the 	CardList component, and same for the CardList. CardList 	needs to know what is in the search box so that it can 	filter out robots accordingly.
	In order for these two to communicate, we have a one way 	data flow, that is, they need to send their data to the 	parent, and the parent tells them what to do.
	Up until now, we have just had some data that we just 	trickled down, but never had it where one of the children 	had to modify data, or communicate, with a neighbor.
	In order to do this, React has an idea of something 	called 'state'. Up until now we learned about props such 	as properties that we keep passing down. But we've never 	changed them because React just reads the props, if i go to 	CardList, it just reads the props that it receives (robots) 	and it just renders something. This one way data flow is 	really nice beacuse this CardList is a pure function.  It 	receives an input and then always returns the same 	output. If robots is always the same, it's always going to 	return the same thing. It's deterministic, pure functions, 	and it's something that we've talked about before. This is 	a really good thing. These components are what's called 	'pure components' or some even call it 'dumb components'. 
	This just means you don't really need to know about 	anything other that the fact that they're pure functions 	that receive something and return something. This is 	really nice because we always know what this is going to 	look like. That's all we've had up until this point. But now 	we have to worry about something other than props. Props 	never change. Props are just inputs that we get and we've 	never modified them.
	In this case, we need a memory in our app, we need one 	to communicate with the other, and also change and 	update accordingly. That's what - state - is in React.
	'State' and you'll hear this in computer programming a 	lot, simply means the description of your app. A 'state' is 	simply an object. An object that describes your 	application, and this 'state' which describes our 	application, is the robots, and whatever's entered in the 	search box. 'State' is able to change. We're able to change 	the value of the search box, the valu of the input, and 	we're able to change what robots array means, what gets 	displayed.
	Props are simply things that come out of 'state'.
	A Parent feeds 'state' into a child component, and as soon 	as (a) child component(s) receives a 'state', it's a a 	property. That child can never change that property. the 	parent just tells it what the 'state' is, and the child 	receives it as 'robots'.
	The first thing we need to do is to start being able to use 	'state' in our app, the description of what our 'state' 	should be. So let's do a constant 'state' for now and show 	you that our 'state' needs to have a 'robots' array, and it 	needs to have, let's call it 'searchfield'. 
	const state = {
		robots: robots,
		searchfield: ''
	}
	But in order to use 'state' we have to go back to our 	original way that we created React components back 	when we first did create React app, we create a class. We 	do that by:
	class App extends React.Component {
	OR

	Now, if we save this, everything is working fine, and we 	have our 'state', which is robots and search field, and this 	'state' as said before is what describes our app. these are 	the things that can change. The component that 	is the parent, that just kind of passes state to different 	components.

246. Building A React App 4
	We're going to get started with something that may be 	new and confusing at first but by the time we get into the 	net section we'll come back and review exxactly what 	we're doing.
	I want to make app more realistic. In a real life app, we 	wouldn't necessarily have the robots written in robots.js 	like we do here.
	In real life, we're not manually entering users, we would 	use something like an API.
	What that means is, well, we want, when this Website gets 	loaded, to make a request somewhere out there, in the 	world of the internet, and grab these robots from a server. 	Well we can actually do that with JSONPlaceholder.
	They actually have a really nice URL that we can use and 	they return what we need, a name, an ID, and a 	username.
	Just by typing in this URL you get a list of users.
	https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users
	Looking at our App component, we can see that it is what 	we call a smart component, unline the SearchBox or the 	CardList, that are just pure functions.
	App.js has state. A piece of data that describes our app. 	Because they have state, they are considered a smart 	component. Smart coponents tend to have the class 	syntax. 
	In real life when we start up this app, robots would 	actually be an empty array because there's nothing there, 	we haven't grabbed the users. So most likely if I save this, 	robots wouldn't display until I load the Website and as 	soon as the Website gets loaded I ask for the users from 	somewhere over the internet. Somewhere hopefully, here 	(jsonplaceholder).
	Let's do that. React comes with a few other things isnide 	Component that we can use. They're called lifecycle 	methods, or lifecycle hooks.  What they do is, if we run 	these methods, it will automatically trigger when this app 	gets loaded on the Website.
	We have three sections.
	Mounting
	Updating
	Unmounting
	The way React works is we do something called mounting. 	When we click refresh, the app component, gets mounted 	into the document, with the id of 'root'.
	If we got back to our HTML file, our website is nothing but 	a div wth an id of 'root'
	When we say we mount a component, we're replacing this 	'root' and adding our entire app. That's mounting. 	Mounting is the start of the app. When mounting, these 	are the hooks that get hit, and they are in order.
	constructor()
	componentWillMount()
	render()
	componentDidMount()
	The first thing it does is it checks "Hey does this  	component have a constructor? Will this App have a 	constructor? Yes, Okay, then run this piece of code 	(constructor()).
	Does this app have componentWillMount()? No it doesn't, 	then ignore it.
	Does it have render()? Yes, then run this render().
	Does it have componentDidMount()? No, ignore it. 
	It runs through this every time.
	It also has something called updating, and that is, 	whenever a component changes. E.g. On our app we had 	the list and the search box, every time we type something, 	the CardList component gets re-rendered because we have 	new information, the function receives new inputs. Same 	with the input component.
	So again, within these components, if we wanted to (since 	theyre 'updating', we can add 
	componentWillReceiveProps(), 
	shouldComponentUpdate(),
	componentWillUpdate(),
	render(),
	componentDidUpdate()
	Finally, with Unmounting, is when a component is 	removed from a page, for example if we change to a 	different page, the component will unmount, the app 	component will get deleted from the page.
	These are called lifecycle hooks because they run every 	time a component does something, and it comes with 	React.
	You can just put them in your class component and they'll 	automatically get triggered.
	Say we want to use componentDidMount, which gets 		called after render(). This says that yes the app 	component is mounted on the webpage, and it is 	rendered, that is, displaying something.
	componentDidMount() {
		console.log("Check");
	}
	Because this is part of React, we're not using arrow 	functions here.
	This gets called automatically, and posts Check in the 	console when your app page refreshes.
	Using this we can do something interesting.
	componentDidMount() {
		this.setState({robots: robots});
	}
	It's really really fast, but we are getting our robots.
	We are saying, run the constructor, with an empty array, 	just to show the order of execution here, we can add a 
	console.log('1'); in our App class.
	console.log('2'); in our componentDidMount()
	console.log('3'); in our render()
	If we save this, and open up our console, we have 1, 3, 2, 	3. Let's change them to words, constructor(), 	componentDidMount(), render().
	constructor, render, componentDidMount, render
	Why did render run again? Well if you remember, we 	created the constructor, we ran render, and then we 	updated the state in componentDidMount(). Because we 	updated state, every time state changes, remember we go 	through the lifecycle, it's updating, it runs render. 
	So because it goes from an empty array to a robots list, 	render gets re-run, and the VirtualDOM notices that 	there's a difference and repaints our web browser to 	incude the robots. 
	Knowing what we just did, we can remove the console.logs 	and finally make this more realistic.
	Instead of using the robots file, we're actually going to 	remove import { robots } from "./robots'; and in 	componentDidMount() we're gunna see a cool way to 	grab the list from JSONPlaceholder.
	I'm going to copy the JSONPlaceholder link, and we can 		put fetch, which we'll learn about later. We're just saying, 	make this hTTP request.
	componentdidMount() {
		fetch('https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users')
		.then(response => {
			return response.json();
		})
		.then(users => {
			this.setState({ robots: users})
		)};
	We can clean this up and bring the functiosn to one line, 	and we can see that we are simply updating state after we 	fetch. We 'fetch' whatever the users are, we get a 'response' 	and we use a little '.json' magic on it which we'll discuss 	later, then we're getting the 'users' and updating the 	users with '.setState'.
	componentdidMount() {
		fetch('https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users')
		.then(response => response.json())
		.then(users => this.setState({ robots: users}));
	}
	Works beautifully! If we remove 
	this.setState({ robots: users})); and replace it with {};
	We see that we don't receive the robots. We only receive the 	robots after we update with these users 	(JSONPlaceholder).
	This looks pretty scary, that was a lot. But to feel a little 	more comfortable, because we're using fetch() and there's 	nothing attached to it, does this mean that it's part of the 	window object? If we open the console and do 	window.fetch, sure enough, fetch is a method on the 	window object. It comes with all browsers now. It is a tool 	for us to make requests to servers. As we can see, 	JSONPlaceholder is a server that returns for us, when we 	do /suers, a list of users that we can use, which is perfect 	for our app.
	What if this was really slow, and we had a whole ton of 	users?
	In that case you could use something like an if statement.
	if (robots.length === 0) {
		return <h1>Loading</h1>
	} else {
		return (
			<div className='tc'>
				<h1 className=''f1'>RoboFriends</h1>
				<SearchBox 	searchChange={this.onSearchChange}/>
				<CardList robots={filteredRobots} />
			</div>
		);
	}
	If we save, we get Failed to compile. 
	./src/App.js
	  Line 30: 'robots' is not defined
	Because its this.state.robots, again because we're referring 	to the state of the app.
	if (this.state.robots.length === 0) {
	Now this will work great! However, if we remove the robots 	list again, and just pass an empty object {}, we now get 	'Loading".
	This is how you build real apps with React. You have a 	Loading Bar, you can just add to 
	if (this.state.robots.length === 0) {
		return <h1>Loading></h1>
	You can  make requests with componentDidMount(), and 	interact with components.
	We have ourselves our app. there's one last thing we'll see 	with React, and we've learned the basics. We can take this 	now to build any app we want. One more piece!
247. Building A React App 5
	Our app is looking good. If we change the size to smaller 	width, it's responsive, however if we scroll down, well we 	lose our search bar at the top. Wouldn't it be nice if this 	was at the top and I have a scroll that I can do and still 	search through my friends?
	We might be thinking, there's probably a CSS style that we 	can add here, but we want to illustrate that React, and 	why React is so good, is because you can create 	components that are reusable. Not just Card components, 	but functionality.
	Wouldn't it be cool if there were something we could add 	around CardList. Maybe we can create a 'Scroll' 	component? And this 'Scroll' component just wraps the 	CardList.
	<Scroll>
		<CardList rovots={filteredRobots} />
	</Scroll>
	Now CardList will be completely scrollable. 
	BUT now we can use 'Scroll', this component, anywhere in 	my app, any time I need scrolling, I can just wrap it 	around. Let's give it a try.
	import Scroll from './scroll';
	Create new file scroll.js, change to Babel/JSX
	import React from 'react';
	const scroll = () => {
		return
	};
	export default Scroll;
	We want to return something, but it doesn't make sense 	does it? 'Scroll' is not a self-closing component, it wraps 	component.
	So how can we tell 'Scroll' "Hey, just render whatever's 	inside of you."
	Up to this point, we've learned about 'props' and we've 	learned about 'state', there's one other thing in React 	that we need to learn, and that is, Children. 'Children is 	<CardList> in this example. 'Scroll' can use 'children' as a 	way to render its children.
	In here, every 'props' object, has children.
	const Scroll = (props) => {
		return props.children
	};
	If we save and go back, everything is rendered.
	If we remove props.children and add <h1>Hi</h1>, we 	simply get Hi on our webpage.
	Props.children is exactly that. If we console.log(props); we 	get an object. Even though we didn't pass any 'props' to 	'Scroll', automatically, every single component in React 	has this property, 'children', and this children, it kinda 	looks confusing here, but has 'CardList'. Type: CardList. 
	Using this 'props.children', we can create Components that 	wrap other Components.
	Up until now, we were only able to wrap these 'lower case 	components' e.g. <h1> which are kind of like the HTML 	tags, but every time we had a custom Component, with a 	capital letter that we built, e.g. SearchBox, well, we 	couldn't really wrap it. Now we can using this.children. 
	Using 'props.children' we can create a scrollable 	component.
	const Scroll = (props) => {
		return (
			<div>
				{props.children}
			</div>
		);
	}
	Now all we have to do is stlye this div. We could create a 	scroll.css file, and add a style so it's scrollable, but we 	want to explore another way, which is using <style>.
	Just like in HTML, using this <style> attribute, we can add 	styles but using double curly brackets {{}}, that is, this is a 	JavaScript expression, and within this I'm returning an 	object, and this object can have CSS styles, such as 	overflowY. CSS = overflow-y, JSX = overflowY. JSX ALWAYS 	uses camelCase.
	<div style={{ overflowY: 'scroll', border: '1px solid black', 	height: '500px'}}
	Now we have a scrollable component!!! Even if we make it 	smaller, it is a scrollable component.
	This is a basic app, but this is the power of React. We've 	created these simple unctions and the only thing we tell it 	to do is, well, we tell it what 'state' it has. The entire app is 	described by these two things, 'robots' and 'searchfield', 	and React takes care of the rest. As soon as 'searchfield' or 	'robots' changes, React trickles down all the information 	as props to all these components, and 'magically' creates 	this view for us. That's why React is so powerful ,it's a view 	layer. It takes care of manpulating the DOM, and all we 	tell it are, what's the state, what are the functions that 	you wanted to work with the state? And the rest? Well it 	optimizes it to make sure everything is super fast. And you 	can see how amazingly fast everything is here, it's a super 	snappy app, and these components are now reusable. I 	can bring 'SearchBox' into another part of the website, I 	can add 'Scroll' to another part of the site. This is what 	makes React so powerful, and why so many sites are built 	on React.
	Once you learn the syntax and you get used to 	everything, that's when React truly becomes powerful. 	Although we took some time to make this app, once you 	get used to this, you can create in, well, less than an 	hour, probably 30 minutes.
248. Building A React App 6
	One final thing we've kind of overlooked until this point, 	well, we didn't wanna get bogged down with too many 	things, if we take a look at our project, well, it's a lot of 	files. Even though it's a simple app, you can imagine how 	much bigger and bigger this /src can get. Organization 	is a big part of working on projects. Folder structure is one 	of the most important things, so let's clean up this code. 	We'll see what Andrei would do to make this app more 	extensible, that is, cleaner so when other people come on 	the team they can understand it.
	Let's create some folders. One will be called components, 	and these are the components that we've built, such as 	'CardList', 'SearchBox', and 'Scroll'.
	The second folder will be called, containers. Containers 	are these smart components, the 'containing' components, 	that have state in them, that have lifecycle hooks, that 	have the class syntax.
	These components, or these containers, we can't use a pure 	function with them, because technically, they're not pure, 	they have 'state' that they modify within these functions. 	And that's okay. It's impossible to have just pure functions, 	because most of the time we need to make requests to the 	outside world.
	These components are special, and we're going to call 	them containers. They contain things, they just pass down 	state to components. 
	Move App.css, and App.js to /src/containers
	Move SearchBox, Scroll, SEgA.woff, Card, and Cardlist to 	/src/components
	Now we get a bunch of errors Failed to compile.
	./src/App.js
	Module build failed: Error: ENOENT: no such file or 	directory, open 	'/Users/aneagoie/Desktop/robofriends/src/App.js'
	All of our directories are invalid. We can rewrite them.
	We are in /src/container while in App.js.
	import CardList from "../components/CardList';
	import SearchBox from '../component/SearchBox';
	import Scroll from '../components/Scroll'
	The ../ instead of ./ moves you up one directory level, e.g. 	from /src/container, to /src/ just like in bash.
	We also have index.js that is referring to App.js which is in 	containers.
	import App from './containers/App'; we're not leaving the 	foler, we're in the same neighborhood.
	Everything works, now it's time to clean up a bit. We use 	this.state a lot, we can just say, using destructuring:
	const { robots, searchfield } = this.state;
	Now we can simply remove this.state as this.state.robots 	and robots, are the same.
	We can clean up if (robots.length === 0) {
	By doing
	if (!robots.length) {
	Because robots.length will equal 0 which equates to false 	in JavaScript, so if we do opposite of that, it's truth.
	Where we see if else statements, we can just use return.
	return !robots.length ? - (this will return only if true)
	<h1>Loading</h1> : (else this will happen) ->
	( 
		<className='tc'>...
	);
	Now things are much cleaner and we have an app that 	renders all our things for us.
	To finish off, remember packaged.json? How we had pre-	built scripts, and we had this 'build' command. If we run
	npm run build
	While it's running, it creates a new folder 'build' and 	within here, we have all of our files, but this time, 	optimized. If we go to static, and go to js, everything is 	minified for us, or CSS is optimized, and everything that 	we need to deploy the website is in the folder. Now we can 	take this build folder and use it anywhere on the 	internet. It even gives you some good information in the 	terminal.
	This build folder can now be deployed anywhere.

250. Keeping Your Projects Up To Date
	Let's say you're downloading a project, or sharing a 	project with other devleopers, but let's say, it was written 	last year. How can we update this project? 
	When we look at the package.json file say we have React 	16.2.0, but the current version is much higher. Let's 	update this project like we would any other project. Let's 	clone the robofriends repo.
	And open /robofriends in our code editor. We can see our 	package.json. Usually when we download a project, we 	want to make sure it works, so we do npm install in the 	directory, and then we do npm run. But during npm 	install we get a bunch of errors or warnings, saying we've 	audited some of your packages and there's a few 	vulnerabilities. Actually quite a lot, and two critical. So 	we can run npm audit fix to fix this. Now we get another 	issue that twenty seven vulnerabilities required manual 	review and could not be updated. 
	This is mainly becuase they may contain "breaking 	changes' which would break our app.
	This is the tradeoff when updating libraries. You are 	using an older version of a library that may have 	changed and now breaks your app completely, so 	sometimes you want  to go one by one to actually check 	and see what the changes are in these libraries.
	If we do npm audit, it will actually give us some info on 	the issues. 
	Before we do npm audit fix --force, which will force all of 	these changes, we're gunna explore this package.json.
	Our react-scripts are out of date. One thing we can do is 	npm update, and it will look at the version and say 	"What's the highest version that I can update to. 
	Because we used the circumflex ^ this says it can upgrade 	to whatever the highest version is in the minor release. 	E.g. 16.1.0 -> 16.10.2
	If we use ">16.0.2" this will update minor releases too, e.g. 	16.0.2 -> 17.0.2.
	We do it with the carrot to make sure that we only ever 	update whatever is in the minor reease, which tends not 	to have any breaking changes.
	We have the React version updated, but we still have these 	vulnerabilities that we need to fix. Let's now run the npm 	audit fix --force, assuming that we've looked through the 	changed and we assume that it's okay.
	Run it, and again keep in mind, when using force you 	want to make sure that you actually review the updates.
	Another thing we can do with React is actually go to the 	React website and checked the actual version
	We can simply change it in package.json, run npm install 	and we're good to go.
	Once the force fix is done, we will see the react-scripts 	updated, which means we have the current and most up 	to date create-react-app. We can npm audit, and it 	should come back with 0 vulnerabilities.
	Now we still have one issue remaining, does our project 	still work with all of the updates.
	npm start -> new create-react-app asks for a target 	browser, so type y to add defaults to package.json
	Boom, looks like our RoboFriends app is still working, and 	if we open up the console, free of errors!
	We can now push these changes to GitHub, so that we 	don't have the security warnings.
	git add.
	git commit -m"Fixing security issues updated libraries"
	git push origin master
	The easeist way to keep your projects up to date and 	secure, is to just simply update the package.json file.

252. Solution: Try Upgrading To React 18
	Open up the package.json, check the versions, and see if 	you can update it and have everything still run.
	First things first, check the React docs website, and at the 	top, or in the docs it will show you the latest version of 	React. We can also play with Release Candidate Versions, 	which are essentially the betas of the upcoming release, or 	nightlies.
	If we wanted to update to Release Candidate, we can't 	just simply say "react": "^17.0.0",
	We have to scroll through the release docs, and towards 	the bottom there should be an installation portion. We 	can copy that and say
	npm install react@17.0.0-rc.2 react- dom@17.0.0-rc.2
	Now if we go back to our package.json we will see 
	"react": "^17.0.0-rc.2"
	We can npm start and check out our app, and everything 	seems to be looking good and working fine.
253. React Review
	In the beginning, we said that things would get a litte 		overwhelming, but lets take a look at how much we've 	really learned. If we look at the
	Atoms
	Molecules
	Organisms
	Templates
	Pages
	image, we can see that we are creating components, or 	atoms and molecules, and combining them into 	containers, or organisms. We then have our template, 	which is our containers and components, or our atoms, 	molecules, and organisms, lined up, or organized. This 	template creates our page. Our page is everything 	together in harmony. Components built into containers, 	laid out and displayed properly, or atoms and molecules 	built into organisms, and those organisms organized, 	and in the place they need to be to function and 	survive/thrive.
	We also learned that there is one way dataflow, which is, 	everything flows from state to props and you can have 	multiple children but the only people that can change 	state is a container component. 
	In our case we only have one container component, but 	you can have multiple and data just flows down one way, 	and any time a child component changes, it just triggers 	and event on the parent.
	We don't have to think about how to render things, we 	were doing things deterministically. That is, we're saying, 	here's the state of the app, render it accordingly, and 	react takes care of it for us.
	We also learned, well exactly what we said, React worries 	about painting and making the website, look good, we 	just give it the data and components. 
	We learned that React has a great ecosystem. We had 	great tools where we're able to start and build and it 	gave us great errors with or terminal/bash.
	Everything is built for you, so that when you're ready to 	get coding, you don't have to worry about configuration.
	When we get to the final project, we are going to be using 	React components to build something really fast and just 	plug and play these components.
	Finally,
	class Clock extends React.Component {
		constructor(props) {
			super(props);
			this.state = {date: new Date()};
		}
		render() {
			return (
				<div>
					<h1>Hello, world!</h1>
					<h2>It is 	{this.state.data.toLocaletimeString()}.</h2>
				</div>
			);
		}
	}
	ReactDom.render(
		<Clock />
		document.getElementById('root')
	);
	this shouldn't look confusing to us anymore. This makes 	sense. This whole thing that looked intimidating before 	this is Just React. You have a majority of what React is in 	this snippet of code. 
	Once you get used to the syntax, it is an elegant solution 	to building beautiful Web apps that are simple, scalable, 	and easy to manage.
254. Error Boundary In React
	React 16 introduced something called error boundaries, 	and it solved the problem that React had previously, 	which was if there was some sort of an error within a 	component for whatever reason, there wasn't a graceful 	way to handle it. On the next render you'd get all these 	cryptic errors and thigns just wouldn't work out nicely, 	and for a user you want to make sure that even if a part 	of your component tree breaks, you can still have a nice 	UI experience, maybe a piece of text that says something 	went wrong, but not have the app break.
	The first thing we want to do is in the components foler we 	want to adda new file, and we'll call this 	componentErrorBoundary. You need React16 or higher, 	so make sure that is the case in your package.json folder.
	In here we can do the regular
	import React, { Component } from 'react';
	class ErrorBoundary extends Component {
		constructor(props) {
			super(props);
			this.state = {
				hasError: false
			}
		}
		render() {
			return 
		}
	}
	export default ErrorBoundary;
	Ideally with this ErrorBoundary component, we can wrap 	the component, or we can wrap a component such as the 	CardList component, with an ErrorBoundary component. 	And if the CardList component fails, we can catch it in 	the ErrorBoundary. This is a bit hard to follow, but React 	has really good documentation on this taht we can 	always read up on later. 
	The first thing we want to do is check if this.state.hasError 	equals true.
	render() {
		if (this.state.hasError === true) {
			return <h1>Oops, that is not good :(</h1>
		}
		return this.props.children;
	}
	This says that if, for some reason there's an error, we're 	just going to spit out an h1 tag. 
	Otherwise, we're going to say that this.props.children will 	render. Remember, because children will be anything 	that's wrapped in-between ErrorBoundary. 	ErrorBoundary is the parent, so children references any 	tags inside ErrorBoundary.
	So render the children, whatever's inside ErrorBoundary, 	otherwise, render h1.
	How are we going to be able to change this hasError? Well 	with React 16 we have a new lifecycle method that was 	introduced, and it's called componentDidCatch() and 	this is kind of like the try catch block in JavaScript. If 	anything errors out, it will run this lifecycle hook and 	this lifecycle hook, gets a couple parameters, which are 	the error itself, and the info.
	componentDidCatch(error, info) {
		this.setState({ hasErro: true })
	}
	Let's save and go to App.js, and import our ErrorBoundary 	from our components folder.
	import ErrorBoundary from 	'../components/ErrorBoundary';
	We will apply it to our CardList, so that if something 	happens within it, we want to have some sort of a graceful 	exit boundary. 
	<div className='tc'>
		...
		...
		<Scroll>
			<ErrorBoundary>
				<CardList robots={robots: filteredRobots}/>
			</ErrorBoundary>
		</Scroll>
	</div>
	Everything saves, compiles, and the page is running 	smooth.
	Now wih this ErrorBoundary, if anything in the CardList 	failes, it's going to caatch it and display our error 	message. So let's see if it works.
	If we go into Cardlist and do:
	const CardList = ({ robots }) => {
		if (true) {
			throw new Error('NOOOOO')
		}
		return {
			<div>...
	If we save and load our page we will get Error: NOOOOO! 	on the page, and in the console we are getting the actual 	Uncaught Error: NOOOOO! 
	AS WELL AS
	The above error occurred in the <CardList> component:
		...
		...
		...
	React will try to recreate this component tree from scratch 	using the error boundary you provided, ErrorBoundary.
	That's great, but we still SEE the error, our page did not 	load. That is because we're running right now in 	development mode. For create-react-app they want to 	show us whenever there's an error in our component. 	Error Boundaries are really useful when you put your 	apps into production, where you don't have these detailed 	logs of what errors you have. Your users are never going to 	see that. 
	If we refresh the page, we can see our app with the h1 	display "Oops that is not good".
	That is what the users will see, development mode just 	shows us the errors directly so they can be fixed.
	Error Boundaries provide us with that extra safety in 	production.
255. Deploying Our React App
	The RoboFriends project is on GitHub. We should be able to 	put up our RoboFriends app on GitHub. But wouldn't it be 	nice if we can use GitHub pages to actually check out our 	RoboFriends app without having us to clone, download, 	npm install, just like we've done with simple HTML projects.
	Well with create-react-app theres a way to do this, using 	GitHub pages. There's a link to this resource :
	https://create-react-app.dev/docs/deployment
	Step 1: Add a homepage to your package.json.
	Open package.json, and add a homepage field
	"homepage": "https://myusername.github.io/my-app"
	"name": "robofriends",
	"homepage": "https://aneagoie.github.io/RoboFriends"
	Step 2: Install gh-pages and add deploy to scripts in 	package.json
	npm install --save gh-pages
	then open package.json and add 
	"scripts": {
		"predeploy": "npm run build",
		"deploy": "gh-pages -d build",
		"start": "react-scripts start",
		"build": "react-scripts build",
	Step 3: Deploy the site by running npm run deploy.
	npm run deploy
	Errors galore! We have an exra comma we need to remove 	at the end of our build script.
		"scripts": {
		"predeploy": "npm run build",
		"deploy": "gh-pages -d build",
		"start": "react-scripts start",
		"build": "react-scripts build"
	Now it runs, it creates a build folder, and is now creating 	an optimized production build. And it's done, and 	public. 
	All we have to do, is go back to our repository, go to 	settings, and if we scroll all the way down to GitHub 	pages, we have to change the source, to gh-pages branch.
	We might have to wait a few minutes or refresh to apply 	the changes. Then if we click on the link, and refresh, 	there we go, our RoboFriends app is now live at this URL so 	we can just copy this URL and go to my project on GitHub, 	hit edit, and add this URL to the "Website" box and save. 
	Now next time anyone checks out our repository, they can 	just click the link to see the live project.
