Section 20: HTTP/JSON/AJA + Asynchronous JavaScript
257. HTTP/HTTPS
	In 1989 Tim Berners-Lee invented the HTML that changed 	the world, the WWW. But that HTML wouldn't really do 	much if we couldn't fetch documents from around the 	world and different computers.
	For that to happen, Tim built the "HyperText Transfer 	Protocol" or "HTTP" for short. 
	The world wide web is built with wires, recall the 	diagrams from early in the course, Internet Backbone, to 	ISP Network, to Wireless Transmitter, to Wireless Modem 	on Home. But these are just wires. There are no 	instructions on how to do things, for that you need 	something called a protocol. Think of it as a standard 	that everyone follows. If everyone agrees, then the protocol 	works and can be used by everyone.
	HTTP is a protocol, which allows the fetching of resources 	such as HTML documents.
	With the use of HTML and HTTP we now have the World 	Wide Web. We can grab HTML files and receive them.
	HTTP is the protocol, or rules, that we use over the wires.
	It is the foundation of any data exchange on the web. It 	paved the way for something called a "client-server 	protocol" which means requests are initiated by the 	recipient which in our case is the web browser. So they 	send out a request using HTTP, the server now 	understands what the web browser is saying because it's 	speaking in HTTP and the server can now respond with 	HTML and the browser understands it because it's 	speaking through HTTP once again.
	HTTP is a common language that a client and server can 	use to communicate.
	The messages sent by the browsers are called requests, and 	the messages sent from the server are called responses.
	HTTP is quite easy to learn because you only have a few 	words. 
	GET
	POST
	PUT
	DELETE
	HTTP is beautiful because it's simple, you don't have to 	overcomplicate things.
	GET means, I want to GET an HtML file. So you ask to 'get' 	something, request, and the server responds.
	POST says, I want to POST something. That is, I'm going to 	send over some data to Google servers and I want you to 	'POST', I want you to add it to your servers or your 	database.
	PUT is, I'm going to send you some data and I want you 	to update some data, that already exists in google servers 	and just update it with this new information. 
	DELETE means 'delete' a piece of data on the backend, on 	the servers or on the database.
	Twitter as an example, GET will be able to receive the 	Twitter feed, POST would be if you created a new user and 	you want to add that user to the twitter servers. PUT 	would be if you made a tweet but you want to make a 	change or an edit, so you edit that tweet, and DELETE 	would be if you wanted to delete your user account, or 	delete a tweet.
	Over the years HTTP was extended to not only worry about 	text. Not only fetch Hypertet documents like HTML, but 	also images and videos and post content to servers like 	with HTML form results. And HTTP can also be used to 	'fetch' part of documents to update web pages on demand 	which is called AJAX.
	We can make a request with any of the four verbs, GET, 	POST, PUT, or DELETE, but what about the response? The 	server responds with two main things. One is an HTTP 	message.
	https://www.w3schools.com/tags/ref_httpmessages.asp
	The second one is usually some sort of data like HTML. So 	far that,s what we've been doing, HTML. In the coming 	lessons, we will learn that it can send other things that 	just simple HTML.
	Let's see this in action. We can use https://example.com 
	If we open up the console, we've used the elements tab and 	the console tab, now let's look at the network tab. Turn off 	all filters, then simply refresh example.com. We now get 	some network details about the website.
	It shows Name: example.com | Status: 200 OK | Type: 	Document | Initiator: Other | Size: 1.6kb, 1.2kb | Time: 	53ms, 47ms | Waterfall
	If we click on eample.com we get a whole bunch of things 	that we don't really need to worry about, but a few 	things, 
	Request URL, this is what we requested. 
	Status Code, this is the status code from the server.
	Request Method, GET, POST, PUT, or DELETE.
	If we click on the Response tab, there's the response 	message. We received the HTML document from the server. 
	If we close this tab and refresh again, we now get the 	same information, but the Status:200 OK changed to 	Status: 304 Not Modified. 
	Because we've already requested this page, Google is smart 	enough to say 'Oh I'm not going to worry about the server 	and requesting the same thing over and over, I already 	have it memorized, so here it is.
	A URL is an HTTP request. We're saying, 	https://www.w3schools.com/tags/ref_httpmessages.asp 	using HTTP (HTTPS) use the Worldwide Web (WWW) 	request, this specific file 	w3schools.com/tags/ref_httpmessages.asp.
	What if we want to send data? What if we actually want 	to send some information to the server instead of just 	requesting with a URL saying I want this information.
	There's two ways that we can do it.
	One is called Query String.
	The second is through the 'body' of the request.
	Using the form we created when we first went through the 	Advanced HTML section, let's open it up.
	In this form, if you remember, we had a <form 	method="GET'> and now we understand what the GET 	means. It's saying with this form, I want you to do a GET 	request.
	If we open our network tab, and view it while we click 	'Register' we can see the request. We see that we have 	made a request, and it has created these things called 	'Query Strings', the first way of sending data to the server.
	/Desktop/register.html?	firstname=&lastname=&email=&password=&birthday=& 	cars=volvo
	With a GET request if you want to send information 	through a form, it adds it to the URL using the ? which 	says "Hey, I'm about to send you some 'Quesry Strings' and 	it's going to have a key and a value (cars=volvo).
	If we click on the request, we can see there are Query 	String Parameters that we have sent. firstname:, 	lastname:, email:, password:, birthday:, cars: volvo
	That's Query String Parameters using 'GET'.
	We did mention one thing though, we can see that we 	have password in here. That's not very secure is it? If we go 	to the form and register secret as the password, and click 	the request in network, now we have our password out in 	the open, sent over the wire, and we even have it up in our 	URL.
	The second way is through the body of the request. The 	way we do this is by using a POST request.
	<form method='POST'>
	This is something you will more often see. We want to add 	some form data to the server, so we want to POST 	something. 
	If we open this in a new tab now, and enter secret for our 	password and hit register, we can open the network tab, 	and see the request for the POST. It has register.html, 	however there are no query strings and no data in the 	URL. 
	After clicking on the request, we can now see that it 	contains Form Data, as opposed to Query String 	Parameters.
	It even says Content-Type: application/x-www-form-	urlencoded and this is just HTMLs way of saying, this is a 	form a this is the body, but not displaying it here.
	Now the server can access it, not from the query params, 	don't worry we'll learn how servers do that when we get to 	the backend section, instead from the Form Data. 
	One of the issues we still have, is that the password was 	visible in both cases.
	One solution to this was the idea of HTTPS. This means 	HyperText Tansfer Protocol Secure. The communication 	between the browser and the websites is now encrypted. 
	Encrypted just means jumbled up so only the client and 	the server know the secret pass or secret key, to read the 	message. 
	This 'S' part the Secure, uses a technology called Transport 	Layer Security, or it's predecessor, Secure Socket Layer
	TLS - Transport Layer Security
	SSL - Secure Socket Layer
	This ensures than any malicious party that would 	intercept the data, would not be able to decipher the 	information. 
	If you're ever handling secure information, make sure 	there's a HTTPS out there
	Let's review!
	Your web browser is an HTTP client sending requests to the 	server machines. When the browser user enters follow 	requests, by opening a file or typing a URL or clicking a 	hyperlink, the browser builds an HTTP request and sends 	it to communicate with the server. Now the server can get 	information back to us. the earliest version, we could use 	URL parameters such as 'www.google.com/about' and just 	specifically get HTML. 
	Then came the form, so we can send more data other 	than the URL saying we want to go to this HTML file. Now 	we can use GET or POST to send data, either through a 	body, or a query string, and a server can take action 	based on that data and return a new page. 
	This is client server communication, this is the 	WorldWideWeb.
258. JSON
	We talked about URL parameters, such as 	'google.com/about' and query string parameters we can 	do with GET. What about the data when we POST 	something? Can we POST anything? Not really. When 	echanging data between a browser and a server, the data 	can only be text. We can't just send a JavaScript object, 	such as 
	const user = {
		firstName: 'John',
		lastName: 'Doe'
	};
	one, because HTTP won't understand it, but also if we're 	sending this data to Google servers, unlike the front-end, 	where HTML, CSS, and JavaScript is a standard, when we 	send a user object to the server, the server can be in any 	language like Python, GO, PHP. If we send them a 	JavaScript object, they will be really confused because they 	won't know what it is, it's not their language. 
	So we need a way to have a standard way to send data 	over the wires and receive it.
	That is what JSON is.
	JSON: JavaScript Object Notation
	JSON is a syntax for storing and exchanging data.
	JSON is text, written with JavaScript Object Notation.
	It's used to send data between machines. Is it the only 	way? No, theres also XML. Both JSON and XML can be used 	to receive data from a web server.
	JSON
	{
	  "employees"
	  [
	  {"firstName":"John", "lastName":"Doe",
	  {"firstName":"Anna", "lastName":"Smith",
	  {"firstName":"Peter", "lastName":"Jones"
	  ]
	}
	XML
	<employees>
	  <employee>
		<firstName>John</firstName> <lastName>					Doe</lastName>
	  </employee>
	  <employee>
	  <employee>
		<firstName>Anna</firstName> <lastName>				Smith</lastName>
	  </employee>
	  <employee>
	  <employee>
		<firstName>Peter</firstName> <lastName>					Jones</lastName>
	  </employee>
	</employees>
	XML used HTML-like syntax, while JSON uses JavaScript 	Object-like syntax.
	But the standard is now more towards JSON because when 	you're writing front-end code in JavaScript, getting a 	JSON data back, makes it easier to load that data into 	and object tree because it's so similiar to a JavaScript 	object.
	JSON format is a more succint way which saves bandwidth 	and improves response times when sending messages back 	and forth between client and server.
	XML is just not very efficient. So JSON now is more of a 	standard, you'll still see XML but it is outdated so we 	won't be talking about it but they both are used to 	transfer data to machines, that might not speak the same 	language.
	JSON however, although it looks similar to JavaScript, it is 	a little bit different, it has these double quotes for 	properties, everything has the be string, wrapped around 	double quotes, but JSON can be read by any language.
	If Google servers was running a different language they'll 	understand JSON, they'll be able to modify that JSON, 	which is just text, into their own version. 
	So now we can convert this user object into a JSON object, 	send it over HTTP, and then the Google servers or whatever 	it is will change it to their own language to understand 	it, and then send a response, again let's say for example it 	was returning, the user has money, 345 dollars.
	const money = 345
	It will then modify this to JSON, send it over the wire, and 	then the web browser	will change it from JSON to 	JavaScript. How can we do that?
	Luckily for us JavaScript comes with it's own JSON 	function.
	JSON.parse()
	var obj = JSON.parse('{ "name":"John", "age":"30", 	"city":"New York"}');
	JSON.stringify()
	var myJSON = JSON.stringify(obj);
	With JSON.parse() if we have JSON (like above) and pass it 	as a parameter of parse, it will return an object which we 	can assign to a variable.
	With stringify, the opposite is true, in that if you pass it the 	obj we just created with parse, it will stringify it (back) 	into JSON.
	So this myJSON variable can now be put into the HTTP 	request to send it over the wire, or the server can respond 	with myJSON.
	Review: JSON is text and we can convert any JavaScript 	object into JSON and send JSON to the server. We can also 	convert any JSON received from the server, into JavaScript 	objects with no complicated translation.
	This text can be used as a data format for any 	programming language.
	Flow: 
	const user = {
		firstName: 'John',
		lastName: 'Doe'
	};
	Before we can send this, we: 
	JSON.stringify(user);
	Now it's converted into a JSON string, and this will be sent 	over HTTP to the Google servers.  The server will then say 	JSON.parse(user) so it understands it, let's say it was 	running JavaScript or Node. Now server understands user, 	and they'll say "Oh he wants the money amount for this 	user", so it gets
	const money = 345
	from the account, it stringifies it
	JSON.stringify(money);
	Then sends it as a response along with Status Code: 200.
	Now the web browser can parse the string that it received:
	JSON.parse(money);
	and can see that "Oh yeah I have a variable money of 	$345. 
259. JSON vs. Form Data
	Originally, the only way to submit form data to a server 	was through the <form> tag in HTML. As we have learned 	with either a POSt or GET request. 
	With JSON you can now grab the contents of the <input> 	in a form and submit those with JSON instead of as a 	form data.
	You can now submit to the server whenever you want 	without it nevessarily being a <form>, through AJAX...
260. AJAX
	We learned about HTTP and JSON, we learned that we 		can send requests that receive responses. But up until now 	every itme we need to communicate with the server, we 	make that request then the browser, when it receives the 	response does a page refresh. Originally page loading was 	simple, send a request to a server, as long as nothing went 	wrong, the assets would be downloaded by the web page 	and displayed on your computer. The trouble with this 	model is that whenever you want to update any parts of 	the page, for example display a new set of products, like 	on Amazon. You'll have to load the entire program 	again. This is extremely wasteful, and results in a poor 	user eperience.
	This issue led to the creation of technologies that allow 	web pages to request small chunks of data, such as HTML, 	XML, plain text, JSON, and display them only when 	needed.
	The technology, is/was called AJAX. 
	AJAX allows you to read from a web server after the page 	has loaded and update a web page without reloading the 	page. And finally, send data in the background while the 	user is interacting with the Website.
	AJAX was pioneered by Google in 2006 and now is what we 	expect of websites and any web app you see online, uses 	AJAX now. See, Google had this big problem, when 	searching something, you would initially type your 	search, and it would do a complete page refresh.But now 	with AJAX, they can maintain the top bar, and have the 	bottom load the data. I can even have suggestions 	(googl(e drive)) brought up from the server.
	AJAX is just a technology, a method of combining pieces 	together. It was achieved using a tool that browsers built 	and it was called XML HTTP request.
	The old way: XHR
	var request = new XMLHTTPRequest();
	request.open('GET', '/my/url', true);
	request.onload = function() {
		if (request.status >= 200 && request.status < 400) {
		// Success!
		  var data = JSON.parse(request.responseText);
		} else {
		  // We reached our target server, but it returned an 			  error
		}
	};
	request.onerror = function {
		//There was a connection error of some sort
	};
	request.send();
	The new way: jQuery
	$.getJSON('/my/url', function(data) {

	});
	We can do AJAX requests with either of these methods, 	however, there's an even nicer newer way that we've 	actually already used when we built our React app, and 	it's supported by the browsers, and that is called 'fetch'.
	The New New Way: Fetch
	fetch('/my/url').then(response => {
		console.log(response);
	});
	As we remember, it was fetch, the URL, then we did this 	'.then(response)' and we received a response.
	This is a really good thing because page updated are a 	lot quicker and you don't have to wait for the page to 	refresh. The site feels faster and more responsive. Also less 	data is being trasnferred which wasted less bandwidth.
	AJAX allows web pages, and by extension, web 	applications, to change content dynamically, and it is 	everywhere.
	It looks intimidated but it's just a matter of doing this 	fetch that does HTTP for you and all you do is say if you 	want to a GET, a OST, and add some JSON data to that 	request.
	Review what happens with AJAX.
	An event occurs, such as logging in. An XML HTTP request 	object, again something that web browsers have 	implemented, is created, and that is using JavaScript.
	XML HTTP request object sends a request to the web server, 	the server processes the request and then the server sends a 	response back to the web page. 
	The response is read by the JavaScript and the user is able 	to login. At the same time only updated a small portion of 	the window.
	This is what a sinlge page application is. and it's a word 	you've probably heard before, and it's a trend in how to 	make web apps. Where you load a base, an almost empty 	page, and build the content on the fly based on the data 	fetched from the server.
	Udemy for example. When we click on, say, public 	relations, we see the page loading one at a time, the top 	bar loaded, then a pause, then the rest of the data. If we 	go back to 'Photography' for example, again, parts of the 	web page load dynamically, and that's the beauty. these 	applications nearly never do a full reload. They destroy 	the previous content, all or part of it, and rebuild it 	based on new data - new page.
	Just like our RoboFriends app! We fetched the URL that 	gave us the users.
	componentDidMount() {
		fetch('https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users')
			.then(response => response.json())
			.then(users => {this.setState({ robots: users })});
	}
	Fetch is part of the window object. Window.fetch works in 	the console. So fetch, this URL. This URL is a JSON object.
	Let's try that in the console.
	fetch('https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users')
	We get something called a Promise. This is something in 	JavaScript that you'll get used to. Promise is saying 'Hey, 	I'm making a request to somewhere over the Internet and 	I promise to let you know when I have this value 	returned.'
	The way we access this promise is,'Once this is returned 	give me the value', you do '.then()'. And this takes the 	response as a parameter.
	fetch('https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users')		
	.then(response => console.log(response));
	We get the Promise, as well as a Response!
	Status: 200, and we also have body: ReadbleStream.
	Fetch, we learned about JSON.parse to parse JSON, but 	fetch actuay comes with its own method called, well, 	'json'. So on the response, if we do .json it will actually 	convert it for us.
	.then(response => response.json())
	So if we go back to our console and do this:
		fetch('https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users')			.then(response => response.json));
	We get a Promise again, which means we need to do a 	'.then' to receive whatever this returns, the promise, which 	is the value. 
	So in that case we do
		fetch('https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users')			.then(response => console.log(response));
	and see what data we receive, so 
	.then(data => console.log(data);
	If we press enter, we get back all 10 of our users. the same 	users that were in our URL.
	We simply used the fetch API, which allows us to do an 	AJAX call after the component mounted. So once our 	RoboFriends app was loaded we made an AJAX request 	using the fetch API. We got a response, which was a 	promise. So we had to do .then, and this 'response', this is 	just standard anytime you do fetch you'll just remember 	response.json. We converted this into something that, well, 	we can use in JavaScript, and because this returned a 	promise, we did a .then, and we got the 'users' and now 	we have the users object. 
	We never had to refresh the page.
	By the way, if we want the JSON whenever we're accessing 	it to look prettier and be a little bit more readable, there 	is a Chrome extension called JSONView.
	We now have the ability to dynamically load our web 	pages, make single page applications using AJAX, and 	AJAX is a combination of tools, using the fetch API, using 	HTTP, and using JSON to communicate with servers. Now 	we have a complete picture of our front-end.
	Our front-end can have HTML, CSS, JavaScript, React. We 	can use AJAX to communicate with the servers, and we use 	JSON to send data over the wire.
262. Promises
	We should all be jumping for joy that we have them instead of callbacks.
	A Promise is an object that may produce a single value sometime in the 	future. Either a resolved value, or a reason that it's not resolved, or rejected.
	A promise may be in one of three possible states, fulfilled, rejected, or 	pending. 
	In order to fully grasp the sconcept we must first talk about what we had 	before promises, and that is callbacks. Something like this:
	el.addEventListener("click", submitForm);
	When something is done, execute this piece of code. Callbacks in 	JavaScript are exactly that.
	I text my friend Bob and say "Hey Bob, what's that really good song by the 	Backstreet Boys?"
	When Bob has time, he texts me back saying "Oh yeah, that song, it's 'I want 	it that way'".
	That's exactly what's happening her too. We have an element that we're 	adding an event listener to, and when a user clicks, we're going to have a 	callback, submitForm. That is, once the user clicks, we're going to answer 	with submitForm().
	// callback pyramid of doom
	movePlayer(100, 'Left', function() {
		movePlayer(400, 'Left', function() {
			movePlayer(10, 'Right', function() {
				movePlayer(330, 'Left', function() {
				});
			});
		});
	});
	Let's say we're creating a game and we have this move player function that 	has the distance to move the player, and the direction.
	If I wanted to move player 100 to the left I'd run that, once that is done, I want it 	to run a callback function, which is to move player 400 to the left again, and 	then another callback function right after that is done to move plater 10 to the 	right and another callback option to move player 330 to the left. 
	We have something called the pyramid of doom.
	This is obviously a simple example but with callbacks, you'll get this nested - 	nested function and you create this pyramid of really really complicated code 	of this happens, then this happens, then this happens, and it's really really 	hard to read.
	Let's see a more realistic example. Let's say we had some sort of app that 	uses Twitter.
	grabTweets('twitter/andreineagoie', (error, andreiTweets) => {
		if (error) {
			throw Error;
		}
		displayTweets(andreiTweets)
		grabTweets(twitter/elonmusk', (error, elonTweets) => {
			if (error) {
				throw Error;
			}
			displayTweets(elonTweets)
			grabTweets(twitter/vitalikbuterin', (error, vitalikTweets) => {
				if (error) {
					throw Error;
				}
				displayTweets(vitalikTweets)
			}
		}
	}
	We have grabTweets function and the first parameter has the URL, for now 	simplified to twitter and twitter handle, and then the callback function after you 	grab the tweets, which has an error parameter and a tweets parameter.
	If there's an error throw an Error, so that just creates an error in JavaScript. 	Otherwise, we're going to display the tweets, but then if Andrei's tweets were 	successful, then we also want to grab the tweets again. Let's do Elon Musk 	this time and again if error, we throw an Error, otherwise, we're going to 	display his tweets, and if that's successful then we're also going to grab 	Vitalik Buterin's tweets, and again if error, throw Error, otherwise display 	tweets.
	That doesn't look very pretty does it? We have to do the same thing over and 	over and over. 
	Now, promises serve the same purpose as callbacks, so why do we have 	two things?
	Promises are new in ES6 and they are a little bit more powerful.
	Let's see what a promise looks like. This move player code in callback form, 	would look something like this in promises:
	movePlayer(100, 'Left')
		.then(() => movePlayer(400, 'Left'))
		.then(() => movePlayer(10, 'Right'))
		.then(() => movePlayer(330, 'Left'))
	This looks much much better, but it can be hard to grasp what's happening 	without really knowing how to create a promise. So let's start with that.
	const promise = new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
		if (true) {
			resolve('Stuff Worked');
		} else {
			reject('Error, it broke')
		}
	})
	new Promise() is just a syntax we will have to get used to. This is how you 	construct a promise. We're creating a promise and this promise has 	parameters that either resolve or reject.
	Inside the function, we can just say resolve (if resolved) return this, reject (if 	rejected) return this.
	We want to have a conditional statement here to trigger the function, so we 	just use if(true) for now which is saying the promise will return a value, return 	resolve, else (if false) return reject.
	We can now put this in our console, and we have this promise object. 	Window.promise.
	How can we get this promise to run now? We can do
	promise.then(result => console.log(result))
	(promise meaning resolve or reject) and this will return
	Stuff Worked
	We ran the promise, since it was true it ran resolve, and resolve sent 	'Stuff Worked' into the 'result' and then console logged the result.
	We can also see in the console that the promise was resolved. If we just 	type promise into the console, we see that we have the promise with Stuff 	Worked. Let's see what we can do with these promises now.
	promise
		.then(result => result + '!')
		.then(result2 => {
			console.log(result2)
		})
	This is called chaining in promises. If we run this in the console now:
	Stuff Worked!
	let's say we have something happen in our chain, let's say it throws an 	error.
	promise
		.then(result => result + '!')
		.then(result2 => {
			throw Error;
			console.log(result2);
		})
	With promises you can atcually do something called .catch() and this will 	catch any error that the promise may have:
	promise
		.then(result => result + '!')
		.then(result2 => {
			throw Error;
			console.log(result2);
		})
		.catch(console.log);
	If we run this now we get:
	f Error() { [native code] }
	To make it easier to visualize:
	promise
		.then(result => result + '!')
		.then(result2 => {
			throw Error;
			console.log(result2);
		})
		.catch(() => console.log ('errorrrr!'));
	errorrrr!
	.catch catches any errors that may happen between the chains .then, 	which is very groovy baby yeah.
	promise
		.then(result => result + '!')
		.then(result2 => result2 + '?')
		.catch(() => console.log ('errorrrr!'))
		.then result3 => {
			console.log(result3 +'!');
		})
	We get 'Stuff Worked!?!'
	catch only runs if something fails in between the .then's that take place 	prior to the catch line. So if we do:
	promise
		.then(result => result + '!')
		.then(result2 => result2 + '?')
		.catch(() => console.log ('errorrrr!'))
		.then result3 => {
			throw Error;
			console.log(result3 + '!');
		})
	We dont get our 'errorrrr!' however, we do get an error in the console, well, 	because an error was thrown. We just did not 'catch' the error with our 	catch statement because the error occurred after the catch statement, 	lexically. 
	We now know how to make promises and use then a little bit.
	We see that with a promise we can give it to a variable (promise) and run 	this and do something with it asynchronously when we run .then, and get 	the result, and do whatever we want with it. We can keep chaining it, and 	catch our errors.
	A promise is something that we have now that we can use around our 	code even though we don't have the value just yet. You can just assign it 	to a variable, like promise. Well when would this be a good thing? 	Promises are great for asynchronous programming.
	When you don't want JavaScript to block the execution of your code, like 	making API calls, grabbing data from a database or maybe optimizing an 	image, you use a promise so that the task happens in the background. 	Once the promise is resolved, THAT's when you'll get the response.
	Let's see something else that makes promises really really powerful. Lets 	say within here that we had another promise after the first one:
	const promise2 = new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
		setTimeout(resolve, 100 'HIII')
	})
	setTimeout is a web API and it allows us to timeout and run something 	after a few seconds, or x amount of time.
	Let's also have another promise and another:
	const promise3 = new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
		setTimeout(resolve, 1000 'POOKIE')
	})
	const promise4 = new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
		setTimeout(resolve, 5000 'Is it me you're looking for?')
	})
	A feature that we have, is something called Promise.all(). It takes an array 	of promises and returns them as an array in the order they were input.
	Promise.all([promise, promise2, promise3, promise4])
		.then(values => {
			console.log(values);
		})
	Now if we copy all of this and run it in our console. We will wait, 1, 2, 3, 4, 	5 seconds and it will return 
	["Stuff Worked", "HIII", "POOKIE", "Is it me you're looking for?"]
	Even though we did less time for the first two promises, because we did 	Promise.all it waited until all the promiess were resolved and then logged 	out the values.
	If we copy and paste all of our promises, and then copy and paste our 	Promise.all, we will see that the result is instant and that is because once 	we ran the promises, they began running the setTimeout in the 	background so by the time we executed the Promise.all, they were ready 	to execute.
	Let's finish up with the final example that has some real world applications 	so that you have an idea of when we want to use promises.
	 const urls = [
		'https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users',
		'https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/posts',
		'https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/albums'
	]
	This is very similar to something you do one a website, where you want to 	grab some information, maybe from your server, from the database, with 	users, posts, and albums, and let's say that theyre all important to us for 	the page, for the profile page of the user. How can we grab them all? 	Again, using promises, we can just do 
	Promise.all(urls.map((url => {
		return fetch(url).then(resp => resp.json())
	})).then(results => {
		console.log(results[0])
		console.log(results[1])
		console.log(results[2])
	}
	This will grab the urls, map that array for 'url' and for each 'url' we want to 	fetch (web API) to grab urls, make an AJAX call to the URL, and then we 	want to run the response through the response.json, again that just comes 	with fetch that you need to do. From here we can do a .then, and this 	whole thing will return an array of all the fetches, so then we console.log 	the results of the fetches, 'results' being the array with all of our url/users, 	url/posts, etc. 
	If we copy and paste all of this into our console, it will return the three 	arrays of objects from the respective, users, posts, and albums URLs. 
	Let's say something fails here, maybe we misspelled one of the URLs. 	Let's add:
	.catch(() => console.log ('error'))
	Now if we run our code, we get 'error'.
	With Promise.all if everything fails because we need users, posts, and 	albums, for our entire page on our website, we get a reject from the 	promise, and we can catch it, and we can do whatver we want with this 	'error' very cool.
	This is why promises are so beneficial.
	Remember, the fetch() simply returns a Promise. 
	If we just do:
	fetch('https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users')
	We will get a Promise {<pending>}
	By adding a .then, we are answering to whatever the pending response of 	the promise was, whether it's resolve or reject, and get to manipulate the 	data.
	So at their most basic promises are a bit like event listeners, except a 	promise can only succed or fail once, it cannot succeed or fail twice. This 	is extremely useful for things that are asynchronous success and failure 	such as API calls, because we're less interested in the exact time 	something became available, and more interested in reacting to the 	outcome. So we're reacting to something that happens asynchronously.
	A promise is an object that may procude a single value some time in the 	future, either resolve, or reject, with a reason why it was rejected.
	A promise may be in one of three possible states. It can be 	fulfilled(resolved), rejected, or pending.

264. ES8 - Async Await
	Async Await is part of ES8 and is built on top of promises.
	Underneath the hood, an async function is a function that returns a 	promise. But the benefit of Async Await is that it makes code easier to 	read. Example:
	movePlayer(100, 'Left')
		.then(() => movePlayer(400, 'Left'))
		.then(() => movePlayer(10, 'Right'))
		.then(() => movePlayer(330, 'Left'))
	This is Asynchronous code. We know that movePlayer is going to happen, 	then next, then next, then next. If we want any sort of action within here, 	we can wrap it in brackets (), all things that you can do with promises.
	With Async Await, it would look something like this:
	async function playerStart() {
		const firstMove = await movePlayer(100, 'Left'); //pause
		await movePlayer(400, 'Left');
		await movePlayer(10, 'Right');
		await movePlayer(330, 'Left');
	}
	The big benefit of Async Await is that it makes code easier to read, and I 	know you're probably not thinking that right now because this is new 	syntax but just give it a chance.
	The goal with Async Await is to make code look synchronous - a code 	that's asynchronous look synchronous.
	If we remember, a promise in JavaScript is like an IOU. I promise to return 	something in the future.
	Something like an AJAX call, resizing an image, getting some information 	from a database, these are all things that take tine. Instead of waiting 	around on them, we want to continue on with our lives and just have it let 	us know when it's done. But we're going to keep working on our own stuff 	while it's doing that in the background.
	If you remember in our "How JavaScript Works" video, we talked about 	JavaScript being single threaded. For our programs to be efficient, we can 	use asynchronous programming to do these things. 
	Syntactic Sugar - something that still does the same thing but is just 	different syntax to make it look prettier.
	This is what Async Await is to promises.
	Promises have this .then that you keep having to chain, Async on the 	other hand has this async word in front of it and some await key words.
	These two do the same thgings, however, with the playerStart function, 	we are declaring a function as async, we let JavaScript know, this is in 	async function, and we declare it with the word function, and then a 	function name. 
	We can do anything we want inside of this function but we have access ti 	a bew wird because we used the async word to define this function. 
	We have the 'await' keyword and this keyword says, "Hey pause this 	function until I have something for you." So we're awaiting the response.
	You can use this 'await' keyword in front of any function that returns a 	promise, which we know that 'movePlayer' does.
	Once the promise is resolved, the function moves to the next line and it 	awaits the next move, and awaits the next move, and awaits the next 	move.
	The cool thing about this is that instead of chainging it, I can now assign, 	just like asynchronous programming, a variable 'first' to await the function, 	and we can assign second to await the second function, and first second 	will have the result of each function but in a variable.
	So it looks very synchronous. You're not using .then, you're not chaining, 	you just have simple synchronous programming. You're waiting for this to 	happen, then waiting  for this to happen.
	If we use our fetch() function, and again if we remember a fetch function is 	a promise, you see that we get a promise from fetch().
	So with the fetch function, if we use our favorite API resource, 	jsonplaceholder, let's grab the users.
	fetch('https"//jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users')
	And this would return a promise so we would have todo .then and run it 	through .json() and .then console.log it lastly.
	fetch('https"//jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users')
		.then (resp => resp.json())
		.then(console.log)
	So if we run this we initially get a promise that is pending, and then it gets 	resolved and we get our users. Now how can we turn this into an async 	function?
	Again the same feature will work the same, but it will look a little bit 	cleaner. It will be syntactic sugar.
	If we remember, we have to declare a function with async keyword and 	this function will say fetchUsers. fetchUsers will have our code above, 	inside of it. But we can now use, in front of anything that returns a 	promise, the await keyword. 
	async function fetchUsers() {
		await fetch('https"//jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users')
		.then (resp => resp.json())
		.then(console.log)
	}
	So now this function is going to pause until we get a response from fetch. 	We can do:
	const resp = await fetch('https"//jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users')
	And now instead of using .then we can do:
	const data = await resp.json()
	Because resp.json returns a promise, we can use the await keyword. And 	now finally we can do:
	 console.log(data);
	So let's copy paste this and see if it works.
	We get undefined, now we can call the fetchUsers() function which is the 	async function. And we get our promise pending as well as our promise 	resolved with all of our users.
	Nothing different from the promise, but now we have a nice step by step 	synchronous looking code, that says fetch this, get a response, run it 	through the json method, and console log 'data'.
	Let's do one more example, even more realistic. Let's say that we have a 	list of URLs and this time we have the users, posts, and albums from the 	jsonplaceholder API. 
	const urls = [
		'https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users',
		'https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/posts',
		'https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/albums'
	]

	And we want to run through them all. In our promises video, we had 	something like this: 
	Promise.all(urls.map(url =>
		fetch(url).then(resp => resp.json())
	)).then(array => {
		console.log('users', array[0])
		console.log('posts', array[1])
		console.log('albums', array[2])
	}).catch('Oops');
	We did Promise.all, we mapped through the URLs, we fetched the URLs 	and responded with the JSON and then we returned the array and the 	array had users, posts, albums and we also did a .catch.
	Let's convert this into an async function.
	const getData = async function() {
		const [ users posts, albums ] = await Promise.all(urls.map(url =>
		fetch(url).then(resp => resp.json())
	}
	We can put the await keyword in front of any promise, so we can put it in 	front of Promise.all. We can use destructuring to say users, posts, and 	albums that we will receive will equal await and then the Promise.all. Now 	we can just console.log what we had right after our promise.
	console.log('users', users)
	console.log('posts', posts)
	console.log('albums', albums)
	We can change these from array[] to the variables themselves thanks to 	the destructuring.
	We can now copy and paste all of this, and run getData() and it will return 	a pending promise and a resolved promise with users, posts, and albums. 
	We get the exact same results, which is very cool, but we did not have the 	catch inside the async await function. 
	So how do we catch errors with async await? This is the part not a lot of 	people are fans of. But it is something that once we get used to is not too 	bad. 
	JavaScript has something called 'try catch blocks' and that is when it sees 	the 'try' word it's just going to automatically run whatever's inside of it. In 	our case all of this block. We want it to run in a try block and then 'try' 	comes with a 'catch' block as well. So a 'try catch block'. And within the 	catch if anything fails within the 'try' well we can catch it in 'catch' so that 	we can just console.log('Oops').
	const getData = async function() {
	    try {
		const [ users posts, albums ] = await Promise.all(urls.map(url =>
		fetch(url).then(resp => resp.json())
	))
	console.log('users', users)
	console.log('posts', posts)
	console.log('albums', albums)
	} catch {
		console.log('Oops')
	}
	So now if we misspell one of the URLs and we copy and paste this and 	run it, we get an Uncaught SyntaxError, because one thing we have to 	remember with catch, is that catch() receives an error. You can 	console.log this error as well:
	} catch {
		console.log('Oops", err)
	}
	Now if we copy and run this, we get Oops TypeError: Failed to fetch.
	Async await, although intimidating at first, because there is a bit of new 	syntax, actually makes things quite nice and synchronous looking. 
	We simply have avariable that we assign the await promise to and then 	the function pauses there until the promise returns and then it continues 	on with console logging.
	We just have to remember the 'try catch blocks' to catch our errors and 	that's it!
	We are now an Async Await Master.
	We can use whichever we prefer, promises or async await, but at the end 	of the day, use which is simpler for you and your team and hopefully now 	when we encounter both of them we are a lot more comfortable.

266. ES9(ES2018)
	In ES9 or ES2018 there are three main new features taht you should be 	aware of. The first one is the Object SPread Operator, and it looks 	something like this. Let's say we have an object animals, and this object 	has a tiger lion and monkey with ages.
	const animals = {
		tiger: 23,
		lion: 5,
		monkey: 2
	}
	With the new Object Spread Operator, let's say we destructure here:
	const { tiger, ...rest } = animals;
	What's going to happen here is it's going to grab tiger, and it's going tog 	rab the rest, which is lion and monkey.
	This is something we were able to do with arrays in ES6. 
	const array = [1, 2, 3, 4 ,5];
	function sum(a, b ,c, d, e) {
		return a + b + c + d + e;
	}
	If we paste this in the console and do
	sum(...array); -> 15
	sum(1,2,3,4,5) -> 15
	This new feature, Object Spread Operator, just means we're able to do 	this with an object as well. 
	add bird: 40 to animals object
	function objectSpread(p1, p2, p3) {
		console.log(p1);
		console.log(p2);
		console.log(p3);
	}
	const { tiger, lion, ...rest } = animals;
	objectSpread(tiger, lion, rest);
	If we run this in the console, we get 23, 5, {monkey:2, bird: 40}. 
267. ES9 (ES2018) - Async
	We're going to discuss the other two new ES2018 features and yo show 	how they work, we're going to use the example that we had before in the 	video about promises, where we loop through all these URLs, our Star 	Wars API.
	const urls = [
		'https"//swapi.co/api/people/1',
		'https"//swapi.co/api/people/2',
		'https"//swapi.co/api/people/3',
		'https"//swapi.co/api/people/4',
	]
	Promise.all(urls.map(url => {
		return fetch(url).then(people => people.json())
	}))
		.then(array => {
			console.log('1', array[0])
			console.log('2', array[1])
			console.log('3', array[2])
			console.log('4', array[3])
		})
		.catch(err => console.log('Ughhh fix it!', err))
	If we copy and paste this into our console, we get four objects which are 	our users.
	The first feature that is new is called finally. As the name suggests, it 	allows us to do something "finally" after a promise has "finished". 
	We will usually add it at the end and this finally block will be called 	regardless if the .then works or the promise errors and catches into an 	error. No matter what, after everything is done within a promise, whether it 	resolves or rejects, and does something that we tell it to. 
	In our case let's say
	.finally(data => console.log('extra', data));
	If we add this and copy paste the whole thing again, we get our users, and 	we also get 'extra undefined'. This is because .finally doesn't really receive 	a parameter.
	.finally(() => console.log('extra'));
	What happens if we throw an error and a catch block gets called? We can 	simulate that fairly easily.
	throw Error;
	Now it will throw an error, skip over all of the console.logs, and execute 	catch(). We will not only get the error from catch, we also have 'extra' 	which was from .finally, which ran after catch executed.
	Finally is great for those times that you need to run a piece of code no 	matter what. After a promise perhaps you want to send an email to a user, 	regardless of whether their request was successful or failed. Maybe you 	want to have a little notification icon. Ther are many was to use finally and 	now there is a way to make sure something happens after a promise.
	The next one we are going to talk about, is a little bit more advanced.
	This is the 'for await of' feature.
	If we remember we had this piece of code in our async await video.
	const urls= [
		'https"//jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users',
		'https"//jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/posts',
		'https"//jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/albums',
	]
	const getData = asyn function() {
		try {
			const [ users, posts, albums ] = await 	Promise.all(urls.map(async function(url) {
			const response = await fetch(url);
			return response.json;
		}));
		console.log('users', users);
		console.log('posts', posts);
		console.log('albums', albums);
		} catch (err) {
		console.log('Ooops', err);
		}
	}
	Async await allows us to restructure our promises in a more synchronous 	way. The cool thing about this new "for await of" feature, is that it allows 	us to loop through our async await calls if we have multiple of them. Just 	like "for of".
	Let's see this in code form. We're going to create a new function, and this 	is going to be called getData2. But first actually, we're going to revisit how 	a for of loop works.
	const loopThroughUrls= url => {
		for (url of urls) {
			console.log(url)
		}
	}
	This new function will do exactly what this one does above by using the 	for await of feature.
	const getData2 = async function() {
		const arrayOfPromises = urls.map(url => fetch(url));
	)
	In this first line all we are doing is creating an array of these fetch 	promises of each one of these requests. Next we can use the for await of 	to loop through these promises.
	for await (let request of arrayOfPromises) {
		const data = await request.json();
	}
	We use the await keyword in front of a promise. We use it after 'for' 	because we are looping over an array of 'promises'. We use it in front of 	request.json() because .json is a promise.
	console.log(data);
	getData2 when copy and pasted with the urls, now does the same thing 	as getData and returns all three objects, users, posts, and albums, in less 	than half the code.
	To review, we have .finally() which can run at the end of a promise, and 	we have for await of which takes each item from an array of promises that 	returns to use in the correct order all of the responses.
268. ES2020: allSettled()
	const promiseOne = new Promise((resolve, reject) =>
		setTimeout(resolve, 300))
	const promiseTwo = new Promise((resolve, reject) =>
		setTimeout(reject, 3000))
	Promise.all([promiseOne, promiseTwo]).then(data => console.log(data));
	If we run this Promise.all statement, what will happen?
	When we run it, we get a pending promise for 3 seconds, and then we get 	an Uncaught (in promise) undefined error.
	Promise.all only resolves, if both promises resolve in the parameters. So 	in order for this to work, we would need a catch statement.
	.catch(e => console.log('Something failed', e));
	Now if we try it, we wait, 1, 2, 3 and get 'something failed undefined'.
	Because Promise.all has to have all promises resolved and not throw any 	errors. But with the new 2020 feature, we have allSettled().
	const promiseOne = new Promise((resolve, reject) =>
		setTimeout(resolve, 300))
	const promiseTwo = new Promise((resolve, reject) =>
		setTimeout(reject, 3000))
	Promise.allSettled([promiseOne, promiseTwo]).then(data => 	console.log(data))
	.catch(e => console.log('Something failed', e));
	Now if we run this, we wait 3 seconds, and we get promises! Returning 	one is 'fulfilled' and one is 'rejected'.
	Promise.allSettled() doesn't care about 'resolve' or 'reject'. It runs all 	promises, regardless of whether they reject or not. The promise only 	comes back when all promises that have been added to it are complete.
269. ES2021: any()
	There is a new method added to promises in 2021! Unfortunately it isn't very useful. But here is an example using our previous sections code.
	Promise.any() resolves if any of the supplied promises is resolved.
	const p1 = new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
		setTimeout(() => resolve("A"), Math.floor(Math.random() * 1000));
	});
	const p2 = new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
		setTimeout(() => resolve("B"), Math.floor(Math.random() * 1000));
	});
	const p3 = new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
		setTimeout(() => resolve("C"), Math.floor(Math.random() * 1000));
	});
	Out of p1, p2 and p3, whichever resolves first is taken by Promise.any().
	(async function () {
		const result = await Promise.any([p1, p2, p3]);
		console.log(result); // Prints "A", "B" or "C"
	})();
	What if none of the promises resolve? In that case Promise.any() throws 	an error!

Section 21: Backend Introduction
271. Backend Basics
	Before we cover Backend Basics, we are going to go over what we 	covered so far on the Front-End.
	
 
	We started with this diagram of asking "Who is google.com?" and going 	through the ISP asking the domain name server for the IP address. We 	then ask the IP address to find the Google servers and then the Google 	servers gives us our HTML, CSS, and JavaScript. Then the link tags and 	the script tags get the CSS and JavaScript. The browser reads the file for 	us, the HTML, CSS, and the V8 Engine or the JavaScript engine of the 	browser uses it to read the JavaScript and we manipulate the DOM using 	JavaScript.
 

	We also learned that the location of the server is important, how many 	trips we make, how many HTTP requests we make is important. The size 	of the files is important. And we learned some of those solutions such as 	content delivery networks, or CDNs, have script into just a bundle.js file, 	and also minimizing our files. 
 

	Then we also learned about this new technology called AJAX where we 	can just fetch users, talk to the server while the user is still browsing the 	website, and receive a JSON respone to update the website in real time.
 

	This picture that we started with looks a lot clearer than in the first video.
	We understand what a Front-End developer does. We see the power of 	React, and we also kno that these arrows simply mean HTTP requests; a 	language that both Front-End and Back-end can speak, and using AJAX 	and JSON we're able to do it dynamically.
	Now the Back-End and the role of a Back-End developer, they are the 	ones that in charge of working with servers and databases. 
	A server is responsible for whatever you get in the browser.
	We can ask the browser for anything; such as 'Go to google.com'. But at 	the end of the day the web server can just give me whatever it wants. If 	it's going to just send me to twitter.com instead of google.com, well, that's 	up to the server. Whatever we need, we just make the request and we 	hope that the server gives us what we asked for. 
	If you remember, a server is just a computer. A computer that's 	somewhere in the world just listening to this HTTP connection, asking to 	see if we have any files.
 


	The most basic of servers, and you might have used this before maybe 	using Hostgator or a hosting site, or even WordPress sites and putting 	them on Hostgator, it uses something called a 'LAMP STACK'.
	This was what was useful for the most basic of websites, that is, if you just 	wanted a simple information website. Maybe a simple WordPress website 	where nothing complex is happening.
	What they used was a Linux server, which is the operating system, just 	like Mac or Windows, Linux will be the operating system of the server. 
	We have the Apache server. Apache is a server, again, a program written 	by somebody, that is really really good at just accepting requests and 	sending out responses of files.
	We have MySQL which is a database. Let's say you had a comment 	section in your blog and you need to store that data, that would be the 	database.
	Then we have PHP, which was just a lanugage that you can use to have a 	bit of logic in your servers.
	The server, just like your computer, has a filesystem where all the files of 	your website are stores. And Apache did just that. Just listen to see if 	anybody entered funfunwebsite.com into the URL. If it got that request it'll 	just default give you index.html. If somebody entered 	funfunwebsite.com/about, it will just give you about.html. It was a very 	simple very easy server.
	That's what most hosting websites like Hostgator use.
	You might be asking, "Could I just download Apache server and just run 	my own server from my computer?" Yes, you absolutely can. But without 	having a server software, our comouter is unreachable. 
	Nobody over the internet can acccess our computer because our 	computer is not a server, it has no code that tells it "Hey, if I get a request, 	send this out."
	As we saw in previous videos, we can create fake servers to test our 	websites. 
	This was the old way of doing things. There wasn't much logic to it, you 	couldn't really expand on it.
	You can't have websites like Facebook or Amazon using this type of 	system. 
 
	Now a new stack exists, something we call an Application Server, looks 	something more like this.
	An application server is different than our previous example because 	unlike using Apache server that is very simple and just does request 	response, just give files there and back. With something like a Node 	server where you have Node.js running, well you can write a script and 	say "If I receive a request for a log in then check the user's username in 	the database. If it matches then let me know and then send a response 	through JSON to the Front-End application. And we can customize what 	these scripts say.
	So far we've only been able to use JavaScript on the web, but now with 	Node.js, we can use it on a server, on a computer that isn't running a web 	browser.
	With Node.js, we can write JavaScript where, if I posed something on 	Facebood, that post on Facebook, when I clicked 'post', it will get sent to 	the Node Server that's listening for requests. It will say that "Andrei just 	posted this message that is in JSON format.' The Node.js and Express.js 	file on the server will read it and it'll say "Ah, we want to store this post in 	our database." and this database is going to store that post, so that when 	we come back next time, it's still there.
	Once that Is done, it's going to respond with a success saying that "My 	post is a success" and now displays in the timeline. We can edit the post, 	delete the post, and again Node Server can delete that post from the 	database and then send a response and remove it from the timeline. You 	just have the power to be constantly interacting with the Front-End.
	In the coming vidoes we will create a Node Server using Express, we're 	going to create our own database where we store user information. And 	we're going to be using AJAX and JSON constantly to create, sign in, and 	update user information.
	If you're building websites and web apps, well, you're not really building 	the PHP and MySQL stuff. As a web developer, you're building something 	more similar to the Node.js/Express.js and PostgreSQL/MongoDB.
	Is this it? Is this the ultimate end to what a Back-End server infrastructure 	can do?"
 

	If you're Facebook, or Twitter, or Amazon, you would do something like 	this. To explain:
	You are just making copies of your computer. Think of each of these 	rectangles as a computer, and because Facebook gets a lot of people, it 	makes a copy of their server and put it maybe in Asia, and one in Africa, 	so that everybody from Asia can access this one, and everybody from 	Africa can access this one, instead of having everybody access one 	server and overload the system. Because they have the same server it's 	just copying these files and putting it on a server somewhere else. That's 	it.
	That's what we call 'scaling'. But they also still use the same database, so 	that if from this server somebody updates the information, well, this 	person over here who uses this server, this user will still have access to 	the information using this server updated.
	And then finally we have something called a 'Load Balancer'. And this is 	similar to what Apache or NginX would do. It listens for an HTTP request 	and it notices that "Hmm, this one over here in North America, it's really 	busy right now."I'm going to transfer this person to this server over here." 	and it just balances the load of the servers. It can keep growing, having 	more and more servers as you have more and more users.
 

	You can also even have multiple databases. Again, these squares each 	represent a computer; a computer that's running a database, a computer 	that's running a server, a computer that's running a load balancer. And 	you can have multiple databases, again, so that they don't get 	overwhelmed with all these requests. Each one maintains a copy of the 	other. This is something that we'll talk about more when we get to the 	Node section and Database section. We just wanted to learn the idea 	behind servers. To work with the Back-End, the main goal is to make sure 	that when we make an HTTP request from the Front-End, that we get a 	response back as fast as possible with correct data.
	That is what  Back-End Development is. Making sure that we return those 	resources so that our Front-End team can be confident that we are going 	to receive exactly what we need.

273. Introduction To APIs
	API or Application Programming Interface is a messenger that takes the 	request and tells the system what to do.
	If we take for example, Skyscanner, it can search the web for all the sites 	of airlines for the best deals and display them. How is it able to do that? It 	found all these different flights from different Websites, but Skyscanner 	doesn't really have the information. This information is somewhere in the 	database of each individual airline. But as an airlinr, you are interested in 	having this data on Skyscanner, because people like us, who want to 	book a flight, might not know to go to americanairlines.com. So by having 	their information listed on a website like this, they can increase their 	revenue. 
	So how is Skyscanner able to do this without having this information 	themselves? Well, that's where API's come in.
	API's are what allows strangers to speak to each other. so that we aren't 	in a closed system.
	We can share data and work with each other and APIs' what allows 	machines, computers, to communicate.
	This might sound familiar, it's kind of like when we had web browsers 	speaking with servers, right? Well yeah because API is a broad topic. It 	applies to the way machines communicate. If we open up the console, we 	always mention the window object, so if we do window.fetch, fetch is an 	API. It is given by the browser to use, so that we can communicate with 	this machine, so that the console can communicate with the browser.
	In the same way, although Skyscanner doesn't have access to the 	databases of the airlines, all of the airlines us APIs or give access to this 	information through APIs so that websites like Skyscanner can have the 	information as well and list it on their sites.
	The best way to think about it is kind of like a waiter. You're sitting at a 	table about to order and the waiter is the messenger that tells the kitchen 	what to do. The kitchen is providing the waiter the restaurant right? The 	restaurant is saying "Hey, waiter, these are the menu items. Please go 	ask the customer what he or she wants and let us know so that we can 	prepare." 
 
	This might sound similar to ^ this right? We're asking another machine for 	something using -let's say a waiter- and this machine gives us a response 	if we talk to it in a certain way that it expects.
	A server has APIs, ways they can communicate with it. such as doing 	"users/about".
	We also have our code previously from our Robofriends app. React which 	is a library built by Facebook, exposes an API, a way to communicate with 	the React package, by doing React.Component and using JSX.
	They tell us how to communicate with it and we are able to communicate 	with it.
 
	Is this an API? It's the back of a TV.
	Yeah! It's an API.
	A TV works in its own way but it gives you access to a couple of things 	such as the HDMI cable, so you can plug into it an communicate with it 	such as displaying your laptop into the TV.
	We've been using APIs in the Robofriends demonstration as well.
	The JSON placeholder, the users that we got, if we go to their website, we 	see that it's a fake Online REST API for Testing and Prototyping. It's an 	API. So it must be giving us information about something that it has. If we 	go down, we see that it has resources and the API tells us we can access 	them using /xxxx. So if we do 	https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/comments, we get the comments in 	JSON.
	Remember how we were able to get images of robots from robohash.org? 	That's an API. We can say robohash whatever, and because the've said 	that the way to access this API is to just type in random numbers after 	robohash.org, we're able to access it.
	People build these APIs usually for business reasons, such as airline 	companies, right? They have incentive to comply to industry standards, 	such as having JSON respones. That makes it easier for developers to 	grab bits and pieces from different places.
	For example we have the hithub api https://api.github.com/users/facebook
	We're accessing the github repo of Facebook, and it tells us how many 	followers it has, what bio it has. If we go to Apple, we can get the Apple 	username as well. And you can see that it is in JSON format because that 	is the standard.
	If we want to build something with gitHub, we can just send a request to 	this, get this data, and create our website based on this data.
	It's not only JSON, there is XML as well. 
 
	This is a sample API that shows us a breakfast menu and here we can 	request this data through HTTP and we get breakfast menu.
	APIs are not just websites. TV I/O those are APIs. An NPM 		package can be an API. It's just a way that people communicate or 	machines communicate and share information. 
	Without it we really wouldn't have wjat we have now with the 	WorldWide 	Web.
	How is this useful to us?
	First off we can build something like Skyscanner quite easily. We can 	actually search API jetBlue, API American Airlines, and they'll have 	information on how we can access this data so we can build our own 	thing.
	All the site is doing is making an HTTP request to something like 	jsonplaceholder.typicode.com, and then grabbing the data and just adding 	some CSS to it to make it look nice.
	There are a ton of APIs online. You can search them, you can read up on 	them. Big companies like Facebook have APIs, Google has APIs, you 	might now Google Maps anytime you see Google Maps on a Website, it's 	using the Google API. We also have silly API like the Star Wars API.
	You can request and change the request. For example, people/2/ will 	return a JSON C-3PO object.
	This is a free to use API, so you can buld a sample app using the Star 	Wars API and all you need to do is just fetch the resource, get the JSON 	converted to a JavaScript object, and then style it and use it in your app 	whichever way you want.
	There's also some really important APIs that if you're building serious 	websites are really useful. That is the "stripe" API. 
	If you want to incorporate payments into your website, this shows you 	exactly what to do. You can add to your server within whichever language 	you want, in our case Node, and it tells you how you can add "stripe" as a 	payment method on your server. It sounds scary but it's all documented in 	here, step by step.
	Another powerful tool is the "Twilio API". Twilio allows sending of 	messages on phones.
 
	Using something simple like this, we can see there's not much to it other 	than using the client.messages.create that they provide. 
	We just say what we want the body of the text to be, to which number, 	and from which number, if we want to include mdeia , maybe a heart, we 	can add that too.
	Let's say a user signs up for a service, well we can send them a message 	saying "Hey we've created a new user, they've provided a phone number, 	send them a message."
	Not all APIs are free however, and when you start bulding real 	applications and companies like stripe and Twilio where there's some real 	engineering effort begind creating these, well they charge, They charge a 	service and that's when you use something like an API key. An API key 	looks like Jibberish. It is a key, a private key, so that every time you 	access their API they know that it's you. And because let's say you have a 	plan for making a thousand requests, they charge you because they can 	recognize you saying that "Oh, this is Andreis key, he's the one making 	the request from a server and we're going to charge him for it."
	In our final project we'll be using a really really cool machine learning API 	and we're going to generate our API key and actually use it. It is free.
	A server somewhere in the world for a company provides an API. In order 	for other people to use it, they usually tell you how to use it, with 	documentation, Most of them comply with a standard like JSON to make it 	easier for developers to use. At the end of the day, an API is just a tool 	used for data communication. It's how a program or software or anything 	running code allows others to communicate with it. It opens up a big 	possibility of making websites that do more thatn just list robots. You can 	now with the same principles, build web sites that incorporate different 	APIs to have the functionality of something like Skyscanner.

Section 23: Leveling Up: The Power of Problem Solving
277. Snapshot Model of Learning
	What I teach you is how to think like a programmer. I don't want to teach 	you to just copy what I do. I want you to solve your own problems. And 	this is intentional and it's something that I actually teach in one of my 	courses and it's called Deliberate Practice.
	Any time you encounter a bit of struggle as you build this final project, 	remember, that's you stretching your imagination, stretching your problem, 	solving skills and improving. So this is why this is an exciting part of the 	course, because we're not just going to build an app, our final project, 	we're also going to develop the most important skill of a web developer, 	which is the ability to solve problems when the answers aren't clearly 	there.
278. LTL: Deliberate Practice
	Deliberate practice teaches us that the learning process should be tough,  	and temporary frustrations are good.
	Anders Ericsson wrote a book called Peak, which is one of the most 	important learning books in Andrei's opinion. This idea of deliberate 	practice is mentioned quite often. He mentions that Rock Climbers, Chess 	Players, and all of these masters can usually visualize all their moves 	ahead of time. Theyre able to create experiences in their mind, make 	accurate predictions, and keep a lot of information in their mind. The way 	they're able to do such things isn't just natural talent, it's deliberate 	practice.
	So what is deliberate practice. Deliberate practice usually happens 	something like this. You start to learn a topic and have rapid progress. But 	then you hit your plateau and then we get frustrated. This is where 	deliberate practice comes in. We want to be just in this area, this area 	where we're finding a bit of frustration, and for us to improve, we need to 	have deliberate practice. We need, according to Anders Ericsson, we 	need specific goals.
	We need intense focus, immediate feedback, and that frequent discomfort 	of being at the edge of our abilities.
	Regular practice might include things like mindless repetition, deliberate 	practice actually requires us to be focused, getting that immediate 	feedback and having that specific goal to know where we want to be.
	Deliberate practice takes place outside one's comfort zone, and requires a 	person to constantly try things that are just beyond their current abilities. 	Thus it demands near maximal effort, which is generally not enjoyable.
	Deliberate practice is not meant to be fun. It's that practice that is a 	struggle. That area where you feel like you're not good enough. It's 	pushing you to your limits.
	How do we access this state of deliberate practice?
	Periods of undistracted focus.
	Push ourselves to the edge of our ability.
	Cycle between comfort and discomfort where you're trying something and 	learning something and pushing your limits, where you find a task too 	difficult, too hard for you to do.
	Next seek imeediate feedback and mentorship. Let's say you're learning 	how to code. Well, try to build your own project and find that it's super 	difficult. Then you start reaching out to your peers, to your mentor, to your 	teacher, and seeing if they can help you out with your problem.
