282. DEVELOPER FUNDAMENTALS: Maintain Your Codebase
	We use third party packages like React that depend on other people 	keeping it up to date. When we make updates sometimes things break 	therefore, and there's a couple of interesting tidbits to see that we may 	encounter, and how to solve them. As we build the app, we  might 	encounter errors, so we will use this as an example of how to debug code,  	solve things, and keep our app uo to date. Ho w to read error messages 	so that  you know what to do and how to solve these problems as we 	encounter them.
	Your app will always need maintenance, it will always need to be 	updated, and along the way you will encounter bugs and outdated 	packages.
	If you hover over the SemVer of your dependency in VSCode it will tell 	you what the latest version is. Then you can simply change the numbers, 	e.g. 17.0.2 -> 18.2.0, and then npm install. However if we do this, we get 	an error.
	What happened is, if you have a version of React over 18, this 	package that we're going to use "react-particles-js" and "react-tilt" too, will 	no longer work. This is a very common occurrence. Sometimes life gets in 	the way and these devleopers stop maintaining their code. 
	Maintaining and keeping up with all these package updates is part of your 	job as a developer.
	So what can we do?
	Often times we can find replacement packages that do the same thing 	and are kept up to date. We can find those and install them. React 	Parralax Tilt, and React Particles BG for example. 
	We can now go back to package.json remove the original errored 	packages, and install the new packages. Now we should be able to see 	them in package.json. 
	Now if we run npm start, we get a module error react particle js and react 	tilt, because it's in our code.
	Now then, we'll go to App.js and we'll remove the import of react-particles, 	and add the import to particles-bg. Then we'll find the implementations of 	react-particles, remove them or comment them out, and add the code for 	particlesbg that we found on the NPM registry into the component we are 	using react-particles in.
	Now we would do the same for react-tilt and tilt-parralax. Here's where we 	encounter a very unique error. A once in a career error.
	When we updated react-scripts to 5.0, in version five of create-react-app, 	they use Webpack five to bundle our JavaScript code. Well in WebPack 	five, it no lonfer allows you to use the keyword process.
	If we use our error message and look at our code, we can see the use of 	the word process in the node modules clarifai App.js. This is because we 	are going to downlaod this Clarifai API package for our app.
	Clarifai is meant to be used on the backend. However when we first build 	out the app, we will learn how to use it on the front end. We will move it to 	the backend because it is actually a security issue.
	For the first part however, since we haven't learned about Node and 	Backend and databases, we're going to use Clarifai API on the front end.
	Well, on the front-end there is no key word process. On the backend, 	process is actually a keyword in Node, something that we'll use. But 	Webpack is essentially saying "Hey, I have no idea what process is". So 	this is a bit of a hack around that. So all I'm going to do based on my 	knowledge, because I know how React works, right? I know that at the 	end of the day, all it's doing is dumping the components that we build into 	this index.html file, into this 'root'.
	So all I'm going to do, because we're developers, because we're 	engineers, and because we know our stuff, well, I'm going to add a script 	here, between the </head> and <body>. Why? I'm just going to say
	<script>var process='test'</script>
	This makes sure that process is in the global namespace and when 	Webpack encounters it, it's just going to assume that 'process' is a string 	called 'test'. 
	Obviously this isn't recommended to keep there, but just in case you 	encountered this error, because we said that we're going to move Calrifai 	to the backend, we don't need to worry about it.
	So if we save this and go back to our page, our app is working, and the 	new Particles BG component is working. 
	BG is really cool, you can give it different options and have really cool 	effects, for example tadpole, which makes the bubbles tadpoles instead. 	We'd navigate to <ParticlesBG> in our App.js, and we would change it's 	attribute type to 'tadpole' instead of 'circle'.
	The idea is to see how Andrei is actually able to think like a developer and 	solve the problems. This is what 'snapshot' model of learning teaches us, 	as we update things, as time progresses, how do we maintain our apps.
	One last thing, we still have a Warning:ReactDOM.render is no longer 	supported in React 18. Use createRoot instead. And we know tha right? 	We've done that in our RoboFriends app. But just do use the code from 	the documentation website, in the upcoming video if you're using React 18 	or higher, most likely, what you're going to have to do is copy the 
	"const root = ReactDOM.createRoot(document.getElementById('root'));" 
	and paste that into the index.js above the ReactDOM.render(), and 	comment out the ReactDOM.render. Then add 
	root.render(<App />).
	If we check now, we still have a Warning that says now, You are importing 	createRoot from "react-dom" which is not supported. You should instead 	import it from "react-dom/client". So to do so, we navigate to idnex.js and 	simply add /client to the end of our ReactDOM import statement. OR, we 	can be fancy, and use destructuring to import the createRoot directly and 	circumnavigate using ReactDOM altogether.
	import { createRoot } from "react-dom/client";
	No errors! Fully updated!
	Yes, this video was a lot, we changed a lot of our app and we haven't 	coded any of it. But we were shown and are able to use this video now as 	an example as we encounter these issues throughout the next couple of 	videos, throughout the backend portion and the database portion, the 	production portion of the course, and see how to think through errors in a 	professional way. 
	Understand where errors are coming from and solve the problems.
	As a developer you will always encounter problems like this when you 	update packages or libraries or whatever. It will always be not so smooth 	sailing, but it's part of the job. When things, update, things break, we need 	to learn how to solve these problems on our own. 
	Hopefully by seeing how Andrei updates his app, something he does 	every year, we can also update our apps every year and keep them 	maintained. 
	It's part of the job.
	Let's build our own project now. All this code that you just saw, you get to 	now, write it and build your own, and customize it with different things like 	the particle BG that was shown.
283. Quick Note: React Tilt + React Particles
	For React 18 and higher, use react-parallax-tilt and particles-bg instead of 	react-tilt and react-particles-js.
	This is am o,portant learning point that there are always going to be 	alternatives to libraries that you use on aproject, and your job as a 	developer is to find the "right one". There are usually multiple libraries that 	do that same thing and work in almost 99% the same way. Good luck :)
284. Building Our Components
	One of the first things we should do, is imagine what we are going to 	build, and try to separate out the components of our application.
	
 
	This is a quick wireframe of what we want our project to look like. 
	We want some sort of logo, we want to have a User's name, displated 	with their rank, and this rank is going to correspond to how many face 	detections they've done, compared to all the other users.
	We're going to want some sort of sign in and sign out feature. So this is 	the home page, but if they click 'Sign Out' they will be signed out and in 	order to sign in there will be a sign in form.
	We have a URL input where we can enter a URL for an image. 
	If we click detect, then it will displace the image with the face detection, 	hopefully on the face.
	We obviously want it to be responsive, but looking at this, if we were going 	to divide this into components, we can see there is a navigation 	component, there is maybe a user info, maybe rank component, there's a 	logo component of some sort. There's an input form component. There is 	an image component. And possibly within that another component that 	does image detection.
	Okay, so the first step then that we can do is start building out these 	components using create-react-app, and the sign-in and registration form 	is maybe something that we can work on afterwards.
	Run create-react-app and name it. Andrei uses, facerecognitionbrain. 
	Now to start off, we can remove some of the things that we don't need. 	We don't need their logo, or anything inside the return div in our App.js 	class App extends Component. HOWEVER, React has changed the way 	their create-react-app initially creates the app. The "class App extends 	Component" syntax has been swapped with a new default "function 	App()". This is fine for static applications, however if we want to involve 	the use of state in our app, we will need to revert back to the dynamic 	syntax "class App extends Component".
	Now that we have a clear slate, let's start tossing in some of the 	components that we will need to render when our app is fully built. "Lay 	the groundwork" if you will.
	<Navigation />		Sign Out
	<Logo />			Logo
	<ImageLinkForm />		Input form
	<FaceRecognition />	Image with Face Recognition applied
	We're actually going to wrap from logo down to facerecognition in {} curly 	brackets. This will evalutate them as JavaScript, so that we can comment 	them out like normal JS /**/.
	Since we know this project is going to get a little big, we can create a 	folder names components where we can store what we build. Within this 	components folder, we can create a Navigation folder, and this will contain 	everything related to our Navigation component. CSS, JavaScript. In our 	case, just JS for now, so we can create Navigation.js. This will be a simple 	component with no state so we can just do a pure function.
	import React from 'react';
	const Navigation = () => {
		return (
			<nav>
				<p>Sign Out</p>
			</nav>
		);
	}
	export default Navigation;
	Then we can go back to App.js and import it.
	import Navigation from './components/Navigation/Navigation';
	./ means current directory 
	Now we have Sign Out on our main page. We actually want this to display 	on the right, so we're going to add a 'style' attribute to our <nav>. We are 	passing it on an object, so we'll say style={{display: 'flex', justifyContent: 	'flex-end'}}> and we can do this because we are passing it on an object, 	which is why we do camelCase for justifyContent. This is why the more dependencies you have in your 	projects, the more likely it is for your project to break or need updating. 	For now, I have added the API docs and information that you will need the 	rest of this project so you can always use them as a reference.

290. Reading Documentation Like A Pro
	Clarifai has actually updated their docs, and as such, have updated the 	method for calling their API. 
	If we go to the docs and go to Making Predictions -> Images then Predict 	via URL we can find the current JavaScript REST API.
	At the top of our App.js underneath we are going to paste in what we copy 	from the documentation.
	You can now use a PAT (Personal Access Token) which is more secure 	than the API key. You can find this if we go to our dashboard, to security 	and then Create Personal Access Token, select All Scopes, name it and 	click confirm. Copy it and paste it into the new code.
	You also need your User-ID which you can find from your dashboard-> 	account.
	The app ID is also needed and this is simply the name you have given 	your app in your dashboard.
	The Model ID is the same as the one we provided earlier. face-detection
	We can actually delete Model Version ID
	The Image URL is an image we will provide.
	To make things a little cleaner, we can actually create a function with an 	imageURL parameter, paste these declarations into it, and then pass the 	imageURL we wish to provide as an argument to the function.
	Then we can copy the raw JSON declaration which constains the 	USER_ID, APP_ID, and the IMAGE_URL, paste it above in the function, 	and return the raw variable. We can then rename the function 	returnClarifaiJSONRequest to calrify :P
	We also have requestOptions that we need to include. Instead of 	returning the raw variable, we const it, and const requestOptions, which 	has the POST method, and headers Accept and Authorization, which has 	Key and PAT in it. Then we can simply return requestOptions for 	verbosities sake.
	We can then also rename the function returnClarifaiRequestOptions.
	This will then return us the method, headers, and body, which will have 	the IMAGE_URL.
	After removing all of this, we can see that the only thing left from the 	documentation is the .fetch request. With MODEL_VERSION_ID being 	optional, we can remove the MODEL_VERSION_ID, and /versions/ and 	this is the fetch we want to do.
	If we take this fetch request and bring it down to our onButtonSubmit 	function, we can paste it after app.models.predict, and input the data. 	MODEL_ID would be 'face-detection' and IMAGE_URL would be the 	returnClarifaiRequestOptions with the argument passed into the fetch 	function being passed into the requestoptions function, like 	returnClarifaiRequestOptions(this.state.input).
	Now we can comment out app.models, remove the .then console logging 	the results, and remove the .catch because we are catching the whole 	response at the bottom of the method.
	Our first method was to use the JavaScript NPM Package (app.models)
	Our Second is No NPM Package (fetch) with no dependencies.
	This method removes the need to import the Clarifai package, and use 	the apiKey altogether.
	When we download the Clarifai NPM Package, underneath the hood, all it 	was doing was just that. Importing clarifai so we can make a nice call 	without having to do all of this, but we did it ourselves.
291. Exercise: Coding Your Image Recognition API
	Well, the first thing we want to do, we want to make sure that there is 	some functionality here where we can detect, what the user enters. 
	So the first thing you might realize is that we need 'state', we need to 	create a 'state' so that, our app knows, what the value is that the user 	enters, remembers it, and updates at any time it gets changed.
	In order to create state, we will define a 'constructor' 
	constructor() {	
		super();
	}
	We have to call super() to use this. 
	Now we will initialize the state object.
	constructor() {	
		super();
		this.state = {
			input: '';
		}
	}
	Notice "this.state" is accessing the state object associated with the class 	component. In this example it is referencing the component's current 	state.
	This sets the state object with an input that is a string. This will be what 	the user inputs.
	This input will have an OnInputChange that we will use with arrow 		functions so we don't get an error.
	onInputChange = (event) => {
	
	}
	As you know anytime there's some sort of an event listener on a web 	page, we receive an event. Which will be the argument passed into the 	onInputChange function. So what do we do with that? Log it!
	console.log(event);
	 onInputChange can now be passed as a prop in ImageLinkForm.
	<ImageLinkForm onInputChange={this.onIputChange}/>
	In React, passing a function as a prop means you're providing a function 	to a child component, allowing the child to invoke that function. This 	allows us to use the onInputChange function from our whole app, 	specifically in the case of the input change in the ImageLinkForm 	component.
	Now if we go to the InputLinkForm,js to add the input change as a prop, 	we can destructure it:
	const ImageLinkForm = ({ onInputChange }) => {
	instead of this.onInputChange
	Now we can edit the input inside the ImageLinkForm function to add the 	onChange attribute. onChange is technically a react synthetic event that 	mimics what the html does with whenever input changes.
	The onChange attribute takes a function as its value.
	This function is executed every time the input value changes.
	If we test our app at this point and open the console we will see that we 	are getting a proxy object. 
	If we go to our console log command and change event to 	event.target.value, and retest it, we will receive the inputs from the text 	input box.
	Now we want to make it so that when we click on detect we can detect an 	image.
	onButtonSubmit = () => {
		console.log('click');
	}
	now 'onButtonSubmit', we can pass to our 'imageLinkForm' again.
	<ImageLinkForm onInputChange={this.onIputChange} 	onButtonSubmit={this.onButtonSubmit}/>
	Now the ImageLinkForm will receive onButtonSubmit.
	const ImageLinkForm = ({ onInputChange, onButtonSubmit }) => {
	And we can add an onClick attribute to our button element and pass it our 	onButtonSubmit.
	How do we display an image down here and have it detect the face? Well, 	luckily for us, we can use an API, and this API I think is one of the coolest 	out there. It's called 'clarifai'.
	You just log in. And once you create your account here, you'll be taken 	onto your profile.
	They might already have my first application for you. If there's nothing 	here you just click on 'CREATE NEW APPLICATION'.
	If you go to API Keys, this key is your way of accessing the 'clarifai' API.
	So in our app if we enter a url, and press detect, we can send that url with 	the API key that they provide to their service and using the API key they'll 	say, oh! that's Andre, he's under the free plan. He hasn't hit his 5000 limit 	yet for the month. Yes. Let's respond.
	So we're going to be using their face detection API. And their face 	detection API, if we go to the js(javascript), we simply run this function, to 	the 'clarifai' servers using our own API and it gives us a response or an 	error.

from clarifai.client.model import Model
# Your PAT (Personal Access Token) can be found in the Account's Security section
# Specify the correct user_id/app_id pairings
# Since you're making inferences outside your app's scope
#USER_ID = "clarifai"
#APP_ID = "main"

# You can set the model using model URL or model ID.
# Change these to whatever model you want to use
# eg : MODEL_ID = 'general-image-detection'
# You can also set a particular model version by specifying the  version ID
# eg: MODEL_VERSION_ID = '1580bb1932594c93b7e2e04456af7c6f'
#  Model class objects can be inititalised by providing its URL or also by defining respective user_id, app_id and model_id
# eg : model = Model(user_id="clarifai", app_id="main", model_id=MODEL_ID)
DETECTION_IMAGE_URL = "https://s3.amazonaws.com/samples.clarifai.com/people_walking2.jpeg"
model_url = "https://clarifai.com/clarifai/main/models/face-detection"
detector_model = Model(
    url=model_url,
    pat="de4436edc9074f428d9fbc565b27a3c9",
)
# The predict API gives flexibility to generate predictions for data provided through URL,Filepath and bytes format.
# Example for prediction through Bytes:
# model_prediction = model.predict_by_bytes(input_bytes, input_type="image")
# Example for prediction through Filepath:
# model_prediction = Model(model_url).predict_by_filepath(filepath, input_type="image")
prediction_response = detector_model.predict_by_url(
    DETECTION_IMAGE_URL, input_type="image"
)
# Since we have one input, one output will exist here
regions = prediction_response.outputs[0].data.regions
for region in regions:
    # Accessing and rounding the bounding box values
    top_row = round(region.region_info.bounding_box.top_row, 3)
    left_col = round(region.region_info.bounding_box.left_col, 3)
    bottom_row = round(region.region_info.bounding_box.bottom_row, 3)
    right_col = round(region.region_info.bounding_box.right_col, 3)
    for concept in region.data.concepts:
        # Accessing and rounding the concept value
        name = concept.name
        value = round(concept.value, 4)
        print(
            (f"{name}: {value} BBox: {top_row}, {left_col}, {bottom_row}, {right_col}")
        )
292. Face Detection Box
	Now that we have our response working and we have access to the 	bounding box coordinates, we can focus on creating a visual for the box to 	encompass the face the application detects.
	To start, we can add a box property to our state.
	constructor() {
		super();
		this.state = {
			input: '',
			imageURL: '',
			box: {},
		}
	}
	Then we can create a function to calculate the location of the face for the 	box.
	calculateFaceLocation =(data) => {
	}
	Once we have a function that we can call for this, we can add it to the 	response we get back from the API when we input and send an image. 
		.then(response => this.calculateFaceLocation(response))
		.catch(err => console.log(err));
	Now we can flesh out the calculate function. We will be receiving the 	response and passing it as an argument (data) to the function. We are 	looking for the same response that we were console logging, so we will 	need to dive into the response object.
	const clarifaiFace = 	data.outputs[0].data.regions[0].region_info.bounding_box
	Since we are just focusing on detecting one face right now, we will use the 	[0] to specify the first face detected.
	Within this function, we now need to do some DOM manipulation.
	const image = document.getElementById('inputimage');
	We can create an id on our image in our FaceRecognition component 	with the calue inputimage.
	We should know that the 'bounding_box' gives us a percentage of the 	image. If it's .22, that means it's at 20% of whatever the height or width of 	the image is.
	In our case, we can say for sure that we want to have the width of our 	image. We cn do so by:
	const width = image.width;
	However, since this is a string and we need a number to do calculations 	with, we will need to do
	Number(image.width);

Section 25: Node.js + Express.js
296. Introduction To Node.js
	Node is Javascript that runs on your machine. 
	Up until Node was created, you could only run JavaScript in the browser 	because the browsers were the only ones that had a JavaScript engine 	like V8.
	Node was created with the V8 engine, so we could run JavaScript 	anywhere, including your computer, robots, anywhere, as long as you 	have Node.js.
	As a matter of fact, we've been using Node this entire time in our 	terminal when we ran commands that NPM gave us like Create React 	app. Well, that was using Node. We were running JavaScript outside of 	the browser.
	Node.js is most popular for building servers. Node.js is probably the 	server of choice for any web apps or mobile apps out there that want to 	create an API or a server. It's also really, really good at building chat 	applications and real time communication.
	You can download Node from nodejs.org and Andrei prefers to use the 	LTS version.
	To check node, you can simply type node -v in your terminal of choice.
	Node can be run anywhere.
	And one of my favorite things to do with note other than build servers is to 	actually create robots using JavaScript. This Johnny five is a JavaScript 	library that you can use with Node and if you have something like a 	Raspberry Pi. It helps you build functions using JavaScript that robots can 	perform.
	If you actually type 'node' in your terminal and press enter, you'll see that 	the prompt changes and you can run Node in the terminal. For example if 	you do console.log('hi'), you get hi. If you do four plus five, you get nine.
	If I type in 'window', I get a reference error window is not defined. This is 	because when we run Node. We don't have the window object. The 	window object was provided to us by the browser and as you know, node 	well, there's no browser that's running it. Instead, Node comes with 	something called global. And global has things just like the window object 	had a bunch of things that we can use, but it won't have browser specific 	things. For example, we won't be able to have global dot fetch. It's 	undefined because well, it doesn't exist. That was provided to us by the 	browser. We also don't have the document object, if you remember. If I do 	document again, I get a reference error because well, there's no 	document. So that's something to keep in mind. Node comes with its own 	properties and methods that we use, but other than browser specific 	things, we can do anything we want with Node.
	One of the global objects that we have is something called process.
	If I press enter again, I have a ton of stuff and in node process means this 	process. What I'm running right now in the terminal is the process, what 	the computer is doing. Now, if I do process exit. I exit this process, and 	now I'm back to my terminal.
	Now that we have Node in our power, we're going to start writing some 	scripts and show you how we can start writing actual JavaScript, not in the 	terminal but in your text editor.

	emember in the last video how I said fetch() is browser specific and not 	part of Node? Well, as of Node 17.5.0 fetch() is now a native feature of 	Node!
	https://blog.logrocket.com/fetch-api-node-js/

300. Running script.js in Node
	Let's expand on our Node knowledge from the previous video and actually 	create a script file that we can run. 
	In our terminal we can do the 'touch' command here to create a file and 	we'll just call it 'script.js'.
	~Users/carme/Documents/Programming
	$ touch script.js
	We now have a script.js in our Programming folder. Now within this folder 	I can now do anything in JavaScript.
	We'll say 'const a = 4' 'const b = 5' And then we can just 'console.log(a + 	b)'. If I save this in order to run this script, all I do is 'node' and then the 	script name: 'script.js'. And because we're in the directory it'll know what 	'script.js' is. And it'll give us 9.
	Now you also see that it exited; it didn't stay in the program, and that is 	because at the end of the file, once it reads everything and executes it; so 	the last thing it executes is console.log, then it automagically runs 	process.exit and exits out of the file.	
	If I did something called 'setTimeout', which we actually have in Node; if I 	do 'node' 'global.setTimeout', I get a function.
	global.setTimeout
	So if I do 'setTimeout', I can run a function again using our arrow 	functions. And within here I can do 'console.log()', and 'setTimeout' is cool 	because it allows you to say, "hey, when do you want this to be 	executed?" And that's the second parameter and I'll say 3 seconds. 
	const a = 4;
	const b = 5;
	setTimeout(() => {
		console.log(a + b);
	}, 3000)
	If I save and I do 'node script.js'; one, two, three, and there we go: 9, and 	the it process.exits. Node is smart enough to run through this and once it 	is done, it ends the process and comes back to the terminal.
	Within node we can actually use things that might be useful for us based 	on what global objects we have. For example one of the most popular 	ones are '__dirname'.
	console.log(__dirname);
	If I save this and run 'node script.js', I get my 	directory 	'~Users/carme/Documents/Programming'
	As you can see, we have the power of the browser and running the scripts 	that we want right here in our Terminal.
301. ES2020: globalThis
	globalThis is a global variable that we now get, and if we type it in our 	browser console, it points to the window object. So:
	globalThis === window -> true
	Yep, globalThis is pretty much the window object that we get as part of 	our browser environment.
	The window object is what gives us things like set interval, set time out or 	any of the features that the browsers allow us to do. 
	globalThis works outside the browser as well.
	If we type global in our terminal, and also type globalThis, it's the exact 	same thing as the global object that Node has.
	It's still not really the window object, however, the global this object now 	exists as both part of the window and note so that you can have unified 	variables across different platforms, different systems. 
	So although globalThis has different Properties inside of a browser than it 	does inside of Node, we're able to have a unified code base if, let's say 	we want to run our app both in code as well as on the browser. 
	Now, keep in mind that if I exit out of here. The reason I'm able to use the 	globalThis inside of note is that I have the latest version of note or at least 	a version that's higher than this. So you might not have global this work in 	Node unless you have a later version, more modern version that has 	implemented this new feature.
302. Modules In Node
	Going back to my example here let's add a new file, and we'll call this 	'script2.js'.
	Now the 'script2.js' can have a constant called 'largeNumber'. And let's 	just say that's 356. 
	const largeNumber = 356;
	export default largeNumber;
	And if we want to export this and now within 'script.js' I do 
	import largeNumber from 'script2.js';
	const a = largeNumber;
	And now I changed a to 'largeNumber' we'll just do 'a + b' if I run 'node 	script.js' I get an error 'Unexpected token import'. 
	And that is because Node has yet to implement the new ES6 feature of 	import statements.
	There are ways around it and you can add packages in order to allow you 	to do this. But we're going to learn the CommonJS way because most 	servers that you'll encounter will have the CommonJS way.
	In order to get familiarized with CommonJS we're going to use the 	'require' form.
	In Node 8.9, well, we will have to do:
	const largeNumber = require('./script2.js');
	Within here we have to give it the file path './'.
	We now have 'largeNumber' in script.js but we also want to export it now. 	The way exports work with CommonJS is you do 'module.export'.
	If I do 'node' and I do 'module' you see that 'module' is a global object that 	we have access to, and using 'module.exports' I can say what objects I 	want to export in this file.
	module.exports = {
		largeNumber: largeNumber
	};
	Now if we run node script.js we get [object Object]5
	Because now we've exported this object, and the way we want to access 	it, in our import delcaration,
	const script2 = require('./script2.js')
	we're exporting a global object which we can name whatever. In our case 	we can say 'script2' and in order to get the property of 'script2' well we'll 	say 'script2.largeNumber'.
	const a = script2.largeNumber;
	Now if I run this I get 361.
	It's a bit of a different style than we're used to on the front-end. And that's 	only because we haven't implemented the, or Node hasn't implemented 	the import and export with this version of it. But it will come eventually. 	Just don't get scared if you see it this way; once you get used to the 	syntax it's pretty much the same.
303. ES6 Modules??
	ES6 imports were added in Node v12.2.0!
304. Modules In Node
	You see, up until now, we had to use the 'require' syntax, which is what 	we call the common JS syntax with Node. 
	Originally Node was created with this syntax in mind because JavaScript 	wasn't created with modules in mind and they never had the import export 	syntax that we have with ES6.
	If you have the latest version of Node you can 	now start to change from 	require to using import export keywords. Let's convert this to ES6.
	So the first thing we can do with script.js is to remove this entire thing and 	we can just simply say:
	export const largeNumber = 356;
	So now this gets exported.
	Now in script.js we can comment out the 'require' statement, and just say, 	using destructuring:
	import { largeNumber }
	So this object, this destructuring simply says out of the script2 object that 	we're going to import, just grab the property, large number.
	Then we have to add
	from './script2.js'
	Now that we've imported largeNumber. And we did object destructuring 	we don't need to call script.largeNumber, instead, we can just simply do 	largeNumber.
	So will this work?
	Well, let's test it out.
	Open up the terminal in VSCode. In here, we have to make sure we're in 	the right folder so we should cd into the folder and in here we have the 	script and script2 .js.
	This should work so let's test it.
	node script.js
	Syntax error cannot use a statement outside a module. Why is that? Node 	gives us some hints. 
	'Warning to Load an ES6 module set type:module in package.json or use 	the .mjs extension.
	This is a little gacha.
	Because node was created with this common import export in mind, we 	need to actually specify how we want Node to read these files, so we 	know that we're using ES6 syntax.
	And I also want to show you how you can find this out on the 	documentation website for Node. 
	Usually what you would do is Google ECMAScript 6 Node.js. You'll 	probably get to the Node.js documentation. 
	We can see here we have module's, ECMAScript modules, and let's go to 	enabling. And if we click on enabling, we see over here we need to add 	the .mjs file extension, or  inside of package.json, we have to update the 	field. 
	So if I click on the type field, I'll read about it here and I say, oh, import 	segments of JS files are treated as ES modules if the nearest parent 	package.json  contains 'type': 'module'.
	So let's rename them script.mjs and script2.mjs, and double check that 	the syntax we're importing is also .mjs (import statement).
	If we try to run it without specifying the file extension, ./'script2', we get 	and error, but if we do ./'script2.mjs' everything works!
	Now for the other option, let's rename this back (some people really don't 	like the syntax .mjs and they prefer this method instead). So, run this 	again and make sure we change this back to .js, and if we run our code, 	it'll give us an error because again, we have to add type: module inside of 	package.json.
	We don't have packaged.json. 
	CREATING PACKAGE.JSON
	We can just run the command 'npm init' and then '-y' to say yes to all the 	options to simply create our package.json.
	So in here we want to add `"type": "module"` so that it reads the 	package .json and says, "Oh, yeah, they're going to be using ES6 	modules".
	Now if we run node script.js, it works!
	If you go on NPM and try to download packages, a lot of them still suggest 	that you use the require syntax, and that is because you're not sure that 	everybody has the latest version of Node.
	Some people might be running a 	Node version that is lower than version 	12. In that case, the code isn't going to work. 
	That's why for the rest of the course and even for the final Smart Brain 	project, I've left our Node files with the 'require' syntax so that anybody, 	any student taking this course can run those without any errors.
	Feel free to update them if you ever want with ES6 imports.
305. ES2022: Top Level Await
	const arr = [100, 200, 400, 50000, 10];	arr[2]; //400
	arr.at(2); //400
	arr[arr.length - 2]; //50000
	arr.at(-2); //50000
	Now, if you remember, I showed you that we can do this cool at method 	now with ease 2022. 
	But then I left you on a cliffhanger and I said the best part of is 2022 is this 	new feature called Top Level Await, and it's now time to talk about it.
	Now, do you remember how in the previous lectures we talked about 	async await and I gave you this exercise where you had to solve problems 	and you had to use the async await feature. Now during those videos, I 	told you that in order for you to use await, you have to wrap it in an async 	function. 
	async function fetchStarship() {
		const response = await 	fetch("http://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users");
		const data = await response.json();
		console.log(data);
	}
	Your ability to use await was dependent on the fact that we use this async 	keyword. Without it, we would get errors. That is no more, because in 	2022, we have top level await.
	If we completely remove the function and just do:
	const response = await 	fetch("http://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users");
	const data = await response.json();
	We get no errors, and if we then do:
	data;
	We get our 10 user object response.
	Now there are still times where we might want to declare an async 	function. There is a catch when using Top Level Await which we will 	cover. Declaring async is nice. It's very descriptive, right? We know right 	away that it is an async function. 
	So there's still use for the async keyword, but there are times where you 	don't really need this. And by just having the await keyword of the top, it 	cleans up the code of it.
	We're going to rewrite the problems solutions without the async functions.
	The power of this feature really comes into play when we start talking 	about Node and Node modules. So we're going to do this exercise. But 	remember that this top level await the true power of top level await is 	going to be explained in the node modules.
306. Exercise: ES2022 Top Level Await
	1. Complete the below exercise files. Do your best, and then take a look 	at the solution file (this is a tricky one!).
	2. Try running the test.js file using the node command in your terminal:
	in your terminal, run the command once you unzip the attached file which 	contains test.js:
	$ node test.js
	the test.js file contains this:
	const response = await 	fetch("https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users");
	const data = await response.json();
	console.log(data);
	error/output you get should be this:
	SyntaxError: await is only valid in async functions and the top level bodies 	of modules.
	I will explain why you are getting this error in the ES2022: Await Import 	video which is coming up. The key to remember with this feature is that 	you can ONLY use the await keyword at the top level (without async 	function) inside of a module! Luckily for us, we just learned what a module 	is!
307. ES2022 Await Import
	We've had two lectures before on ES2022. We talked about the at() 	feature that works on arrays and then we talked about the top level await 	and how you can now use the await keyword without that async keyword 	wrapped around it. 
	Now the true power of this top level await can finally be demonstrated with 	this lecture that we just did. We talked about ES6 modules and how now 	we can just import things really, really nicely and we use script and 	script2.js to demonstrate it. 
	How can we use top level await to expand upon this? Well, remember, 	await helps us await something like a fetch function which returned a 	promise, but we can also use it on things like import.
	So what we can do is actually have imports later in our file and have them 	conditionally import things. So for example, if I do something like:
	if (1 === 1)
	Obviously, it's going to say true for now, but I'm just giving you an 	example. So if one equals to one. I'm going to await import. And I'm going 	to import script2.js.
	if(1 === 1) {
		const { largeNumber } = await import './script2.js';
	}
	along with
	const b = 7
	console.log(largeNumber,b)
	If we run this, node script.js, we get an error.
	Unexpected string
	So what we need to do is actually wrap this in brackets.
	const { largeNumber } = await import('./script2.js');
	Now we get error: largeNumber is not defined.
	If you remember, we're declaring a variable inside of these brackets. So if 	you remember the lecture on scope, it's not a global scoped large 	number. 
	What we can do is say:
	let a;
	if (1 === 1) {
		const { largeNumber = await import('./script2.js')
		a = largeNumber
	}
	const b = 7
	console.log(a,b)
	So if we go back to a here like we had it before save I know this is a little 	convoluted, but hopefully it explains the topic nicely. If I run this again. It 	works.
	So the cool thing with the top level await is that now I can include 	modules conditionally. Maybe I say that hey, import this module if 	something is true, if a condition isn't met, then we don't want to waste 	resources importing a module of the top of our file, only imported if we 	actually need it.
	There's lots of cool things that you can do, like code splitting with it. But 	this is a cool new feature that really allows us to do some powerful things 	in JavaScript and especially while working with modules.
	For fun, if we change 1 === 1 to 1 === 2, we get 'undefined 7' which 	means we did not import the largeNumber module based on a false 	condition!
308. Types of Modules
	Three kinds of modules in Node
	We've seen that we have this type which we already know about which is 	a module that you create yourself like 'script2' and has its module exports 	and you give it the path to the file so you can find it.
	There's also something called 'built-in modules' and built-in modules are 	modules that come already pre-installed with Node.
	For example one of the more popular ones is the 'fs' module:
	const c = require('fs')
	This module, if I console.log(c) is a module with a whole bunch of things 	once again, but it allows you to read the file system.
	That is, if you had a text file and you wanted to read through the text file 	and extract out how many times the word 'hello' was said, well you can 	write that by simply using the file system module and using something like 	'readFile:' or 'readFileSync:' which will output; do something like 'readFile':
	const c = require('fs').readFile;
	It will output the contents of the file and then you can do whatever you 	want with it.
	Another popular one is the HTTP module which as you can guess is the 	module that we'll use later on to build a server.
	const c = require('http');
	And then finally the third type of module is something from npm, a 	package. And one of my favorite packages to use with Node is something 	called 'nodemon' and nodemon is really really nice. 
	I'll have to do 'npm init' first to make sure that I have a 'package.json' file 	and if I pass the '-y' it'll just say yes to all the prompts that I get 	beforehand. 
	I have my 'package.json', and now I'm going to say 'npm install 	nodemon --save-dev' If I go to 'package.json' now I have nodemon in 	'devDependencies', and we haven't seen this before. 
	Up until now we've done '--save' or nothing at all and automatically put it in 	'dependencies' 'devDependencies' are dependencies that we only use 	when we're developing. When we actually release this app or the server it 	won't be included because nodeman is just for our enjoyment while we're 	developing.
	"devDependencies": {
		"nodemon': "^1.12.7"
	},
	nodemon now can be used because it's in 'node_modules'.
	If you go to '.bin' you see nodemon here. Because it's in the package 	'node_modules->.bin' I can use the command such as "start" and say 	"nodemon":
	"scripts": {
		"start": "nodemon"
	},
	Node is smart enough to know that if I run nodemon it's going to look in 	the '.bin' folder and find it there; if it finds it there, which it does, it'll run the 	command. So let's see what happens after we change back to our original 	a + b example.
	npm start
	[nodemon] 1.12.7
	[nodemon] to restart at any time, enter 'rs'
	[nodemon] watching: *.*
	[nodemon] starting `node script.js`
	361
	[nodemon] clean exit - waiting for changes before restart
	But you see that something is still running. 
	If I change this now to 6:
	b = 6;
	and I save, it'll watch those changes for me:
	[nodemon] restarting due to changes...
	[nodemon] starting `node script.js`
	362
	[nodemon] clean exit - waiting for changes before restart
	it keeps listening to changes and it will output everything. Again if I change 	this, so I don't have to keep running the 'node script.js' command.
309. Building a Server
	I think it's time to create our own server now. So let's create a new file 	called 	'server.js'. [touch server.js] And in order for nodemon to listen to 'server.js'. 	We need to add server.js to our npm start script.
	"scripts": {
		"start": "nodemon server.js"
	},
	Now if I save this nodemon will listen to this as long as I say 'npm start'.
	Node comes with its own HTTP module and that's what we're going to use 	to create our server. It's very very simple to use. 
	const http = require('http');
	We simply grab the HTTP module and then create a server by defining it:
	const server = http.createServer(() => {
		console.log('I hear you! Thanks for the request.);
	})
	server.listen(3001);
	In this 'createServer' we'll create an arrow function and within here for now 	let's just do 'console.log('I hear you! thanks for the request)' And then finally 	we have to do 'server.listen()' and give it the port number that we want to 	listen for. 
	You can do 3001 if you want. Now let's do 'node server.js'.
	And it looks like it's just hanging there. 
	Let's go to port 3001 on our localhost that is our machine and see what 	happens. If I open up a new tab and go to localhost 3001 nothing's 	happening. If I open up the console, nothing's there, but if I go back I see 	"I hear you. Thanks for the request" in the terminal. That is because  	'server.js' is running in Node. It's running here in the Terminal. But it's 	listening to connections. 
	So when I hit 'refresh' here or I press Enter and try to connect, it will be 	listening and it will run 'console.log()' and you can see the second "I hear 	you. Thanks for the request" just here. If I refresh two more times. Well 	again I 	get two more. 
	But right now the browser is just hanging there because we're not doing 	anything. We're not responding with anything. You can see at the bottom 	it says 'waiting for localhost'. We're just console logging on our end but 	we're not getting any response. 
	Let's do that.
	I'm going to remove the console log and use the parameters that 	'createServer' gets us. So any time we try to connect, we have a 'request' 	then a 'response' parameter that we can use.
	const server = http.createServer((request, response) => {
	Within here we can do something like 'response.setHeader()' ... 'Content-	Type', we're letting you know what kind of content we were going to send 	and it's going to be, 'text/html', and this is just the standard way of 	declaring content type and we'll say 'response.end()' with an '' tag that 	says 	'Helloooooo'.
	const server = http.createServer((request, response) => {
		response.setHeader('Content-Type', 'text/html');
		response.end('<h1>Helllooo</h1>');
	})
	We have to restart our server. If I go back and refresh I get 'helloooooo'; if 	I go to the Network tab I see that localhost I get a 200 response; if I click 	on this I see that the response was 'helloooooo' If I go to Headers I see 	that it was a 200 status code and the content type was 'text/html'.
	So for example in here I could have said 'console.log(request.headers)' I 	could've also said 'method' and finally something like 'url'. 'request' gives 	us a lot information about the requests that we receive. And just so we are 	clear on what each one of these are. I can say 'headers', 'method' and 	'url'.
	const server = http.createServer((request, response) => {
		console.log('header', request.headers)
		console.log('method', request.method)
		console.log('url', request.url)
		response.setHeader('Content-Type', 'text/html');
		response.end('<h1>Helllooo</h1>');
	})
	I see that I get headers, our host: 'localhost: 3000'. We see that 'user-	agent' 	was Mozilla, Macintosh. So that's what we used. We used an Apple 	to make that request.
	The method was a 'GET' method and the url was just the backslash [\]. 	Let's just comment out the headers for now and show you how things 	change. 
	If I now enter 'localhost:3000/profile/123' I get 'method GET' and 'url' was 	'/profile/123' So we get information about the request that the front-end 	made, which is very cool.
	Now with our response we responded with 'text/html' but you remember 	JSON right? We talked about how useful JSON is when doing AJAX 	requests.
	We can change Content-Type to application/json.
	response.setHeader('Content-Type', 'application/json');
	Now we can send a JavaScript object such as 'user' and we'll say "name: 	'John'" "hobby: 'Skating'".
	const user = {
		name: 'John',
		hobby: 'Skating'
	}
	Now this user, I can pass here, but remember in order to transfer 	between the wires we need to use 'JSON.stringify'.
	response.end(JSON.stringify(user));
	If I save and let's go back and refresh I get a JSON response again 	"name: 'John', hobby: 'skating'" and if I click on the Network tab here I see 	that the 'Content-Type' is 'application/json' and the response is in JSON 	format. 
	So on the front-end we'll run 'json.parse()' And then this 	[{"name:"John","hobby":"Skating"}] in order to convert it to a JavaScript 	object.
	We've just created our very first server but this HTTP is pretty bare bones; 	as you can imagine, building servers is something that everybody does 	that has a website and there are many tools that we can use now that are 	more elegant than running HTTP, and the most popular and the best one 	to use with Node.js is something called Express which we will talk about 	next.
310. Introduction To Express.js
	Express is the most popular and highest rated backend framework since 	2017.
	We're going to delete the server we built previously. Now let's do "npm 	install express" so now we have Express available to us.
	To create a server with it, we can do:
	const express = require('express');
	const app = express();
	app.listen(3001);
	and then we can do npm start to run nodemon
	we see that our server is running. If we go to localhost:3001 we get cannot 	GET / and that is because, well, again, we're not doing anything, but in 	just three lines we have a server.
	Express has really extensive documentation on getting started and a ton 	of information on how to use it.
	For now, we will build out our own.
	app.get('/', (req, res) => {
    		res.send("Helllooo!");
	});
	If you remember, in our localhost, we got 'cannot GET /'. To do a get 	request with Express, we simply do dot get and the first parameter is the 	path. We used the root path '/'. 
	The second parameter receives a request and a response and in Express, 	instead of writing everything out, the common way of doing it is doing rec 	and res.
	We can say response.send. And we'll just send a 'hello', just like we did 	when we used the HTTP module that comes with node.
	If we save, we see that it works! And if we open up the console and  	network tab, we can see the response 'hello' and if we look at the 	headers. we can see that the Content-Type was automatically set for us, 	which in this case is text/html.
	Instead of doing something like res.send("<h1>Hello</h1>"), we can also 	now just send JSON. So lets create a User object again.
	const user = {
        		name: 'Sally',
        		hobby: 'soccer'
    	}
	and then add it to the res.send
	res.send(user);
	Now if we refresh, we will see the JSON object. And if we check the 	header, we will see application/json.
	You can see the power of Express, even just from the simple example, it 	takes away a lot of the repetition and makes our life a whole lot easier.
	We've only done get here, but you can also do post requests.
	app.post('/profile', (req, res) => {
    		res.send("Helllooo!");
	});
	So, for example, if I did post /profile, well, now if I refresh. I get an error 	because now I've made the request method, which was 'get' from the 	Browser, but here I only accept a post request from '/profile'.
	app.get('/profile', (req, res) => {
    		res.send("Helllooo!");
	});
	If I do '.get', we get cannot GET /profile because even though we are 	going 	to the proper URL (/profile) now we're doing a get request. 
	Any time we refresh the browser, it's a 'get' request. And in order to do 	that, we need to actually send some data over.
	I'll show you how to do that in the next couple of videos. But just to show 	you how Express works, I can keep adding these routes, lets do 'get 	/profile' that returns 'getting profile'.
	app.get('/profile', (req, res) => {
		res.send("getting profile");
	});
	If we do another 'get', this time to the root URL, and this one, we'll just say 	getting root.
	app.get('/', (req, res) => {
		res.send("getting root");
	});
	app.get('/profile', (req, res) => {
		res.send("getting profile");
	});
	app.post('/profile', (req, res) => {
		const user = {
        			name: 'Sally',
        			hobby: 'soccer'
    		}
    		res.send("Helllooo!");
	});
	If I save this now and refresh /profile, and go into the network tab, I'll get a 	two hundred, OK, with the response of 'getting profile'. And if I do no 	profile ('/') and just the URL, I get 'getting root'.
	The way Express works is it checks each one line by line. So, for 	example, it will check, did the request have the root URL ('/'), did the get 	request have /profile, did the post request have /profile? 
	If it matches any of these. It will run whatever the function inside of it is.
	If I do a URL that doesn't exist ('dasd'). I get a 404 not found because, 	well. We did a get request to this URL that doesn't exist. 
	So using this, we can use the 'app.get' method, the 'app.post' method, the 	'app.put' method and the 'app.delete' method that we're going to actually 	demonstrate in the next couple of videos.
	As we can see, it's a simple matter of saying if you go to this route, do 	this, if you go to this one, do this and everything is just logical and trickles 	all the way down.
311. Express Middleware
	Middleware is an important concept in Express.
	If we npm start a basic server that sends 'testest':
	const express = require('express');
	const app = express();
	app.use()	
	Express has a concept of middleware and if I do 'app.use()' this is a 	generic Express middleware that I can enter in here. And what 	middleware does is, as the name suggests, as the request is coming in 	it's going to pass through this 'use' function and then trickle down to 	whatever we GET, POST, 	PUT, DELETE, and this middleware is going to 	do something to the request that we just had in order to perhaps make it 	easier to work with down here.
	app.get('/', (req, res) => {
		res.send('testest'');
	});
	app.listen(3001);
	Let's demonstrate that example: 'app.use()' receives 'request', 'response' 	and then something called 'next'.
	app.use(req, res, next) => {
		console.log('<h1>Hellooo</h1>')
	})
	If we save and go back here and run localhost, it's hanging. 	Even if we 	open up the Console, nothing's happening. 
	If we go back I see that 'helllooo' gets triggered because I'm console 	logging it but it never reaches here well that is because in order for the 	middleware to keep passing data through it needs to call 'next'. The third 	parameter.
	console.log('<h1>Hellooo</h1>')
	next();
	So now by calling 'next' if I refresh I receive 'testest'.
	There are many many cases that I'll show you later on where middleware 	is important, and middlewares are great scripts that people have pre-	written 	that you can add to your server.
312. Express Version Update
	In the next video, we will be installing a package:
	npm install body-parser
	However, if you are running a version of Express that is 4.16+, it now 	includes the same functionality inside of Express.
	Instead of adding these lines in the code like I will show you in the next 	video:
	app.use(bodyparser.urlencoded({extended: false}));
	app.use(bodyparser.json());
	If you are using Express 4.16+ you can now replace that with:
	app.use(express.urlencoded({extended: false}));
	app.use(express.json());
313. Postman
	app.get('/', (req, res) => {
		res.send("getting root");
	});
	app.get('/profile', (req, res) => {
		res.send("getting profile");
	});
	app.post('/profile', (req, res) => {
		const user = {
        			name: 'Sally',
        			hobby: 'soccer'
    		}
    		res.send(user);
	});
	Let's try to get this 'post' request to work that we couldn't in the previous 	video.
	In order for us to test something more than just GET, we could use the 	Terminal, but there's a really nice tool that allows us to make these 	requests to a server and see what we get back, called Postman. If you go 	to www.getpostman.com it is one of the best tools if you're ever working 	with a server. It's free and you can download it for your operating system.
	We have here the requests that we want to get. And a few that you've 	never 	seen before but don't worry about them. They are very very rarely 	used. 	We see the ones that we care about DELETE, PUT, POST, GET 	right there and you simply enter the URL that you want to have this 	running on.
	So in our example let's do 'npm start' again. And while the server is 	running let's just go to '/profile'. And then in here we want to say 	'localhost:3000/profile'.
	We want to do a GET request over here. If I click send, I get 'getting 	profile'. It gives me the Headers and the Body, which is great!
	Now because we have this tool we can also do a POST request. So let's 	say that we are no longer sending the 'user'. 
	We want to send a response 'res'. And from this response we want to 	return 	whatever user that we submit to '/profile', so we can simply remove 	our user object, and leave res.send(user) as this will send the 'user' that 	we submit as a response through Postman.
	We're going to change this to a POST and with a POST we can add 	things 	to the body, and you see over here that there's a few options: 	'form-data' the 'x-www-form-urlencoded', 'raw', and 'binary'.
	The first two are HTML forms and raw is for JSON/text/html etc.
	If we click on 'x-www-form-urlencoded', we get key and value text boxes to 	input. here we can put name and andrei (name: andrei).
	Now when I send this I get 'Sally' and 'soccer' still, but we see that the 	POST request is working. If I want to access this information I do 	'(req.body)'.
	console.log(req.body)
	If we click send we get 'undefined' and that is because Express only gives 	us a little bit. We still need a few packages if we want this to work. 
	If we ever want to access '(req.body)' well we need to use a middleware 	and the middleware that we want is called body-parser and you'll use it on 	most Express servers, so you'll just get used to it. 
	It's 'npm install body-parser'. And now this body-parser, what it's going to 	do, is it's going to grab whatever information we receive and parse it just 	like JSON or forms into something that we can use.
	In order to actually use it well, we know how middleware works, we say 	'app.use()' and body-parser it will come with a 'bodyParser' that we'll just 	require with the package that we just installed. 
	In 'app.use()' we simply do 'bodyParser.urlencoded', which is how we're 	able to get this ['x-www-form-urlencoded'], and we just have to pass it a 	parameter: {extended: false}. 
	app.use(bodyParser.urlencoded({extended: false}))
	If we try it again, save, 'npm start', and back and send again, we {name: 	'andrei'}!
	If you're building a server for a form that gets submitted you'll use this.
	There's also JSON that we can use, and the way we use JSON is we click 	on 'raw' and in here we can send whichever things we want 	(text/html/json/etc); in our case it's going to be JSON, and with JSON we 	can now say what we want to send. 
	If we ever do any wrong syntax, it'll tell us that this is wrong JSON.
	Let's just say for the '/profile' I want to add a new user and this user will be 	called Jenny and Jenny will have a hobby of tennis.
	{
		"user": "Jenny",
		"hobby": "tennis"
	}
	If we send this we get an empty object because we've only told it to parse 	'urlencoded'.
	body-parser needs to also know about JSON. In order to do that we can 	add under our other 'use' statement:
	app.use(bodyParser.json())
	If we save that and send again,  we have { user: 'Jenny', hobby: 'tennis' }.
	Now we could add this user to our database and then send a response 	'Success'.
	app.post('/profile', (req, res) => {
		(code to add to database)
		res.send('Success');
	});
	Now if we try it, we get a response 'Success' that we've "posted "Jenny" 	"tennis"". In this way we can also do PUT, which is very similar, we just 	send the data, but PUT is used for updating, if we want to update perhaps 	Jenny to have a different hobby. Then DELETE to delete maybe that user, 	something that we'll get into in the next video.
	314. Quick Note: req.header
	Just a heads up that in the next lecture I am using req.header inside of a 	console.log to retrieve the header data in a GET route. Depending on 	which 	version of express.js you use, (a more recent version has changed 	the syntax) it now is req.headers. req.header will only return the function 	declaration.
315. RESTful APIs
	A REST API defines a set of functions which developers can perform 	requests and receive responses via an HTTP protocol, such as GET, 	POST, PUT, DELETE.
	A web browser sends a request and just hopes that the server returns 	what the browser asked for. A Google server can send us whatever it 	wants. It doesn't have to follow the rules that perhaps the web browser 	sets. 
	With RESTful API we're able to create an API that is RESTful - something 	that follows the rules that everybody can agree on so that we have 	compatibility between different systems.
	So a RESTful API is an architectural style and it's an approach to 	communications; an agreed upon set of rules so everyone plays nicely. A 	RESTful API users GET to receive a resource, PUT to change the state or 	update a resource, a POST that creates a resource, and a DELETE to 	remove it.
	The RESTful API is a way to define our server so that it specifies what it 	can provide and how to use it.
	the URL parameters should make sense.
	For example if we're doing '/profile', we expect that if it's a GET request 	we're going to get a profile.
	If it's a POST request we're going to add a profile.
	If it's a PUT perhaps we're updating the profile, and this URL makes 	sense.
	You can think of this as the noun of what we want to receive. The HTTP 	request is the verb and everything after the slash [/] is the noun.
	Finally REST APis are something called 'stateless'. meaning that calls can 	be made independently of one another and each call contains all the data 	necessary to complete itself successfully. 
	A server doesn't need to keep memorizing things. Each request that 	comes in has enough information that the server can respond with, 	regardless of who that person is.
	Let's build a small little app that has a RESTful API. 
	With our previous example, we can start the server. 
	We can clean up the gets and posts and leave: 
	app.get('/', (req, res) => {
		res.send("getting root");
	});
	This GET request will have a request object that we receive. Now this 	request object can have a few things:
	app.get('/', (req, res) => {
		req.query
		req.body
		req.headers
		req.params
		res.send("getting root");
	});
	'req.query' is what we get when we do a GET query. For example if I do a 	'console.log(req.query)' and go to localhost and add a query string:
	/?name=andrei&age=31
	Press Enter, go back and see in the terminal that I have { name: 'andrei', 	age: '31' } because I have access to the query string 	[console.log(req.query)].
	We also have request body [req.body], using something like your 	'urlencoded' or 'JSON' body-parsers, we can add that middleware to 	receive whatever the request sends in the body.
	We also have req.header [req.headers].
	if I 'console.log(req.header)' and use Postman, we can put anything in the 	Headers tab, ex. name: sally, and if we send this as a GET, we get all our 	headers, but also name: 'sally'.
	Finally we also have something called req.params. If we 	'console.log(req.params)' - this is a syntax where you use the parameters 	of the URL ('/').  If I do the semicolon and then let's say 'id' ('/:id'), save this 	and now we have access to whatever the parameter is of the URL.
	So let's just say 'localhost:3001/1234' and I'll remove the header, and 	'Send', I now have { id: '1234' } in the terminal being logged. You can have 	as many parameters as you want and you can nest them.
	When we send a response we also want to have a bit of an option as to 	what we want to send. With our response we can say that with sending 	we also want to respond with a status, let's say a status of 404 and we 	could say send("Not found").
	res.status(404).send("Not found")
	If I refresh and send, I get 'Not found' with a status of 404.
	If I go back to my browser and refresh, I have in my Network tab a 404 	status.
	Up until now we're just sending data back and forth. But what if we want to 	serve something called static assets, that is, an 'index.html', a CSS file, 	and a JavaScript file.
	Usually we want an index file and having our app run, which we can 	create within this project. 
	Do a new folder called public. This name might be familiar from when we 	did 'create-react-app'. Public was there and it held all our static files.
	In here let's just do a new file, index.html, and within this public file we'll 	just do a quick HTML document that says <title>HI</title>, and <body>It's 	working</body>.
	In order to get Express to send static files, we can remove everything but:
	const express = require('express');
	const bodyParser = require('body-parser');
	const app = express();
	app.listen(3001);
	Then we'll want to do another middleware 'app.use()'.
	This comes with Express already, so we do 'express.static()' and within 	here we give it the file path.
	In our case we know that we want to get the '__dirname', which we've 	seen 	in a previous video, which is the directory of where we are. 
	In our case its node and we want to go to the public folder so we do 	'public'. This is going to print out 'node' then 'public'. 
	app.use(express.static(__dirname + '/public'))
	So now if I save this and go back to our GET request I get 'it's working'.
	If we open up our Console and our Network tab, and do a hard refresh, 	we get '200' , we get 'text/html', and the response is my HTML file.
	If we had a CSS link it would load the CSS in my public folder. 
	If we had a JavaScript file then it would load it, because within the body 	we can add a script tag. 
	This is a server that we just built that serves simple static assets. And as 	soon as we want to have something like an API where we interact with it, 	GET, POST, PUT, DELETE, that's when we start using our 	methods that 	we learned previously.
316. Node File System Module
	In the previous video we talked about something called the FS module 	that comes with Node, and this is something that is built into Node.
	We need a file first, so let's do 'touch script.js' in our terminal, and create 	a new script. In the script.js, we can have
	const fs = require('fs')
	FS here stands for file system and if you have node downloaded you have 	access to this. By just doing this, 'fs', File System allows you to access our 	file system.
	I'm going to do a bit of coding and then I'm going to explain.
	fs.readFile('', (err, data) => {
		if (err) {
			console.log('Error!');
		}
		console.log(data);
	})
	I just imported the FS module that comes with node and the FS module 	has a method readFile() that well it looks like it reads some sort of a file.
	The first parameter is the actual file that we want to reference, so the path 	to the file, which we don't have yet. So let's create one. 'Hello.txt' inside 	the same folder we are working in. Then we can read this file:
	fs.readFile('./Hello.txt', (err, data) => {
	Now this readFile method is going to find the file with the path './Hello.txt'. 	It's going to read it and then if there's an error, it's going to spit out an 	error.
	If there's some sort of data and everything successful, it's going to spit out 	data. 
	With the way we can run this file in node, first we'll have to save it. We'll 	say node script.js Enter and we get '<Buffer 68 67 6c etc...'. What is that? 	(Hex Code) . If no encoding is specified, then the raw buffer is returned.
	We have to do a little bit of encoding. And what that means is if I do 	'toString()', I get 'Hello!'.
	console.log(data.toString());
	So the 'readFile' reads the file as the name suggests, and it's going to spit 	out whatever it's reading (data).
	Now why do we have to put in the 'toString()' here. Just so you know 	'toString()' does something, by default if you don't add anything, it's going 	to use the encoding called UTF 8 and UTF 8 is a type of encoding.
	console.log(data.toString('utf8'));
	This works all the same. UTF 8 is pretty standard with HTML 5. And pretty 	much everybody uses it on the web.
	It's a way for us to have characters, whether they're Latin characters, you 	know ABC's or we have Korean Japanese, Chinese characters, they're all 	represented by a number, and UTF 8 is a way of encoding these numbers 	to make sure that we can read them.
	It's actually quite a complex subject, so I recommend the Wikipedia page 	that talks about UTF 8. For now just know that UTF 8 is a type of 	encoding that is pretty much standard across the web. 
	You might have also seen ASCII and ASCII was before UTF 8 existed. 	ASCII was mainly for Latin based languages, so the ABCs; and UTF 	expanded that to include many more from many more languages from 	different parts of the world.
	Let's just add one other method to our repertoire. Let's also add 	'fs.readFileSync()':
	const file = 	fs.readFileSync('./Hello.txt');
	console.log(file.toString());
	This returns a second 'Hello!'.
	However, if we add a number to each console.log, '1' to readFile, and '2' 	to readFileSync, and run it, we get:
	2 Hello!
	1 Hello!
	This is because readFile is asynchronous, that's why it has something 	called the callback function. 
	It is saying: hey I'm going to read this file, and when I'm done with it, you 	just keep going on with your business and keep reading. Line three, line 	four, line five, line six, all the way to 11. When I'm done, I'll let you know 	and I'll either give you an error or some data.
	'readFileSync()', which is synchronous, says: I'm going to read this file. 	Don't do anything, just wait here when I'm done I'm going to assign it to 	'file' and then you can keep going. 
	So as you can see here what happens is we read the file and because 	readFile is asynchronous, it's going to run this and come to line 10 and 	run the 'fs.readFileSync()' and the 'readFileSync()' because it's saying hey 	wait until I'm done this, it is going to print out '2 Hello!' and then the 	asynchronous code is going to say: hey I finished reading the file, here's 	the data and it prints out '1 Hello!'
	If we have a massive file that has something more than 'Hello!', if we're 	doing 'readFileSync()', what happens is we're going to halt or pause the 	execution of our file and it's going to read the entire text. The program is 	just going to be waiting for that to finish; versus the read file which says: 	hey you can keep going ahead. I'll let you know when I'm done with this. 
	So when you're building a server and let's say an express server with 	routes. 	Well in that case we want to use readFile().
	Let's say the 'Hello!' text is kind of boring and we want to add to it. Well we 	can do something like 'fs.appendFile()'. 
	fs.appendFile('./Hello.txt', ' This is so cool!', err =>
		if (err) {
			console.log(err)
		}
	})
	We give it the file we want to append. So in this case it's './Hello.txt'. 
	If this doesn't exist, it'll actually just create the file for us. 	
	And the second parameter will say what we want to add. So let's say we 	also wanted to add maybe a space and then say "This is so cool", now 	there is an exclamation mark. 
	And finally third parameter takes in an error and this error will just say that 	if there is an error. Let me make this smaller so you can see - let's just say 	if there is an error, we'll just 'console.log'
	If we run this, our terminal will return the two console.log statements, and 	our file will be appended.
	2 Hello!
	1 Hello!
	(in Hello.txt.) Hello! This is so cool!
	If we run it again we get:
	2 Hello! This is so cool!
	1 Hello! This is so cool!
	(in Hello.txt.) Hello! This is so cool! This is so cool!
	So we learned the append. Let's comment this out so that it doesn't 	interfere with any of the other ones. Let's do a 'write' this time:
	fs.writeFile('Bye.txt', 'Sad to see you go., err => {
		if (err) {
			console.log(err)
		}
	})
	Now if we save and run the script, bye.txt will be created in the current 	directory, with the content 'Sad to see you go'.
	By the way, just so you know what happens, if let's say I misspell the read 	file and now it's 'hell.txt' Well if I run this, I'll get an error and you can see 	that because it errors out I get the error message, 'Error!'.	
	The final one we're going to do is going to be the 'delete'. So with that 	'delete' we can do 'fs.unlink()'.
	fs.unlink('./Bye.txt', err => {
		if (err) {
			console.log(err)
		}
		console.log('Inception')
	}
	The name is a little bit off here but 'fs.unlink()' and we'll say the 'bye.txt' 	and notice that I was able to just do 'bye.txt' but just to keep it consistent, 	I'll just say './bye.txt' with the './' before it and with this one. Again if there's 	any errors just 'console.log(err)'.
	Just for fun, let's just add a 'console.log()' here after it has done deleting 	and we'll say 'inception' because we're saying 'bye' to the './bye.text'. Mind 	blown.
	Comment out the writeFile, and run it, and when it executes, Bye.txt is 	deleted and 'Inception' is logged in the console.
	Now we're able to use programming in JavaScript outside of just web 	browsers and outside of just building websites and servers. 
	What we can do now with this knowledge is we can make our lives easier. 
	You can think of cases maybe you want to read an excel file and there are 	things that you can use, using read file and using NPM modules that you 	can read. Let's say. Columns in an excel and do some math.
	Using something like Node, you can create perhaps something that in 	every six hours, sends out five e-mails. 
	Another thing you can do if you had a robot for example something similar 	to a file system instead of reading from the file. You can read from	the 	robot, maybe through wireless maybe through wires and get an input of 	some data that the robot has. 
	Maybe it detected a cat. Well using that input, you can do something you 	can respond maybe send you an e-mail saying that the robot just detected 	a cat and send that e-mail out or send an output.
	That's what we call input output, where you're getting an input from 	another source, another machine and then you are also outputting 	something for consumption, which is really really exciting because it 	opens up the world where we can create tools for ourselves.
