319. Solution: Sant's Node Helper
PART 1:
	const fs = requite('fs');
	// 1 - What floor does Santa end up on
	// ( --> should go UP one floor
	// ) should go DOWN one floor
	function question1() {
		fs.readFile('./santa.txt', (err, data) => {
			console.time('santa-time');
			const directions = data.toString();
			const directionsArray = directions.split('');
			const answer = directionsArray.reduce((acc, currentValue) 				=> {
				if (currentValue === '(') {
					return acc += 1;
				} else if (currentValue === ')') {
					return acc -+ 1;
				}
			}, 0)
			console.timeEnd('santa-time');
			console.log('floor:', answer);
		})
	}
	question1()
PART 2:
	// - When does Santa first enter the basement
	function question2() {
		fs.readFile('./santa.txt', (err, data) => {
			console.time('santa-time');
			const directions = data.toString();
			const directionsArray = directions.split('');
			let accumulator = 0
			const answer = directionsArray
			.some((currentValue) => {
				if (currentValue === '(') {
					return accumulator += 1
				} else if (currentValue === ')') {
					return accumulator -= 1
				}
			})
			console.log('basement entered at: ', answer)
		})
	}

	Some looks through the array step by step and if it finds something that 	matches, it stops looping. So with 'directionsArray.some', we have the 	current value and this current value is the item or the bracket that we're 	getting.
	We made a mistake here because what we want to do from '.some' is we 	want to return something we want to return when a goal is met. So at the 	end we're going to say 'return accumulator' and when the accumulator is 	less than zero.
	return accumulator < 0;
	But we want to know when does Santa first enter the basement. So that is 	a: what number of directions does Santa make before he enter the 	basement. So we also need a counter. So this counter is going to be 	equal 	to zero.
	let accumulator = 0;
	let counter = 0;
	And we're going to increase the counter, every time it goes through an 	item. 
	counter ++;
	return accumulator < 0;
	And now the answer, well not this, but the answer is going to be the 	counter itself. So:
	console.log('basement entered at: ', counter);
	Actually made a mistake here because it's still zero because the counter 	is being incremented after the return statement. So 'currentValue', let's 	actually name this 'currentItem'. 
	.some((currentItem) => {
	It's a lot more descriptive: 'currentItem'. This is being returned before you 		even hit the counter. So in our case, unlike above, we don't really need 	the return statement so we simply remove those and the code will now 	continue iterating.
Section 26: FINAL PROJECT: SmartBrain Back-End -- Server
320. Setting Up Our Server
	Here we are going to build out the back-end for our FaceRecognition app. 	This will give the front-end something to communicate with, so that the 	app can live on more than just our local machine; we can actually deploy 	it. 
	Currently we have a sign in and a register page, but those are not linked 	to anything. Once we build a back-end, we can interact with it, and even 	include a database so there's memory, and we can store actual users.
	With Node and Express, we are simply creating URLs and endpoints and 	providing a response to the front-end.  
	If we look at our app, we can figure out what endpoints we may need to 	create. A sign in, a register, and a profile for when we are logged in. 
	As we build this out, we won't really be touching our front-end. We need to 	figure out what the functionality is that we want, and test it out on 	something like Postman. With Postman we're going to test out server like 	we did in the	previous section, get all of our end points working, and then 	finally connect it to our front-end. 
	Let's create a new folder, face-recognition-api, and run an 'npm init' inside 	it to create our package.json. Once this is complete we can install some 	dependency packages that we will be using to build out our server. 'npm 	install express' so that we can build a server nicely, 'npm install nodemon' 	so that we can run it in our scripts and make sure our server is ufnctioning 	properly, and 'npm install body-parser' so that we can parse and have 	access ti the request body (req.body) to read JSON and form data.
	Now we can 'touch server.js' to create our JavaScript server file. Once 	done, we can change our package.json to be able to run Nodemon with 	our server. 
	"scripts": {
		"start": "Nodemon server.js"
	}
	Remember that with JSON we need out key AND value values to be 	wrapped in quotations "". With that, every time we run npm start, we will 	launch our server, and be able to track it in our terminal. Now let's get our 	basic skeleton of our Express app.
	const express = require('express'); OR import express from '/.express'
	const app = express();
	app.listen(3001, () => {
		console.log('App is running on Port 3001');
	});
	Inside our app.listen, we can pass a second parameter which is a 	function, and within this function we can do things like console.log.
	Now that our app is running, we can create a basic route to make sure 	everything works nicely.
	app.get('/', (req, res) => {
		res.send('This is working :)')
	}
	This will be on the root route '/', and will get a request and a response. 	Inside the 'get' we will do a res.send. If we go over to Postman and do a 	'get' from localhost:3001/, we get our response.
	Now that we have everything set up, it's a good idea to plan our API. This 	is something that as a developer we really want to do before we just start 	coding. We want to have an idea of what our API design will look like. So 	let's think about it. We can wrap it in comments and work on it one by one.
	*/
	/ (root) --> res = this is working
	We want a root route that responds with this is working.
	/signin --> POST = success/fail
	We want a signin route that will be a POST request because we're 	POSTing some data, some JSON, some user information, and it's going 	to respond with either success or fail.
	/register --> POST = user
	We want a register route, which will be similar to signin, in that it will have 	a POST request, because we want to add the data to the database, or in 	our case a variable in our server with our new user information. So instead 	of success/fail, we will return the newly created user to make sure that 	everything is working.
	/profile/:userId --> GET = user
	We also want a homescreen route with the ability to access the profile of 	the user. So we'll have /profile with an optional parameter of 'userId' so 	that each user has their own homescreen. This will have a GET request, 	which will get the user information and return us the user. 
	Because we want to work with the ranking or anytime a user posts a new 	photo we want to make sure that their count of how many photos they've 	submitted goes up, we'll need a way to keep score, a variable that 	increases by one every time a user submits a photo, and then also checks 	against other users to see who has submitted how many, and allocate the 	user to a rank referentially. 
	/image --> PUT --> user
	With that in mind, we can have an image endpoint route, that will be a 	POST, or actually a PUT, because the user already exists and we want to 	make sure that there is an update on the user profile. This will simply 	return the updated user object, or whatever we've updated, in our case a 	count of some sort.
	With signin, even though we are not creating a new user object we still 	want to use POST, because any time we're sending a password, we don't 	really want to send it as a query string, as that would be visible in the 	URL. We want to send it inside the body, ideally over HTTPS, so that it is 	hidden from man-in-the-middle attacks and is secure.
	This may change throughout the project, but now we have an idea of what 	we want to create.
321. /signin and /register
	We're going to test this using Postman we don't need to worry about the 	front-end now; we want to build our server to be working properly and only 	at the end once everything is working, we can plug it into the front-end 	and we can also let the front-end team know, "Hey these are the 	endpoints that we expect." And this is what we will respond with. So the 	first thing we want to tackle is the 'signin' route.
	If we think about this, we will do app.post() for signin. And when we do the 	signin route we're going to have a request response. And then just to test 	it's working we will do res.send('signin').
	app.post('/signin', ((res, req) => {
		res.send('SignIn');
	})
	Let's save and head over to Postman, input 'localhost:3001/signin', 	change it to 'POST' and hit Send.
	It can be beneficial to work in incremental steps to make sure 	everything is working iteration by iteration, as well as the 	gratification of getting those small wins each time.
	Perfect! Now we will use something that we will continue to use in the 	future, which is .json instead of .send. .send can also send JSON, but 	Express has	a built in JSON method on response that we can use, and it 	has a bit of added features when responding with JSON and we want to 	focus on using that.
	So from now on we will be using res.json(), and there is a slight difference 	in what we receive. We receive a JSON string this way. (signin vs. 	"signin").
	If we think about SignIn, we aren't just going to want to say 'signin', we 	want to check wahatever the user enters on the front-end. and it's going to 	come back as a request, and we want to check it with our current list of 	users and make sure their password matches. 
	From the sounds of this, it would be beneficial to have a database, 	however, since we haven't gotten there yet in the tutorial, we're going to 	create a variable and this is going to be called database. 'database' will 	have a 'users' property, and this property will be an array of objects. This 	array of objects will have an id property with a value of let's say '123', a 	name property, with a value of let's say 'John', and an email property, with 	a value of let's say' john@gmail.com'. It will also have a password 	property, and let's assign it a value of 'cookies', an entries property, which 	refers to how many entries of images they have done for their ranking, 	with a value of let's start with 0, and lastly, a joined property, which will 	have a value of 'new Date(), which comes with JavaScript, and will just 	create the date when this part gets executed.
	Now let's create another user, we'll have an 'id' of '124', it'll be 'Sally', 	'sally@gmail.com' and her password will be 'bananas'.
	Signin is going to get a request, and we will need to access it via 	req.body(), and here it is wise to use a conditional statement. To be done 	soon.
	Now that we have a couple users stored in our database that we can 	reference with our request, let's set up a request in Postman. To do this 	we will make sure we are POSTing to port 3001/signin, and we will pass it 	raw data, in the form of JSON or application/JSON, since we are not using 	an HTML form.
	In the input we can provide a JSON object with an email and password to 	represent a user attempting to login via our front-end.
	{
		"email": "john@gmail.com"
		"password": "apples"
	}
	We can pretend that John is logging in, and access it in our app.post().
	Within our app.post(), we can now write that conditional statement.
	if (req.body.email === database.users[0].email && 
	    req.body.password === database.users[0].password) {
		res.json('Success!');
	} else {
		res.status(400).json('Error logging in.')
	}
	We could loop through our database for this check, but since we are using 	a database variabble we will just access it with bracket notation. If we 	save and Send this JSON request, we will get an error, cannot read 	property of undefined (email). 
	It is important for us to see this error becuase it is very common and 	people forget it all the time. 
	When we're sending data from the front-end using JSON, we need to 	parse it because Express does not know what we are sending.
	This is why we have body-parser.
	const bodyParser = require('body-parser');
	Now that we have bodyParser in our project, we need to use this 	middleware on the data, therefore we do:
	app.use(bodyParser.json());
	Success! And just to check if the other side of things is working, let's 	change the password to 'apple' instead of 'apples'. Now we get 'Error 	loggin in.'
	As mentioned earlier, we could loop through this with a 'for' loop or 	forEach, to check in case Sally tries to login, but that would be a bit of a 	hassle in the case that we had a hundred users and we had to check 	against all of the users and passwords every time a login happened. This 	is why we use databases, they are really good at grabbing and checking 	against different inputs versus a variable or an array here where we have 	to loop through everything.
	Since everything is working nicely for now, let's move to the next step, 	/register.
	app.post('/register', (req, res) => {
		
	})
	This register means that a new user is going to be created, so we won't be 	doing any checks like signin. we want to grab the req.body and enter the 	new information into our database.
	In this case we have a database object that has a user object, that has an 	array, so we can do 'database.users.push() to add to the array.
	It can be wise to start off with what the request is going to look like, so 	going back to our database, when we create a new user, if we remember 	from our Register form, we get a name, email, and password. Knowing 	that, we can head over to Postman and create those.
	We will do a POST to localhost:3001/register, and we will send raw JSON 	with a name, email, and password.
	Going back to our code, we want to create a user like the other users, so 	let's copy all of the properties and values and do:
	database.users.push({
	            id: '123',
	            name: 'John',
	            email: 'john@gmail.com',
	            password: 'apples',
	            entries: 0,
	            joined: new Date(),
	        }
	However, we don't want to add John again, we want to grab whatever we 	get from req.body. Using destructuring, we can simply do:
	{ name, email, password } = req.body;
	This will establish a name, email, and password with the values given by 	the req.body. We can then use those in our JSON push, as well as 	statically changing our id for this example.
	database.users.push({
	            id: '125',
	            name: name,
	            email: email,
	            password: password,
	            entries: 0,
	            joined: new Date(),
	        }
	Entries will stay 0 because a new user will have no entries yet, and joined 	will stay as new Date() so that when this is executed it runs the Date 	method to provide the date joined.
	If we attempt to Send the Postman request, we will get an infinite loading 	loop, well, because we haven't sent a response. Express is just hanging 	there because we haven't returned anything. 
	Always remember to include a response.
	res.json(database.users[database.users.length - 1]);
	BOOOOOOOOOM. It worked!
	We get:
		{
	            "id": "125",
	            "name": Ann",
	            "email": "ann@gmail.com",
	            "password": "oranges",
		"entries": 0,
		"joined": "2025-02-19T00:29:48.794Z"
		}
	Let's go back to our root route and change "This is working :)" to a list of 	our database users.
	res.send(database.users)
	Now if we run a GET on our / root route, we get the array of user objects 	with John and Sally, but where's Ann?
	If we go back to our register POST request on Postman, and run it again, 	and then run our Get on our root route, now we get all three users. But 	why?
	This is another good case for why we need a database. The reason Ann 	wasn't added the first time around is because we changed the root route 	to include 'database.users', which meant Nodemon had to restart, the 	server had to restart. Since there are JavaScript variable every time we 	restart the server, this gets run all over again, so the database initiates 	with just the two users, until we send that request with Ann.
	Again, we dont really use variable to store infortmation that we need to 	'persist', that is to last and be memorized by the system. 
	Databases are really really good because they run on disk somewhere out 	there in the world and they keep this information without going down or 	restarting. If they do go down, they have backups so that the user always 	get added and we don't lose any information.
	We will get to databases and actually implement this with a database in 	the future, but it's important to be aware of the pain points that are present 	without the use of databases.
	We would have to loop through these users if we had to check 'signin' or 	we have to always keep in mind that whenever the server restarts we lose 	all of our data.
322. /profile/:id and /image
	We first have the /profile/:userId, which will be a GET, because we are 	GETting the user for their homepage.
	app.get('/profile/:id')
	This syntax [:id] that means we can enter in our browser anything and 	we'll be able to grab this 'id' through the 'req.params' property.
	app.get('/profile/:id', (req, res) => {
		const { id } = req.params;
	})
	In order to get our users we want to loop through our sample database 	and find the matching 'id', and if it matches, we want to return the user.
	We have to do a 'for' loop here. The way we do that is 	'database.users', and although I like using 'map' for this case, we're just 	looping through the users and we dont really need to create a new array. 
	It's just a database and we want to keep that constant so we'll just say 	'forEach()' and if you remember 'forEach()' will run through the users one 	at a time and we'll say that if 'user.id' which is what will get from that 	database equals the 'id' that we received from the 'params'. we will 	respond with JSON, that user [req.json(user)], otherwise we will respind 	with req.json no such user, and we can even give it a status code of 404 	which means 'Not Found'.
	database.users.forEach(user => {
		if (user.id === id) {
			res.json(user);
		} else {
			res.status(404).json('No such user.');
		}
	})
	If we go to test this out, in Postman, we can run a GET to 	localhost:3000/profile/123, and it will return John, whose ID is 123. 	However if we do 124, we will see that Sally isn't there and we get 'No 	such user.
	If we troubleshoot, we will see there's an error, 'Can't set headers after 	they are sent.'
	This is because we're looping through the database, and instead of 	returning from the loop, we just continue looping. So add:
	return res.json(user)
	And then if the user isn't found, we don't necessarily want to do that in the 	loop, so we can create a variable named found, and give it a true or false 	value depending on if the ID was found.
	let found = false;
	database.users.forEach(user => {
		if (user.id === id) {
			found = true;
			return res.json(user);
		}
		if (!found) {
			res.status(400).json('Not Found')
	})
	Now if we test this out, we can get both John and Sally, and even Ann if 	we run the register form prior.
	So we have this endpoint working and we have one last one which is the 	image and this one we just wanted to update the user to increase their 	'entries' count.
	Every time they submit an image we want to increase their 'entries'. Let's 	do that by creating our final endpoint which is 'app.put()' and we'll call it 	'/image'. We'll get a request, response. 
	app.put('/image', (req, res) => {
		
	})
	And again here we will have to find the 'id' of the user again. To 	update their entries. In this case we can just copy what we've done 	before, which it would be a good idea to refactor into a function for 	reusability, but for now we will just copy/paste.
	app.put('/image', (req, res) => {
	const { id } = req.body;
		let found = false;
		database.users.forEach(user => {
			if (user.id === id) {
				found = true;
				user.entries++
				return res.json(user.entries);
			}
			if (!found) {
				res.status(400).json('Not Found')
		})
	})
	Instead of actually having 'params' in this case we will receive the user's 	'id' from the body. We'll say that if the user's 'id' matches, in that case we 	will respond with the 'user.entries' and this 'entries' we want to actually 	increase. So we'll say 'user.entries++'.
	Now if we want to test this out in Postman, we can do a PUT on 	localhost:3000/image, and in the body, we can pass the users id.
	{
		"id": "123"
	}
	This gives us the response "1", and if we send it again, we get "2" and so 	on. 
	If I go back to the 'root' which returns our users and change that to 	GET I 	get John with two entries and Sally with zero entries. Let's make sure it 	works for Sally as well. If I do "124" I get now entries of "1" and going 	back to the GET users I get John with five entries and Sally with one 	 	entry.
	We got ourselves a nice API server! It can definitely improve. There is a 	few things that we obviously want to change such as 'signin', to not just 	have it for John. 
	We have a few repetition of code that perhaps we can extract this out into 	a function. But I wanted us to have something working something that we 	can use so that we can connect it immediately with our front-end.
	323. Quick Note: Bcrypt
	In the next video we're going to talk about this package called Bcrypt-	nodejs. Now if you go to this page now in NPM you'll get a little warning 	here saying that this package has been deprecated and that they 	recommend you use something else. You either use Bcrypt or bcrypt.js.
	The difference being, bcrypt is in C++, however, since this is our own 	personal project, we're going to just bcrypt-nodejs still, because this one 	was created specifically so it works on Mac, Linux, and Windows.
	The other packages are based on whatever your operating system and  	you may encounter some errors. So if you want you can use those 	packages, especially when you have a real life app that stores user 	passwords.
324. Storing User Passwords
	Security is VERY VERY important on the web. As developers, it is our 	responsibility to ensure whatever user information we handle, we do so 	 	with care and in a secure fashion.
	Up until now, we have been shown a simple signin process, however we 	haven't seen a secure way of doing it. This video will show us how to do 	just that, using bcrypt-nodejs.
	Bcrypt is a library that is very very powerful, in that it allows us to create a 	very secure login.
	To this point, we have had 'password' as a property, and the value in 	simple, plain text. In real life, we are NEVER EVER storing passwords as 	plain texts, like a string, in our database. This is how companies get 	hacked and passwords get released of users.
	What we want to do, is store passwords in something called 'hashes'. We 	will get to thatshortly but for now, we are going to reformate our 'database' 	to emulate how things will more realistically be stored.
	We will delete our password key value pair, and after our users: [] array, 	we will add a login: [] array.
	const database = {
		users: [
			{
				id: '123',
				name: 'John',
				email: 'john@gmail.com',
				entries: 0,
				joined: new Date(),
			},
			{
				id: '124',
				name: 'Sally',
				email: 'sally@gmail.com',
				entries: 0,
				joined: new Date(),
			},
		],
		login: [
			{
				id: '987',
				hash: '',
				email: 'john@gmail.com'
			}
		]
	}
	In order for us to handle the sensitive information, that is, if John let's say 	enters our site and wants to do a Sign In he's going to give us his email 	plus his password, which is 'cookies'. Now, he is trusting us with this 	information.
	We're definitely not going to add it as a query string in a GET request, 	we're going to add it to a POST request. So it's in the body of the request.
	We also wanted to send it over HTTPS. That is something that requires a 	certificate. And we're not going to get into that but we definitely want to 	include it through an HTTPS request so that it's encrypted.
	When he sends 'cookies' it's going to get jumbled up and nobody in the 	middle can see this, or they'll be able to see it but they won't understand 	what it means. 
	Only the server once it receives this password can actually understand 	that this stands for 'cookies'.
	So one is HTTPS. The second thing is, how do we store that password? 	Well we store it in a hash, and bcrypt, the package that I was just talking 	about, allows us to do this.
	Bcrypt gives us a way to hash a password and a way to compare 	passwords or hashes.
	bcrypt.hash("bacon", null, null, function(err, hash) {
		// Store hash in your password DB.
	});
	// Load hash from your password DB.
	bcrypt.compare("bacon", hash, function(err, res) {
		// res == true
	});
	bcrypt.compare("veggies", hash, function(err, res) {
		// res = false
	});
	If we use the first function to store the hash, we can put it in our /register, 	since that is where we first give our password, and instead of 'bacon' this 	is where we pass the user's password, and we have also have a couple of 	parameters that they give us nd these are options that we can enter, but 	for now we'll just leave it the way it is. We can console.log(hash) which is 	the hash that we recieve in the function, so bcrypt is going to hash the 	password and then return this hash.
	app.post('/register', (req, res) => {
		const { name, email, password } = req.body;
		bcrypt.hash(password, null, null, function(err, hash) {
			console.log(hash);
		})
	});
	If we remember to import bcrypt first, and then head over to Postman to 	check it out, we can register a user, and when that happens, if we check 	our terminal, voila! We have a hash string!
	So that is what a hash function does; it takes a string, and jumbles it up 	into something that we cannot logically interpret as the original string. I.e.
	oranges => $2a$10$LMgmTXfuNtqlFq4gIY6yf.LF1tSSiXzuQIEX68Asok5e4sp7ew5Dm
	Another interesting quality about hash functions, are that they are one-	way. Meaning if you enter oranges and get the return hash, you cannot 	convert it back. 
	If we send the same string again, we see that the hash value is different 	the second time versus the first.
	Although hash functions always return the same thing for the same 	value, bcrypt is more secure and adds another element that makes it 	'impossible' to ever find out what the original password is.
	Now we can store this hash in our database for the login and anytime we 	sign in, we want to check if the user's passwords match. 
	Let's go to our /signin, and at the top, we can use the compare hash 	function. For an example, let's grab the actual hash that we received and 	replace 'hash' with it, and change the password argument to our actual 	password, 'oranges'.
	app.post('/signin', (req, res) => {
		bcrypt.compare("oranges", '$2a$1
		$LMgmTXfuNtqlFq4gIY6yf.LF1tS
		SiXzuQIEX68Asok5e4sp7ew5Dm',
		function(err, res) {
			console.log('First guess', res)
		});
	In the following function we can test the falseness as 	well by using an 	incorrect password.
	bcrypt.compare("veggies", hash, function(err, res) {
		console.log('Second guess', res)
	});
	However if we try to run a signin we get an error saying hash is not 	defined, so we need to replace the 'hash' with the hash string from the first 	function.
	Now if we run a signin, it doesnt matter what since we are sending static 	values of 'oranges' for true with the 'hash' string, and 'veggies' as false, we 	get in our console, First guess true, Second guess false!
	In this way, bcrypt is very powerful, and we will use it when we implement 	an actual database to our registration. 
	Using bcrypt, we can safely store users' information, users' passwords, 	into our database and even if someone hacks our database, they will have 	a very hard time getting our passwords. They may be able to get our email 	addresses, but not our passwords.
	ALWAYS send any sensitive information from the front-end to the 	back-end using HTTPS in a POST body, and if you get something like 	a password, the way you store it into the database is using 	something like bcrypt to generate a hash, and every time a user 	signs in, check that has with whatever the user input.
325. Resource: Storing User Passwords Securely
	This is an update of a technical post I wrote for a tech company (Rangle). 	It is completely optional, but it discusses proper ways that companies 	should store user passwords:
	Cybersecurity got ever more attention lately as all industries realized how 	important it is to have strong protection of their computer systems. At the 	top of the list of security issues are passwords. Many people believe it is 	the weakest link in internet security (some believe it is permanently 	broken, https://www.wired.com/2012/11/ff-mat-honan-password-hacker/).
	In 2016, Yahoo admitted to having over 1 billion account credentials 	stolen, and LinkedIn announced 117 million accounts had been 	compromised in previous years due to weak password management on 	their part.
	This tutorial will cover the user experience from the front end, all the way 	to the database. Let’s get started.
	Step 1:
	It all begins when you ask your user to create an account by setting a 	username and password. This is where user experience will need to play 	nicely with proper security measures. United States National Institute for 	Standards and Technology (NIST) is currently finalizing their 	recommendations for password management. Some of these include:
	Your minimum password length should be at least 8 characters, and the 	maximum as large are 64 characters for the user. The longer the 	password that the user sets, the better. This will also play nicely if said 	user is using password managers like 1Password since their generated 	password will be long and fit this criteria. There is nothing worse than the 	password limit being too small for a password generated by 1Password.
	Do accept both ASCII and UNICODE characters and don't set rules about 	which characters should and shouldn’t be included (P@ssword1 is NOT a 	good password). Instead, encourage people to set long passwords with 	high entropy (upper case letters, lower case letters, digits, special 	characters).
	Don’t allow password hints.
	Avoid security questions. Although this is good for an extra layer of 	security, the information can be easily discovered by an attacker in this 	day and age. It’s just an extra implementation step; a step that a user has 	to take while offering very little added security. Yahoo had this information 	stolen-- which was saved in plain text, allowing the attacker to easily see 	this information about each user.
	Use 2FA (2 factor authentication) if you want an extra layer of security in 	your application, but avoid using SMS as this can be easily hacked to 	have the attackers phone receive the confirmation code.
	Once the user has registered, there are two things to keep in mind:
	Don’t let the user know the password was incorrect when logging in. 	Instead, mention that the username and password combination is 	incorrect. You want to minimize information that an attacker can get, and 	the less they know (such as that the user exists), the better.
	The only time passwords should be reset by an administrator is when they 	suspect that an account has been compromised. Otherwise, passwords 	should only be reset by a user when he or she has forgotten their 	credentials.
	Step 2:
	Now that the user has entered a good password, they will submit the form 	and trust you to take care of their credentials. In the second step, you are 	transmitting the form (account sign up) data in a POST body to your 	server. You do it this way for a few reasons:
	POST requests are never cached
	POST request will not remain in the browser history
	No restriction on data length
	Most importantly, use HTTPS so that this data will be encrypted and 	cannot be attacked by someone who is observing your network traffic. 	HTTPS is the regular HTTP protocol with SSL/TLS encryption which 	means that only the server can read what you send it, and only you can 	read what the server sends back. To use HTTPS, simply purchase an 	SSL certificate and follow the steps in this article:
	https://www.keycdn.com/blog/http-to-https/.
	Step 3:
	Now that we have received the username and password to our server it is 	time to do a few things. Before we get started though, always remember 	to never store passwords in plaintext. Instead, you will want to use some 	of these popular npm packages to hash the password: argon2, scrypt, or 	bcrypt. We prefer bcrypt for three reasons:  1. bcrypt is 15 years old and 	has been vetted by the crypto community. Although argon2 won last 	year’s password hashing competition, it is still fairly new and we would like 	to see it have a longer lifespan in the crypto community.  2. scrypt is 7 	years old and also a good choice, but bcrypt is a bit easier to implement in 	a node.js server with simpler documentation as you will see below in the 	example.  3. The bcrypt npm package is better over other bcrypt 	implementations available on npm since it is native, highly popular, and 	vetted by the community without trying to reinvent the wheel.
	Now you might be asking yourself, why not just use a hashing function like 	SHA256, add a salt (randomly generated bytes to place in front of the 	password) for each user, and store those in a database? The problem 	with computing power increasing is that attackers can now use GPUs to 	try out passwords at over 100 million per second and see if they get a hit. 	That’s why you want to use hash functions that were specifically designed 	to be slow. Although it is fast enough so the user won’t notice (about 	100ms), it is long enough to make it infeasible for an attacker to try out a 	long list of passwords. bcrypt allows you to add a saltRound (10 is the 	recommended value) which iterates 2^10, or 1024 times over the 	password in a process called key stretching. Finally, bcrypt 	implementation is also safe from timing attacks. Here is a simple overview 	of how to use bcrypt for password management. (you can check out their 	repo for more detail):
	$ npm install bcrypt
	/*
	* You can copy and run the code below to play around with bcrypt
	* However this is for demonstration purposes only. Use these concepts
	* to adapt to your own project needs.
	*/
	import bcrypt from'bcrypt'
	const saltRounds = 10 // increase this if you want more iterations  
	const userPassword = 'supersecretpassword'  
	const randomPassword = 'fakepassword'
	const storeUserPassword = (password, salt) =>  
	bcrypt.hash(password, salt).then(storeHashInDatabase)
	const storeHashInDatabase = (hash) => {  
		// Store the hash in your password DB
		return hash // For now we are returning the hash for testing at the 			bottom
	}
	// Returns true if user password is correct, returns false otherwise
	const checkUserPassword = (enteredPassword, storedPasswordHash) =>  
	bcrypt.compare(enteredPassword, storedPasswordHash)
	// This is for demonstration purposes only.
	storeUserPassword(userPassword, saltRounds)  
	.then(hash =>
	// change param userPassword to randomPassword to get false
	checkUserPassword(userPassword, hash)
	)
	.then(console.log)
	.catch(console.error)
	That’s it! You can now rest assured that you have stored the user 	password securely. The interesting thing with security is that it is always a 	cat and mouse game. Security advice changes with the time as new 	exploits are discovered and computing power increases. New attacks will 	be discovered but so will new solutions. Remember, the best security for 	your application means constantly keeping up with vulnerabilities, 	software updates, and new tools. For now, you can breathe easy…
326. Connecting To Our Front-End
	Let's start both of our servers. It's important to think about these as 	separate computers because in a real life application, these would most 	likely both be two separate computers somwhere, running separately, and 	communicating through HTTP.
	If you try to run both on the same port, the second one will prompt you to 	run the app on another port instead, so let's run our React app on 3001, 	and our Express server on 3000.
	To finally connect our front-end and back-end, let's start on our front-end 	with our React app. We know in order to access our back-end, or the 	outside world, we can use fetch. In our case, if we start under our 	constructor function, we can use a life-cycle hook, componentDidMount. 	This comes with React if we remember.
	componentDidMount() {
		fetch('http://localhost:3000/')
			.then(response => response.json())
			.then(data => console.log(data))
	}
	We can actually shorten the final .then if we simply do:
	.then(console.log)
	So let's try this out and see if this works. If we remember, we do the base 	root in our server, and the route is just getting the users from the database 
	(res.send(database.users);)
	If we go to our localhost:3000 and refresh, we get an error: 
	Access to fetch at 'http://localhost:3001/' from origin 'http://localhost:3000' 	has been blocked by CORS policy: No 'Access-Control-Allow-Origin' 	header is present on the requested resource. If an opaque response 	serves your needs, set the request's mode to 'no-cors' to fetch the 	resource with CORS disabled.
	This error is a security feature. We get this error ecause what we're trying 	to do right now is communicate with the outside world using HTTP. But 	our browser doesn't really trust whatever is trying to access it, becuase 	they have no way of knowing that our server is secure. It could be a 	hacker secretly trying to make a request from your web page to their web 	site that downloads some bad software onto our page. 
	This is called Access Control. Access-Control-Allow-Origin to be exact. 	For us to test this out on localhost, we need to do something called 	CORS.
	What we want to do then, is install an NPM package called cors. Just like 	body-parser, you will see it very commonly out in the 'wild'. If we look at 	the npmjs site, we can see that it is a middleware, that simply requires an 	app.use.
	So let's install CORS.
	npm install cors
	Then use it right underneath our body-parser middleware:
	app.use(bodyParser.json());
	app.use(cors())
	Now if we refresh our React app on localhost:3000, and check the 	console, we have an array of both of our users in our database logged 	out! However we may notice we're receiving two iterations of that array. 	This is due to React's developer environment, and we can bypass this by 	simply waiting until prod, or by disabling StrictMode. However, we will just 	move forward as such for now, and revisit this if it becomes an issue down 	the line.
	Now let's work on the /signin route, where we're going to send the 	information from the front-end, to the back-end.
	This component has two inputs, the email and the password, that when 	we click Sign In, we will have to send through the request body. We will 	have to send it to the server, and the server will check if the user exists, 	and then give us a response. 
	We can remove componentDidMount since we no longer need it, and 	although we create our fetch function in the app.js, because Signin is it's 	own little component, and the rest of the app after signin doesn't care if it 	succeeded or failed, it's good to keep the functionality inside the signin 	component. To do that we want to turn the signin component, into a smart 	component, meaning it has a state.
	Let's convert this into a class 'Signin' which extends React.Component. 	And this will have a render method, that receives, all of the Signin's 	original return function.
	class Signin extends React.Component {
		render() {
		<article>
			...
		</article>
	}
	We also receive some props, which was onRouteChange, so we can 	destructure our props.
	render() {
		const { onRouteChange } = this.props;
	Now we need to get the input values from our Email input and our 	Password input, so we can create event listeners.
	onEmailChange = (event) => {
		this.setState({signInEmail: event.target.value})
	}
	onPasswordChange = (event) => {
		this.setState({signInPassword: event.target.value})
	}
	These event listeners will update the state of signInEmail and 	signInPassword. However, we need to create, or construct, this state!
	constructor(props) {
		super(props);
		this.state = {
			signInEmail: "",
			signInPassword: ""
		}
	}
	In order to use the props, properties, of our onRouteChange, we need to 	pass them into our state. Note the edited bold parts.
	If this is working the way we intend it to, we can now create an 	onSubmitSignIn event listener which will eventually fetch data from our 	server, but for now we will just console.log the current state.
	onSubmitSignIn = () => {
		console.log(this.state);
	}
	Can we really have more than two smart components? Our app has 	states 	in SignIn, then app.js has state also. Yeah, absolutely. You want to organize 	your app in a way that makes sense. For example, sign in is its own 	enclosed thing. So using signIn, even though it's a child of app, it can have 	it's own state.
	As long as this state is only concerned with signIn, then it is a good way to do 	this. Pure state if you will. As opposed to constantly updating app.js and 	making it grow to many many lines of code.
	Let's see if this logs correctly. Before we do that, we need to assign our 	onSubmitSignIn event listener to our Sign In button onClick event. By doing 	this, it will run our onSubmitSignIn function/event listener, meaning we will 	now need to put our route change function and it's route, inside this function.
	onClick={this.onSubmitSignIn}
	onSubmitSignIn = () => {
		console.log(this.state);
		this.props.onRouteChange('home');
	}
	Now if we enter some test inputs and click submit, well, we get our state 	object in the console, however, the values are not populated. That is 	because we created the onSignInChange and onPasswordChange 	functions, but we haven't added them to the event.
	So let's go to the respective email and password inputs, and add an 	onChange attribute.
	onChange={this.onEmailChange}
	onChange={this.onPasswordChange}
	It works! Now we have the information, and we can send it to our server. 	We can do that by running a fetch function to our server.
	However fetch() by default does a GET request, but what we want her is a 	POST request.
	onSubmitSignIn = () => {
		fetch('http://localhost:3000/signin')
		this.props.onRouteChange('home');
	}
	The way we do that is within the second parameter of the fetch function. 	We can pass an object here that describes the request we want. In our 	case we'll have a method, which will be POST, and we'll attach headers, 	and headers will be an object that defines the content, which will be JSON, 	and finally, we will add the body, which will contain the JSON stringified 	object of the actual content we want to pass. 
	onSubmitSignIn = () => {
		fetch('http://localhost:3000/signin', {
			method: 'post',
			headers: {'Content-Type': 'application/json'},
			body: JSON.stringify({
				email: this.state.signInEmail,
				password: this.state.signInPassword
			})
		})
		this.props.onRouteChange('home');
	}
	It works! If we enter john@gmail.com into the email input, and apples into 	the password input, we get success in our network response, and no 	errors :), however if we input the wrong user or pass, it gives us a 400 bad 	request, and a network response of Error logging in.
	Even with these proper responses however, our route is still changing on 	the event which in turn takes us to the 'logged in' page. We want to add a 	condition to execute the route change only upon 'Success!' as the 	response from the network/server.
	To do this, we can do a .then on our fetch request (which is a promise 	being 	returned, therefore allowin us the .then method.) First though, we need to 	convert the data to a workable data type, by doing a .json() on the response.
	.then(response => response.json())
	.then(data => {
		if (data === 'Success!') {
			this.props.onRouteChange('home');
		}
	}
328. Registering Users
	We have a pretty good framework for what this should look like based on 	our Signin page, so we can simply convert our register.js file to the format 	we have for signin.js by following the steps above.
	After we convert it from a const to a class, and add our constructor 	function as well as our email change and password change functions, we 	can also change our 'signInEmail' and password, to simply 'Email' and 	'Password', and we can also add a 'Name' property with an empty string 	as the value.
	this.state = {
		name: '',
		email: '',
		password: ''
	}
	Now we can add another method for our on name change. And also 	change the name of the objects passed into our state.
	onEmailChange = (event) => {
		this.setState({email: event.target.value})
	}
	onPasswordChange = (event) => {
		this.setState({password: event.target.value})
	}
	onNameChange = (event) => {
		this.setState({name: event.target.value})
	}
	The first thing we want to do is we want to use destructuring to get our 	component or 'onRouteChange' from 'this.props' and let's add 	'onXChange' 	on all the other inputs.
	onChange={this.onNameChange}, etc...
	We can then change our onClick event in our register button to execute 	this.onSubmitRegister, after we copy our signin function and rename the 	signins to registers, and also after we update the JSON body to update 	the state name, email, and password.
	body: JSON.stringify({
		name: this.state.name,
		email: this.state.email,
		password: this.state.password
	})
	We can now get rid of the destructured onRouteChange since we have it 	in our onSubmitRegister function.
	And within this function, when we execute onRouteChange, we want to go 	o 'home', since we're now registered. If we remember on our server, 	/register' returns the last user, which means the 'data' in our response.json 	ithin our .then on our onSumitRegister fetch, will be a 'user'.
	.then(user => {
		if (user) {
			this.onRouteChange('home');
		}
	)
	So if we get a user back we will route-change to 'home' and we also 	probably want to update the user profile on the front-end.
	If we look at the front-end however, we don't have a user profile yet, but 	maybe we should. Let's add it.
	It will be an object 'user' that contains an email, id, name, and all the 	properties of a user in our 'database', except the password, all as empty 	strings or 0 integers.
	,
	user: {
		id: '',
		name: '',
		email: '',
		entries: 0,
		joined: ''
	}
	Ideally when we register we can update these.
	So within our register submission, we can also call a function, and name it 	something to the degree of updateUser, or loadUser, and pass it the user 	object.
	Since it's something that the entire app needs, we can build it in the App 	component, meaning we need to add this.props.
	if (user) {
		this.props.loadUser(user);
		this.props.onRouteChange('home');
	}
	Now let's go to App.js and create the function, which will update the state 	(this.setState) and update it with the user object and it's properties and 	values. And all these values, will come from the user parameter, so lets 	change the name so we don't get confused to 'data' from 'user'.
	loadUser = (data) => {
		this.setState({user: {
			id: data.id,
			name: data.name,
			email: data.email,
			entries: data.entries,
			joined: data.joined
		}})
	}
	That was a lot of work without testing the code so let's check it out.
	We get the error 'loadUser is not a function' and that is because we never 	pass 'loadUser' into 'Register', so let's do that in our App.js.
	<Register loadUser={this.loadUser} 	onRouteChange={this.onRouteChange}/>
	Let's try it! No errors. Let's see what the response was of 'register'. We get 	the response; Wes is user '125'; 'password' is '123'; 'entries' is 0', and 	'joined' at this time.
	But again, we don't really want the password, so we can simply remove 	that from the server.js response.
	If we go to the localhost root route; we've refreshed again so an extra user 	is not there but if you register again... We have our user!
	Now there's just a bit of repetition right? We just pretty much fetch in order	to connect with our server and both parties want to make sure that they 	send the right data and the server responds with the right data. 
	There's definitely a lot of cleanup to do in terms of errors that could 	happen, refactoring repeated code and so on, but we are starting to get 	the point.

330. User Profile Update
	If we look at /profile/:id, it isn't really useful for going home, because once 	we sign in we have our user information, but maybe it will be useful for 	use to grab our profile and update our name or our email. That's 	something we can incorporate in the future, but we want to focus on our 	last route /image, which allows us to incrememnt the number of our 	entries.
	Every time we submit on the front-end we want the user to hit this route so 	that we can increase their entries count. Let's give that a go.
	Firstly, we will be changing onButtonSubmit to onPictureSubmit, in our 	app.js as well as our ImageLinkForm.js.
	Next, if we look at our app.js and see our ClarifaiAPI call, that's when we 	do onPictureSubmit, and this is specific to our homescreen. What we 	want 	to do is if Clarifai gives us a response, in that case, on top of displaying and 	calculating, as long as we have a response, we want to fetch the 	localhost/image route.
	.then(response => {
		if (response) {
			fetch('http://localhost/3000/image')
		}
		this.displayFaceBox(this.calculateFaceLocation(response))
	})
	This is a POST request but we really want it to be app.put because we are 	updating. Either will work, but this is a way to keep up with the standard. 	Now instead of method post like in our onSubmitSignIn, we will actually 	have method put.
	fetch('http://localhost/3001/image', {
		method: 'put',
		headers: {'Content-Type': 'application/json'},
		body: JSON.stringify({
			id: this.state.user.id
		})
	})
	And since we don't actually need anything from the response but the id, 	we will do that instead.
	Now let's save and see if that works. Back to our app, sign in as John, 	find an image, and submit it. We get the face, and the box, however our 	current rank isn't updated, and we get a console error: 
	PUT http://localhost:3000/image net::ERR_ABORTED 404 (Not Found)
	Let's ignore the error for now, presuming it may be resolved when we 	execute a response with the .then, updating our entries count.
	.then(response => response.json())
	.then(count => {
		this.setState({users: {
			entries: count
		}})
	})
	After adding this portion, we realize that our server API is running on 	localhost:3001, not 3000, so this fixes the error, and if we inspect our 	image 	call in our network tab as we click 'detect', we get in our payload {123} which 	is our ID, and if we look at the response, we get 1, or 2, or however many 	entries we have now done.
	First thing we want to do now to enable the changing of the rank on our 	front-end, is to correct our setState which updates users, when it should 	be user.
	Now if we click 'detect' our rank increments by one! However, our user 	changes to undefined, why is that?
	Well if we look at our code, we can see that we are updating the entire 	state object, which we dont want. We want the user to stay the same, we 	just want to update the entries of the user.
	The way to get around this is by using something called Object.assign.
	This is part of the JavaScript spec. The first parameter is the target object, 	in our case this.state.user, and the second parameter is what you want to 	extend it with, in our case, entries: count.
	.then(count => {
		this.setState(Object.assign(this.state.user, { entries: count }))
	})
	Babushka! It works!
	So we can sign out. We can register. We can sign in. We have our end 	points, and using this we can imagine how we can expand our app.
	We can add a DELETE for example to '/profile/:id' to delete your account. 	We can add a PUT method to update your account.
	There's many many things that we can do but now we have seen the power of 	the server and the front-end finally connecting through 'fetch' to create some 	real functionality.
Section 27: Databases
332. Introduction To Databases
	All companies store data. From a simple record book of a small business, 	maybe even on a journal or a piece of paper, to massive companies like 	Amazon that need computers to store Petabytes of data. Now in order to 	store this information pretty much every company and sometimes 	individuals too need something called a database.
	We saw in the last section how hard it was to have our server remember a 	new user. Every time the server crashed. The variable would get reset 	and then it would start all over. Or if we wanted to do a login or a signin 	check and iterate through the e-mails and passwords, doing a loop 	through javascript that's inefficient. That's where database come to the 	rescue.
	A database is a collection of data.
	There can be numbers, there can be dates, there could be password 	hashes, there could be user information. Databases allow us to organize 	this data in a way that is useful to us and it makes data management 	easy. 	The tools built to do this are called database management systems or 	DBMS for short.
	It's a collection of programs which allows us to access databases and 	work with data. And it also allows controlled access to database users.
	We can communicate with the database, store information that's useful for 	us and also allows us to update, insert, delete, look up whatever's in the 	database.
	This idea of a DBMS really started to take shape in 1960s.
	There are two types of DBMS that are really popular right now, these two 	types are represented really well by Postgres and MongoDB.
The first type, are relational databases, and these are probably the most popular.
 
	Relational databases consist of two or more tables with columns 	and rows. So in this case users is a table and full_name, 	username, text, created_at are columns and whatever values they 	have here are rows. Each row represents an entry and each 	column stores a very specific type of information like name, 	address or phone numbers. And then the relation between tables 	and field is called a schema.
	In a relational database the schema must be clearly defined before 	any information can be added.
	Twitter for example you can see the types of tables that we would 	have and how we would organize it. We would have a users table, 	a tweets table, a following table and we can connect all this 	information, for example, by connecting different pieces of the 	table.
	For example the username will be the same in the users table as it 	is in the tweets table. That way we can say that the username in 	the tweets table is the foreign key of the username in the users 	table. And then from_user will be the foreign key of username in 	the following table. Now things like full name or id, something that 	identifies each row in a table is called a primary key.
	So how do they actually communicate with the server with the 	Backend. Well all relational databases use something called SQL. 	And SQL allows us to communicate just like HTTP did between the 	frontend and the backend. 
	With SQL we're able to communicate with the database and modify 	or get or update however we want.
	The second type of DBMS's are called non-relational databases or 	NoSQL databases.
	
 
	A non-relational database lets you build an application without 	having to define the schema first unlike a relational database.
	With relational databases, the tables have to be kind of predefined, 	we need to know how our app is going to look, to make these 	tables, so that once the app is public we can start entering user 	information and tweets and followers.
	With a non-relational database, we can just define it as we go.
	They each have a different way of storing the information, so with 	that, they are very very different.
	This is another powerful reason to use a non relational database is 	that, if your data requirements aren't clear at the outset of your 	project and maybe you have a massive amount of unstructured 	data. You may not have the luxury of developing a relational 	database with a clearly defined schema. They offer instead a 	greater flexibility in that a non relational database are more like 	folders, just assembling related information of all types.
	MongoDB is something called document oriented. It stores 	information as documents.
	To illustrate the difference of the two types:
	
	 
	You can think of relational databases with this type of storage 	where this folder lives somewhere on a computer that stores this 	information. 
	Any time we need to ask a database for something, we grab the 	users and if we also want to grab the tweets of the user. We find 	the user that we're interested in. Then find the tweets of the user 	that we're also interested in with the foreign key that links to the 	users and we can also find however many followers that user has. 	We grab that information and display it on our web app.
		 
	MongoDB on the other hand will actually have each user as a 	document. So if I wanted to grab the first user. Well I just grabbed 	this document and it has all the followers, the tweets, the profile 	everything is in this one document.
	We can start to see the difference each has and why one may be 	useful for certain usecases and another for different cases. 
	E.g. 
	You can see this MongoDB way being very useful if you have 	something like a profile maybe a linkedin profile. 
	But if you needed something where you're working with just 	following data or just tweets data, maybe with the tweets you want 	to see what is the average tweet size. Well it's really easy to just 	grab this file and calculate that versus this way where you might 	have to extract it from each one of the users.
 
	You can think of relational as each table has a Blog Posts, a Blog 	Tag, a Blog Comments and it links these tables to give you the blog 	information versus a non-relational that stores this entire block post 	in an entry and has comments, tags, categories and all other 	related data, just in one single place.
	If we recall, MongoDB is of the type, NoSQL databases, so what 	does it use to communicate? 
	MongoDB has its own what we call query language and it is just 	the MongoDB query language.
	They both aim to do the same thing, which is to communicate with 	the database, and provide an easy way for the server to 	communicate with the database.
333. Installing PostgreSQL
	We're going to start setting up Postgres on our computer so that 	just like we did with servers in Front End we're going to have a 	terminal that's running our front end and a Back End that is running 	our server, but also another terminal that is running a database 	because as you remember these are just computers running 	somewhere. In the same way that we ran our server on our 	computer. We can also run our database on our computer.
	MongoDB/NoSQL has gotten popular, however nothing beats a 	relational database for really developing your knowledge of 	databases.
	Once you understand relational databases it gives you really really 	good tools and mental models to work with other databases and 	database types.
	MongoDB is very, very simple. You can think of it as a JSON data 	structure where we just have JSON that looks like an object and we 	store that information there. With SQL and relational databases it's 	more complex than that.
	So let's get Postgres set up on our computer. The first thing we 	want to do is download something called a GUI, or graphical user 	interface for Postgres.
	This exists for pretty much any database out there. I like PSequel 	because it's free, but it is Mac only.
	Once you have a PostgreSQL GUI installed you'll have it in your 	application folder and you can follow along.
	Now let's go back to the terminal. In order to have Postgres 	installed - there's many, many ways. 
	The best way especially on Mac is to use Brew which we've already 	talked about. Every time you do something with homebrew, again 	it's a way to download packages from the Internet on your 	terminal. You always want to make sure that you do a brew update 	to make sure that everything is updated. And also a brew doctor 	which just makes sure everything is working on your system.
	Once you have that set up we're going to just say 'brew install 	postgresql' and this will install for us Postgres.
	brew install postgresql
	To start Postgres. You need to do 'brew services start postgresql' 	and don't worry this isn't Postgres specific. Any database that you 	are downloading you're essentially downloading the software and 	then you want to start the service. Each one has its own 	commands 	but it's as simple as Googling and figuring out how to run the 	database.
	To stop it we simply use 'brew services stop postgresql'.
	brew services start postgresql
	In order for us to use Postgres we need to create a database. And 	the way we do that is to use the 'Createdb' command that has 	come 	once we've installed Postgres. And here we can call it whatever we 	want. So let's call it 'test'.
	createdb 'test'
	If we open up our SQL GUI, we can see it hasks for the host, which 	since we just installed and created it on our local machine, would 	be localhost. The port number is default 5432.
	When I did the 'Start postgresql' and 'createdb' the default of '5432' 	was already set. I can actually set what ports and change it up. But 	we want to keep everything standard so we'll keep it the way it is.
	It also asks for the database, which we named test, and then we 	can hit connect.
	We are now connected to the database, which doesn't currently 	have anything in it, but we are connected.
	In order to do the same thing we just did with the GUI, in our 	terminal, we can type in 'psql' again which is a command that we'll 	get when we install Postgres and we'll say which database we want 	to connect to. In our case *it will be the 'test'.
	psql 'test'
	Just like when we run node and it changes terminals. Now we're 	inside of Postgres that's running on our computer.

414. Install PostgreSQL for Windows
	Navigate to the Windows download page on the PostgreSQL 	website.
	https://www.postgresql.org/download/windows/
	Click download the installer:
	https://www.enterprisedb.com/downloads/postgres-postgresql-downloads
	Then select the version and OS you would like the download for.
	Once downloaded, execute the installer, select Next for each step of 	the process:
	 Welcome Setup -> Installation Directory (default 	Program Files) -> 	Select Components (default all; PostgreSQL Server, pgAdmin 4, 	Stack Builder, Command Line Tools) -> Data Directory (default 	PostgreSQL\17\data) -> Database Superuser Password 	(P0stgr3s!) -> Port (default 5432) -> Advanced Options - Database 	Cluster Locale (Default [Default locale]) -> Pre-Installation 	Summary -> Ready To Install
	Once installation is complete you will be prompted to launch Stack 	Builder at exit. Deselect this checkbox, and select Finish.
	We are finished, however we do not have the ability to use commands 	in our command line/terminal (e.g. psql).
	This is because we do not have our environment variables set for our 	Windows system.
	To enable PostgreSQL commands in our command line, we will need 	to add the PostgreSQL/..bin folder to our environmental variable 	path's.
	C:\Program Files\PostgreSQL\17\bin
	Hit the Start key, and navigate to 'Edit the system environment 	variables', then select 'Environment Variables...', select the 'Path 	Variable' in the table of 'System variables' (once selected it will be 	highlighted blue), then select Edit..., after the 'Edit environment 	variable' window is open, select New, and paste or input the \bin 	folder location, then select Ok, Ok, Ok.
	Now if we try to use the 'psql' command, we will be prompted for a 	password for user (windows user), however we did not set this.
	To work around this, we can use an addition flag/parameter with our 	command:
	psql -U postgres
	This will specify we are using postgres and to use the postgres user, 	which we did set a password for. Enter the password here:
	P0stgr3s!
	The password doesn't get shown with stars or any of this kind of 	feedback, which is a common thing in the terminal area. You don't get 	feedback for security reasons. However, it is inputted.
	We are now in postgres in our command line/terminal.
415. Using PSQL and PGADMIN
	Use Command Prompt for Windows, Not Bash.
	Now that we are in postgres, we can learn how to use PSQL and 	PGADMIN. Postgres ships with a few command line helpers, like 	createdb, however we will need to add some flags/parameters 	because postgres is the only one we have a password for right now. 	Then our last paramter will be the name we would like to assign to our 	database.
	createdb -U postgres test
	You will then be prompted for the password. After entering, it will 	create the database.
	We can check that it worked by accessing the database. We can do 	this with the command:
	psql -U postgres test
	Once again we are prompted with the password, and upon entering, 	we will be accessing the 'test' database. This is currently empty, so 	let's create a table.
	CREATE TABLE users (id SERIAL PRIMARY KEY, name 	VARCHAR(255), email VARCHAR(255));
	This creates a table 'users', with an ID, which is an auto increment, 	which means the first user gets one, the second user gets one, and so 	on.
	We have a name column, which is just text with 255 characters max, 	which means we can't store more in this column, and the same for an 	email column. If we hit enter, the table will be created successfully.
	We can verify the table by using another postgres command, which is:
	\dt
	This stands for display tables.
	We can see that it is working, so now let's insert our first row:
	INSERT INTO users (name,email) VALUES 	('John','John@gmail.com');
	This will insert a row into the users table, which will enter a name and 	email, those values being 'john' and 'john@gmail.com'.
	Make sure to use quotes inside your value parameters, and don't 	forget the ; semicolon at the end of each statement otherwise 	postgres will not execute.
	Let's verify that again by using the select query and select star. We 	want everything from the users table, semicolon, and enter.
	SELECT * FROM users;
	It worked!
	 id | name |     email
	----+------+----------------
	 1  | John  | John@gmail.com
	(1 row)
	This is using SQL through the command line, but we also have 	PGADMIN which came packaged for free with the PostgreSQL install.
	Search your Windows Start for pgadmin, and execute it.
	It will then ask for a password, which is the same as the Postgres user 	password we set (P0stgr3s!).
	We are now connected via PGADMIN to Postgres, and if we select 	the PostgreSQL 17 tab, then databases, and select our named 	database 'test', we can then select Schemas, go to Tables, and here 	we can see our 'users' table. Within this tree we can see Columns, 	which will display all of our columns we have declared.
	However we can't see any of the data we have, to do this, right click 	'users' and select 'View/Edit data' and select 'All rows'.
	This opens up the query tool and we will see what's already in our 	database.
	To add another entry, we just select Add row, or alt + shft+ a, so lets 	add a Jane entry.
	We will notice the save button all the way at the top of the window is 	grayed out, and this is because this is the PGADMIN save button, 	which instead of saving something through the database, it will save 	the entire entries in here into a file, which is not what we want. We 	want to save it to the database, and to do so, we select the Save Data 	Changes button, or press f6.
	We can also just insert other queries as well by right clicking our 	'users' table in the tree, and selecting 'Query Tool'. This opens up a 	clean slate, where for now, we can run:
	SELECT * FROM users;
	And by clicking Execute Script, or f5, this will display our table below.
	You can create more than entries. You can also create an entire table, 	by right clicking 'Tables' in the tree, and selecting 'Create' and 	'Table...'.
	Here we can create a table using the GUI client. Be sure to select the 	right owner, Postgres in our case.
	Lets create a login table, which is something we will need for the 	Smart Brain project. We can select the tablespace, which is a disk 	location where database objects such as tables and indexes are 	stored, as well as add comments to the table.
	If we select the 'Columns' tab at the top, we can add new columns by 	clicking the + button.
	After doing so, we can declare the table column with name, data type, 	and so forth. 
	Let's name this column id, because usually each table has its own id, 	and let's give it a data type of serial, then finally selecting 'Yes' for the 	Primary Key.
	We can actually close all of that because it was for examples sake, 	but this is PSQL and PGADMIN to manage and access/edit 	databases in Postgres.

The most common issue you will find is this: https://stackoverflow.com/questions/11460823/setting-windows-path-for-postgres-tools. This means you will just need to set the path correctly for PostgreSQL:
Step 1:) Goto Control Panel\System and Security\System
