OR another option:
Thanks to fellow student Dimitris for this great guide:
For any of the Linux users following the course and interested in installing PostgreSQL along with a GUI (eg. pgAdmin), their website has wonderful instructions, and so does their wiki (for example, this link is for Debian and Ubuntu based distros).  
Also, one way to issue the commands you typed in the video to start, stop, restart PostgreSQL in Linux is: 
sudo systemctl start postgresql     # starts the server
sudo systemctl stop postgresql      # stops it
sudo systemctl restart postgresql   # restart it
sudo systemctl status postgresql    # check the server's status
The "createdb test " command and the "psql 'test' " command are the same (at least for Debian/Ubuntu systems) from what I saw. 
When it's first installed, PostgreSQL just has the 'postgres' user, and the way to initially enter PostgreSQL is by typing  sudo su - postgres , and then psql .  After Andrei creates the 'test' database, we can create a user with the same name as our current logged in user, to be a database administrator. This way we can just type in psql 'test'  from the command line and enter the database without the need of logging in as the 'postgres' user, just like Andrei does in the lecture. This can be done with CREATE USER your-user-name-here WITH SUPERUSER; , and we can verify that he was created with \du . Now we can exit by typing \q  and then exit , and enter our database just like Andrei does, with psql 'test' . 
Lastly, with pgAdmin4 we need to create a connection with the server the first time we use it, and this is done by right-clicking 'Servers' on the left pane, and choosing 'Create' > 'Server'. We give our server a name, and in the 'Connection' tab we type in 'localhost' as the host, just like Andrei shows in the lecture, and press 'Save'. 
Troubleshooting: (Thank you to contribution from Samuel)
Issue: Homebrew install of Postgresql will not execute successfully. $ brew link postgresql  results in failure due to directory not writable. New version of Homebrew will not allow sudo commands and System Integrity Protection prevents changing permissions. 
Details: I tried to use homebrew to install postgres and kept running into issues with syslink. When I ran $ brew link postgresql  as homebrew suggested, I kept running into an error that it couldn't be completed because certain folders were not writable. I thought this would be easily remedied by running sudo but unfortunately the most current version of homebrew no longer allows the use of sudo commands due to security risks. My next thought was to my root user and use the macOS GUI interface to change the permissions on this folder because I am not sure how to do this on the terminal. Regardless of being logged in as 'root,' the OS would not let me change the permissions of the folder. I also attempted to use sudo and change the permissions in terminal and it did not work either. After several days of banging my head against the wall try all kinds of things  to find a solution, I discovered that since El Capitan, macOS introduced System Integrity Protection aka 'SIP' or 'rootless.' As it turned out, once I disabled SIP, logged back into 'root' and changed my regular accounts permissions to Read/Write on the problem directories, I was able to go back to my regular account and successfully execute $ brew install postgresql . Now it works and I'm so happy to be returning to my lessons!
Steps to Resolve: (WARNING! these steps require you to mess with very critical stuff on your computer. it is best to exercise EXTREME caution when performing this and to revert the system back to its secure state at the end)
Assuming you currently have postgresql installed through homebrew but unable to link due a scenario like the one mentioned above, here is what I suggest to resolve your issue...
1. Run $ brew link postgresql 
2. Write down the directory path that the error says it is not able to write to. (e.g. usr/local/share/man/man7) NOTE: you'll want to actually write this down on paper or take a picture of the screen on your phone because you will not be able to use copy and paste)
3. Enable your 'root' user account if you have not already done so. (https://support.apple.com/en-us/HT204012) NOTE: make sure to make a really good password for this account and write it down somewhere safe. This is a powerful account and there's no way to recover the password.
4. Disable System Integrity Protection. (https://www.igeeksblog.com/how-to-disable-system-integrity-protection-on-mac/)
5. Log into 'root' user account
6. In Finder menu bar select GO > GO TO FOLDER... (CMND + SHFT + G) and type in the path from Step 2.
7. Right-Click/ Cntrl-Click the folder and select Get Info
8. Click the plus sign at the bottom of Sharing & Permissions
9. Add your regular account to the list and change the permission to Read & Write
10. Go back to your regular account, run $ brew uninstall postgresql , then $ brew update  and $ brew doctor . If those are all set run $ brew install postgresql .
11. You should be able to install without any problems now. However, if you run into a linking and permissions problem again, run $ brew link postgresql to figure out the problematic directory and repeat Steps 5 - 10 with whatever other directories are giving you trouble.
12. If everything is up and running properly. It is probably best to at least enable SIP again (instruction in the article linked in Step 4).
(To check that everything is working. I recommend running $ brew services start postgresql  then $ createdb 'test' . In my case, it was when I originally tried to run createdb and got "command not found" that I realized something was wrong.)
336. SQL: Create Table
	Let's create a table. To do that we can use this syntax format:
	CREATE TABLE table_name (column_1 datatype, column_2 datatype, 	column_3 datatype);
	If we enter this, we will get an error type 'datatype' does not exist. 
	Datatype is referring to what Posgres or our relational database knows. We 	have to say ahead of type what type of data each of the columns holds. We 	can see these at:
	https://www.techonthenet.com/postgresql/datatypes.php
	We have a few data types that we can hold. String datatypes, numeric 	datatypes, and date and time datatypes.
	For a numeric data type We have integer which is a 4 byte integer. We have 	bigint which allows for a bigger number and all these different things, we also	have money which is currency value.
	The reason we have to give this datatype is because of the way databases 	work. They're optimized to make sure that as long as everything is the same 	in their column they're able to work really really fast to perform actions.
	So let's use some of these. Lets name our table users, and lets make our 	first column 'name' which we can use the 'text' datatype for, and our second 	will be age, which we will use the 'smallint' datatype for. Smallint is a 2-byte 	signed integer, which means it's max number value is -2^15 to 2^15, 	or -32,768 to 32,768, and nobody is going to be older or younger than this, 	so it will suffice.
	Our third column will be 'birthday' which can have the datatype of 'data'.
	If we press enter, we get our table created successfully.
	         List of relations
	Schema | Name  | Type  |  Owner
	--------+-------+-------+----------
	  public   |  users  | table  | postgres
	(1 row)
	We might be asking how many times do we run these commands. Well 	ideally once you create your database and it lives somewhere on a computer 	somewhere in the world you only run this once.
	You create a table of what you want your database to look like and the rest is 	just filling in and inserting information and reading information off of it. 
	So this is the command that we're going to set up when we create our own 	database to set up our schemas. And then after that it'll just run on its own. 	And It will be able to communicate with the back end.
	\d: This command provides a comprehensive overview of all relations 	(tables, views, indexes, sequences, etc.) in the current schema. It lists all 	these objects along with their types and owners.
	\dt: This command specifically lists all the tables in the current schema. It is 	more focused and provides a cleaner output if you are only interested in 	tables.
	\q: This command is used to quit the interface.
	337. SQL: Insert Into + Select
	We have created our users table but now we want to insert some information. 	With SQL, we have the 'INSERT INTO' command, that we're able to insert 	into whichever table name we want. We specify the column and then the 	values of each of those respective columns, always ending in a semicolon ';'.
	INSERT INTO table_name (column_1, column_2, column_3) VALUES 	(value_1, 'value_2', value_3);
	IN SQL the capitalization is a standard, however, if we input it with 	lowercase, e.g. 'insert into' it will work as well. Let's insert something into our 	table. Keeping in mind we could simply insert into an idividual column, e.g. 	'users', but here we will be inserting into all three.
	INSERT INTO users (name, age, birthday) VALUES ('Dakota', 29, 	'1995-08-08');
	Press enter and it is inputted. If we do \d we can see our table, but how can 	we see the values that we just entered? Our GUI will have it displayed under 	content for our users table.
	To grab this information we can use a psql command: 
	SELECT column_1 FROM table_name;
	SELECT (name, age, birthday) FROM users;
	Or to grab the entire columns without speeling everything out, we can simply 	use the '*' symbol.
	SELECT * FROM users;
	  name  | age |  birthday
	--------+-----+------------
	Dakota |  29  | 1995-08-08
	(1 row)
	Let's add one more user:
	INSERT INTO users (name, age, birthday) VALUES ('Kathleen', 28, 	'1997-02-23');
	We know how to create a table. We know how to insert into a table and we 	know how to grab this information. We can already start picturing what 	powers we can have now with our final project. But there's still a few more 	things we want to learn.
338. SQL: Alter Table + Update
	 INSERT INTO users (name, age, birthday) VALUES ('Seth', 31, 	'1993-10-23');
	We've added one more user so it's a bit bigger of a database to work with.
	Let's say we realized while working on this project that we also needed a 	'score' column. To add a column to a pre-existing table, we can use alter 	table in SQL, using this format:
	ALTER TABLE table_name ADD column datatype;
	ALTER TABLE users ADD score smallint;
	We have now added a column, however the values in this column are null. To add values to a table, we can use these commands:
	UPDATE table_name;
	SET some_column = some_value
	WHERE some_column = some_value;
	UPDATE users SET score = 50 WHERE name='Dakota';
	   name   | age |    birthday    | score
	----------+-----+------------+-------
	Kathleen |  28 | 1997-02-23 |
	Seth        |  31 | 1993-10-23 |
	Dakota   |  29 | 1995-08-08 |    50
	(3 rows)
	Let's say we want to add scores to both Kathleen and Seth, and they have 	the same score, we can do it this way:
	UPDATE users SET score = 100 WHERE name='Kathleen' AND 	name='Seth';
	HOWEVER this will NOT work. We get UPDATE 0. Just like JavaScript, psql 	uses the AND operator to mean both conditions are met, to satisfy the 	executed function. 
	We need to use OR.
	UPDATE users SET score = 100 WHERE name='Kathleen' OR 	name='Seth';
	UPDATE 2
	   name   | age |  birthday  | score
	----------+-----+------------+-------
	 Dakota   |  29 | 1995-08-08 |    50
	 Kathleen |  28 | 1997-02-23 |   100
	 Seth        |  31 | 1993-10-23 |   100
	(3 rows)
339. SQL: Conditional Selections
	Let's add one more user for an even larger database.
	Now Let's learn a few more SQL commands that are useful. What if I wanted 	to grab all users with names starting with 'A'.
	We can simply say SELECT all FROM users WHERE name and this is a 	new one we're learning LIKE. And like allows us to add a condition. In our 	case we can say 'A' And then the percentage sign which means anything 	after a. If I click semi-colon and enter, we get Amber.
	SELECT * FROM users WHERE name LIKE 'A%';
	If we change that to 'a%' we won't get any results, because the conditional 	query IS CASE-SENSITIVE.
	For this reason, consistency is very important when storing data in our 	databases.
	We can also query for all names ending in a digit or letter, like n, as so:
	SELECT * FROM users WHERE name LIKE '%n';
	    name   | age |    birthday    | score
	----------+-----+------------+-------
	 Kathleen |  28 | 1997-02-23 |   100
	(1 row)
	Let's say I want to get everything sorted in order. Maybe from the highest 	score to the lowest score. 
	Well we can select all from users and we can order by the column we want to 	order by. In our case it will be the score. And if we want to do ascending or 	descending- let's do descending first.
	SELECT * FROM users ORDER BY score DESC;
	   name   | age |  birthday  | score
	----------+-----+------------+-------
	 Kathleen |  28 | 1997-02-23 |   100
	 Seth        |  31 | 1993-10-23 |   100
	 Amber    |  35 | 1989-06-13 |    88
	 Dakota   |  29 | 1995-08-08 |    50
	(4 rows)
	test=# SELECT * FROM users ORDER BY score ASC;
	   name   | age |  birthday  | score
	----------+-----+------------+-------
	 Dakota   |  29 | 1995-08-08 |    50
	 Amber    |  35 | 1989-06-13 |    88
	 Kathleen |  28 | 1997-02-23 |   100
	 Seth     |  31 | 1993-10-23 |   100
	(4 rows)
	You saw how fast this is even if this was hundreds of lines it will be really 	good at grabbing information, updating information, maybe ordering it. It is 	extremely powerful. Pretty much since the 1960s it's been just the language 	of choice for databases because it's so good.
340. SQL: Functions
	SQL can also have functions. There are three popular ones that we will 	discuss.
	Right now we have our table users, and it has four users with names, ages, 	birthdays, and scores.
	Let's say we wanted to get the average score of the users. We can do that 	with this:
	SELECT AVG(column_name) FROM table_name;
	We pass the column name inside the AVG function, just like in JavaScript. It 	goes FUNCTION_NAME(argument).
	SELECT AVG(score) FROM users;
	         avg
	---------------------
	 84.5000000000000000
	(1 row)
	We also have the SUM() function which will give the sum of the numeric 	values in a column:
	SELECT SUM(age) FROM users;
	 sum
	 -----
	 123
	(1 row)
	Finally, we have the COUNT() function. Say if we want to count how many 	users are in our database:
	SELECT COUNT(name) FROM users;
	  count
	  -------
	     4
	(1 row)
	There's a few more functions that you can use but these are probably the 	most common.
341. Joining Tables Part 1
	We're going to start introducing this idea of connecting the tables and how 	we can do that. Remember Primary Key and Foreign Key.
	I want to create a login table with CREATE TABLE and within this we can do 	a few things.
	CREATE TABLE login (
		ID serial NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,
		secret VARCHAR (100) NOT NULL,
		name text UNIQUE NOT NULL
	);
	We have 'ID' with datatype 'serial', which is an auto-incrementing integer 	value which is equivalent to serial4; a 4-byte signed integer that is auto-	incrementing. This means that each new ID will increment 1, 2, 3 and so on, 	creating unique ID's for each user/row. 
	In this case, we  are using it to create a unique ID for each login entry.
	'Not null' means that this has to be a filled in property.
	Remember when we had the score column in the users table, but hadn't 	added any values so it came back as null, not null means that's not possible 	here. The column must always be populated.
	We also have 'primary key' and setting primary keys when you're creating a 	table is one of the most important things. By saying that the ID here is the 	primary key. It's saying that this is what I want you to access when I'm looking 	for things. Primary key is always unique to each row. And primary key is 	one column per table, usually set to something like an ID or something 	unique like an email.
	Next we have 'secret' which is a variable character of 100. Which means it 	will not be greater than 100.
	VARCHAR(size) is where 'size' is the number of characters to store. 	Variable-length string.
	Our secret is going to be our hash. And it also has 'Not null' which means 	there always needs to be a value here.
	Lastly, we have 'name' which is 'text' and it is 'unique', meaning that if a name 	already exists then we can't insert into it.
	UNIQUE: ensures that the values in a particular column 	or set of columns are 	unique across rows in a table.
	If we run this script, our table is created, and we can then inspect it with:
	SELECT * FROM login;
	 id | secret | name
	----+--------+------
	(0 rows)
	Let's enter some information in here. I'm going to say INSERT INTO login. 	We don't need to now insert anything in the ID because that's actually done 	for us because it's serial, it will just automatically increment. But for the secret 	we can say that secret will be a hash and the name will be some sort of a 	text.
	INSERT INTO login (secret, name) VALUES ('abc', 'Dakota');
	If we run that, it will be input into our table, and an ID will be automatically 	assigned.
	 id | secret |  name
	----+--------+--------
	  1 | abc    | Dakota
	(1 row)
	Lets add one more:
	INSERT INTO login (secret, name) VALUES ('def', 'Kathleen');
	 id | secret |   name
	----+--------+----------
	  1 | abc    | Dakota
	  2 | def    | Kathleen
	(2 rows)
	What if we try to enter Kathleen again?
	ERROR:  duplicate key value violates unique constraint "login_name_key"
	DETAIL:  Key (name)=(Kathleen) already exists.
	Because we said we want 'unique' for name.
	Let's add one more user from our table:
	INSERT INTO login (secret, name) VALUES ('ghi', 'Amber');
	 id | secret |   name
	----+--------+----------
	  1 | abc    | Dakota
	  2 | def    | Kathleen
	  5 | ghi    | Amber
	(3 rows)
	Notice how Amber's ID is 5 now instead of 3, that is because we ran the 	command but were unable to input the data. The ID serial still incremented on 	those iterations, which in our case was two, one error for Dakota, and one for 	Kathleen. 
	However this still doesn't really matter, because these are still unique primary 	keys.
	We have Andrei, Sally and John that also live with Andrei, Sally and John in 	the Users table. When we design databases like this we can now have 	Andrei, Sally and John referencing the users.
	If I wanted to grab Andrei or Andrei's secret which is 'abc', in that case I have 	a foreign key which is this name column in the login. This is the foreign key 	that references the primary key, the 'name' column in our case, because 	these are the unique identifying values in our 'users' table.
342. Joining Tables Part 2
	Now that we have these two tables, we are going to see the real power of 	relational databases.
	Going back to our drawing the most important part of relational databases is 	this idea of schemas, of having tables that are just concerned about storing 	information specific to them such as users, tweets and following and 	connecting them through a primary key and a foreign key.
	We've already created a connection with a primary key and a foreign key, but 	how to we grab information and utilize them.
	If we go back to our table we remember that we ran our query, we created 	the Create login table here and we created a name variable that, when we 	planned this, was going to match whatever the users table had.
	We can use something called JOINs. now with our queries.
	This is a new word in SQL and probably one of the most powerful, because 	we don't necessarily want to have one massive table with hundreds of 	columns, hundreds of rows.
	We don't necessarily want to have one massive table with hundreds of 	columns, hundreds of rows. Databases aren't designed to just have- just like 	an Excel sheet tons of rows and columns and have all that information in one 	place because it's not efficient.
	A good way to design a database is to have these small little tables that are 	just concerned, just like separation of concerns, concerned with their minimal 	needed information. And if something else is needed we can just join tables 	together to grab information.
	If we do \d, we get a List of relations:
	              List of relations
	 Schema |     Name     |     Type     |  Owner
	--------+--------------+----------+----------
	 public | login               | table          | postgres
	 public | login_id_seq | sequence | postgres
	 public | users              | table          | postgres
	(3 rows)
	We know about our login table and our users table, but what is login_id_seq?
	The way relational databases work is when you create a primary key it 	creates another file that contains this primary key.
	We can see that the type is sequence.
	This is a file that most likely we won't access, but internally Postgres is going 	to make sure, because we set this primary key, it's gonna be really, really fast 	to grab data from the login table when we request it.
	Let's say I wanted to get information from 'login' and 'users'. Perhaps I want 	to get Sally's information but I need her 'birthday', 'score', 'age' as well as her 	'secret'.
	By using JOINs we can do this. 
	I can say SELECT star, so everything FROM users, and now if I do JOIN, I 	can say another table name that I want to join with the users. In our case login 	and we have to say ON what are we going to join or not.
	How is it going to know what matches with Login? If I go back here, how is 	the database going to know that I want to match the first line with Andrei, the 	second line with Sally, a third line with John?
	The ON key word allows us to make sure that we match on something. In our 	case it's the name their primary key here and the foreign key here. So let's 	do that ON users.name equals login.name semicolon.
	SELECT * FROM users JOIN login ON PRIMARY_KEY_COLUMN = 	FOREIGN_KEY_COLUMN;
	SELECT * FROM users JOIN login ON users.name = login.name;
	   name    | age |  birthday     | score | id | secret |   name	----------+-----+------------+-------+----+--------+----------
	 Dakota   |  29 | 1995-08-08 |    50   |  1 | abc    | Dakota
	 Kathleen |  28 | 1997-02-23 |   100 |  2 | def     | Kathleen
	 Amber    |  35 | 1989-06-13 |    88   |  5 | ghi     | Amber
	(3 rows)
	We have name, age, birthday, score but also ID, secret, name altogether.
	We've just joined these values and they all match because we've done 	login.name and users.name.
	With JOINs comes great power, now we can have small little tables based on 	what we actually need, and any time something specific is needed we just 	join tables.
	We can do multiple tables, multiple JOINs in order to select the information 	that we need.
	test=# SELECT (id, age, secret) FROM users JOIN login ON users.name = 	login.name;
	    row
	------------
	(1,29,abc)
	(2,28,def)
	(5,35,ghi)
	(3 rows)
	When performing a join query in PostgreSQL (psql), if you encounter an 	ambiguous column name error, you should specify the table name or alias for 	the column to clarify which table the column belongs to. For example, if you 	have two tables with a column named id, you can specify which id you want 	by using the table alias, like table1.id or table2.id.
	test=# SELECT (users.name, id, age, secret) FROM users JOIN login ON 	users.name = login.name;
	         row
	---------------------
	(Dakota,1,29,abc)
	(Kathleen,2,28,def)
	(Amber,5,35,ghi)
	(3 rows)
343. SQL: Delete From + Drop Table
	What if we wanted to delete something. Let's say Dakota wants to just not be 	part of this app anymore and he's going to remove his user. He can send a 	request.
	On the back end we can simply do delete the table name users and then we 	say which user. Well we use the WHERE key word and here say name 	equals 'Dakota', press enter. And with delete, we actually have to do delete 	FROM so delete FROM users WHERE name equals Dakota. And we have 	one delete.
	DELETE FROM users WHERE name='Dakota';
	DELETE 1

	 Seth     |  31 | 1993-10-23 |   100
	 Amber    |  35 | 1989-06-13 |    88
	Now that we're done with these examples, let's delete a table. Lets create 	another table named test. Then do:
	DROP TABLE test;
	test=# \d

		   name   | age |  birthday  | score

	SELECT * FROM users WHERE score > 90 ORDER BY age ASC;
		   name   | age |  birthday  | score

	 name   | age |  birthday  | score
	----------+-----+------------+-------
	 Kathleen |  30 | 1997-02-23 |   100
	 Seth     |  30 | 1993-10-23 |   100

	SELECT age, SUM(score) FROM users GROUP BY age;
	   age  | SUM |
	----------+-----+------------
	    30   |  200 
	    35   |   88 
	You shouldn't really select for anything different than what you're grouping by, 	otherwise you will get back results that aren't necessarily sensible. E.g.
	SELECT name, SUM(score) FROM users GROUP BY age;
	name   | score
	----------+-----+------------+-------
	 Kathleen |   100
	 Seth        |   100
	 Amber    |    88
	We get the 'name's and we get the score, but the SUM doesn't execute 	because it cannot sum separately listed rows. Since we are listing all 3 	names, they all 3 must have a score, and the SUM doesn not have a space 	to populate.

Section 28: FINAL PROJECT: SmartBrain Back-End -- Database
345. Setting Up Your Database
	We're finally combining everything we know into one place and by the end 	of this, we have a unified understanding of how everything works in the 	world of web development.
	We have our front end Smart Brain with the sign in page. We have our 	app, built on React. We also have our server running on port 3000, 	listening to requests, but we don't need our const database object 	anymore.
	We can create our own PostgreSQL database now, and have the server 	interacting with it through SQL, updating everything, and then responding 	to Express, back to the front end.
	BEFORE we actually start to code anything, we have to design our 	database.
	So let's create a database named smart brain. Open up cmd, cd .. twice to 	root, then:
	createdb -U postgres 'smart-brain'
	password:
	psql -U postgres 'smart-brain'
	password:
	It is helpful to envision or think of our three different parts of our app, as 	three different computers. One with the front-end React app, one with the 	back-end Express server, and one with the back-end database.
	Now let's connect to our database via pgAdmin.
	Although databases like Mongo DB are really easy to get started with and 	they're dynamic and you don't have to think too much about when you're 	starting off, I think relational databases offer a really strong way to think 	about how your app should be structured and how everything and all the 	pieces come together. It's a really, really powerful tool that I see the 	industry shifting back to more and more.
	We're not necessarily storing information like LinkedIn or Facebook, 	where everything is a document model. We have users that are 	interacting with each other. But within our app, we can have different 	things. We can have login information. If we expand our app, we can have 	maybe information about the type of images that they've submitted.
	So we are going to want a users table like our object we;'ve created, 	however remember what I said about passwords in the way we store 	passwords, we don't necessarily want to create a user object or a user 	table with all these columns.
	We want to separate things out and connect them through foreign keys. 	So we're going to create a users table and then a log in table. And have 	those tables connected through perhaps the email field.
	CREATE TABLE users ( id serial PRIMARY KEY, name VARCHAR(100), 	email text UNIQUE NOT NULL, entries BIGINT DEFAULT 0, joined 	TIMESTAMP NOT NULL );
	Our id is going to be serial datatype, and this will be our primary key. We 	will also have name, which will be variable characters up to 100. An email 	field, which will be text, and will also be unique so that we don't register 	the same user over and over, and it also cannot be null, this is because 	we're going to use it in our log-in table. We also want to know entries, and 	since it can be a very large number (if someone was dedicated enough) 	we will assign it bigint, and we will also assign it a default value of 0. 	Lastly we will have joined which will take the timestamp from when the 	user joined the database.
	We also want to make sure that we store our login information, the email 	and the hashes, because remember, we're never storing plaintext 	passwords.
	CREATE TABLE login ( id serial PRIMARY KEY, hash VARCHAR(100) 	NOT NULL, email text UNIQUE NOT NULL );
	Login will once again have an id that is serial and the primary key, as well 	as a column 'hash' which will also have a varchar of 100 since we know 	bcrypt always gives us a certain length hash and it'll never be over 100. It 	will be not null because we absolutely need this information, and then 	finally email, which will be the same as the users table, text, and unique, 	and not null. 
	We've got our tables made!
	We understand what our data is going to look like, this is going to be a 	database that will persist no matter if the server goes down, it's going to 	make sure that this data stays put.
346. Connecting To The Database
	Let's connect our database to our server.
	In order to do that there's tons of tools. We're using NPM again and in this 	video we're going to work with my favorite KNEX.js.
	It works with 	PostgreSQL, MSSQL, MySQL, MariaDB, SQLite, Oracle, 	pretty much any relational database. So let's install it.
	npm install knex
	We also want to install the specific package for the database of our 	choice:
	npm install pg
	In the knex documents it tells you exactly what you need to do to connect 	to the database. So let's copy this, go to our server.js. Let's import by 	doing const
	KNEX = require('knex'), 
	and then declaring it to a const.
	const db = knex({
	        client: 'pg',
	        connection: {
	          host : '127.0.0.1', //localhost
	          user : 'carmer95', //add your user name for the database here
	          port: 3001, // add your port number here
	          password : 'P0stgr3s!', //add your correct password in here
	          database : 'smart-brain' //add your database name you created here
	        }
	});
	Run npm start to make sure there's no errors.
	Let's go back to the document page here and copy and paste just a 	simple, maybe select statement to make sure that everything is working. If 	we go back to the left hand side over here we have the query builder and 	select. So let's just grab something from our database. Even though we 	don't really have anything. You see that we just have knex.select and then 	whatever we want to select from the database.
	db.select('*').from('users');
	and if we log it out, we get a whole bunch of something. Good stuff.

348. Registering A User Part 1
	We left off in the last video with this console log of the KNEX query that 	we just did.
	Now this on the right here is what KNEX does. It builds our SQL 	statement for us. So what we just console logged, was the query 	statement that postgres or KNEX created for postgres.
	You can see over here that the builder is an object that has all the 	information we need to speak with the smart-brain and do this query. 
	If I actually want to access something from the users, I will have to do our 	friend .then() because this returns a promise and we get our response. In 	our case it will be data, this data - again we don't have to do Json 	because we're not sending it through HTTP - we get back the data and if 	we just console.log this data let's see what we get. 
	db.select('*').from('users').then(data => console.log(data));
	Save and go down and it's an empty array.
	We don't have any users yet but we're getting a response. So let's do that. 	Let's create a statement to insert users when they register.
	Go to the /register endpoint, and we can see that now instead of using the 	database.users.push, we can just use KNEX!
	db('users').insert({
		email: email,
		name: name,
		joined: new Date()
	}).then(console.log)
	We want to insert an object and this object is going to contain whatever 	we had in the users information. 
	So we want to insert an e-mail, in which case there will be email. We have 	the name, which again has the name that we see from the body. We also 	have the password but remember in our table we don't have the password 	under the users so we'll leave that blank.
	If we go back to our table example here and connect back to here we see 	the user's ID is automatically generated name, email, entries defaults to 	zero so we don't have to enter anything. And joined is the date that 	they've joined.
	So let's just do joined new date and once we insert let's just do 	a .then(console.log) to see what we get back.
	If we use Postman, or our app, and register a user on the /register 	endpoint, that user will then be added to our database. If we go into our 	terminal we can SELECT * FROM users; and see our newly registered 	user data. SICK!
349. Registering A User Part 2
	We've made our database communicate with the server, and we've 	registered our first user.
	Now we want to respond to the Front-End with this new user.
	If this is success, that is, we get a response, we want to do a .then.
	.then(response => {
		res.json(response);
	});
	If we get a response, in that case we want to send a response.Json and 	this response.json will respond with response. That is as long as we use 	something else that comes with KNEX, returning().
	Instead of us doing another select statement and grabbing Ann we can 	just say returning '*' all. 
	db('users')
		returning('*')
		.insert({
			email: email,
			name: name,
			joined: new Date()
		}).then(response => {
			response.json(response)
		});
	This says that users insert Ann and return all the columns.
	If we register another user, we see that our response is now a json object, 	inside an array, of the user after it's registered, or inserted, into the 	database, but it only	returns the user just registered.
	Let's make sure we're just getting the object by doing:
	.then(response => {
		response.json(user[0]);
	We also want to add a .catch on  the end that will catch and show any 	errors. We can even give it a .status(400).
	.catch(err => res.status(400).json(err));
	The issue with this though, is that we are technically giving our information 	about our system, which we don't want. One of the big security principles 	is that you want to make sure that you don't give the client any information 	about your system.
	Instead you would just want to do something simple, like:
		catch(err => res.status(400).json('Unable to register."));
	If we save and go to register a user, we now get the user object as a 	response, which allows us to access the name and entries on the front-	end, so our front-end now shows the Name of the registered user, and the 	default 0 entries.
	If we do a select all statement in our database, we can see the users that 	have been registered.
350. Getting User Profiles
	Let's use KNEX to finish up some of our other routes.
	If we go to /profile/:id, we don't really use this in our app, but it is 	something for future development.
	This is a good example, because sometimes backend engineers develop 	endpoints that might not be used for the front-end, but they might need in 	future installations, such as this one where maybe we might have a profile 	page for each one of our users and they can edit this, and maybe they 	can have a post, a delete, a put.
	With profile/:id, we just want to grab the user, which is very simple with our 	database. We can remove our database function and do:
	db.select('*').from('users').then(user => {
		console.log(user);
	})
	We want everything from the users table, and we can just console.log it to 	test it out. Now if we do a postman request on /profile and do /1 to make 	sure we're targeting a user that exists, id 1, we get in our console a list of 	all of our users.
	With profile ID we want to make sure that it is the ID and not every single user 	that we're grabbing. We can use .where() for this.
	Within the WHERE clause if we go back to KNEX and I want to demonstrate 	to you here that this is what a developer does. They don't know every single 	spec of a library or a package. You simply get really good at reading 	documentation and seeing what you need, such as where, where you pass in 	an object of what we want to check against.
	In our case we want an object where ID is the ID that we received in the 	params.
	db.select('*').from('users').where({
		id: id
	})
	.then(user => {
		console.log(user);
	})
	If we send a request through postman to /profile/1, we get back in our 	console, our user with the id: 1.
	However we want to grab the object of the user so we will do user[0] and also 	since we are in ES6 we can shorten id: id to just id.
	db.select('*').from('users').where({id})
	.then(user => {
		console.log(user[0]);
	})
	It works and returns the user object! Let's clean up the code a little. We can 	remove found as well as commenting out the found condition. And we can 	also change our console.log into a res.json so that is what is responded 	when we send the /profile/id request.
	What if I do a number that doesn't exist? I get 200 OK with no response. 	Because well, we don't have that user. But let's just console log and see if we 	get back a user from this. If I save and send I get an empty array. So it's still 	returning something, it's just telling me that there's no user.
	If we do a .catch And I do the error with response.status 400 and we'll just do 	'Not found'.
	.catch(err => res.status(400).json('Not found'));
	Save this and go back to postman click send again with the wrong ID. I 	still get 200 OK not the error that we catch because in javascript if I open up 	the chrome dev tools, a trick of javascript, if I do Boolean empty array 	(Boolean([]). It's technically true.
	The way we want to check against the empty array we simply say if user dot 	length exists in that case it will be greater than 1 or equal to 1 we'll respond 	with the user. Otherwise we'll respond with our status not found and maybe 	we can also in here say error getting user.
	.then(user => {
		if (user.length) {
			res.json(user[0])
		} else {
			res.status(400).json('Error getting user.')
		}
351. Quick Note: Knex.js Breaking Changes
	Quick Note: Knex.js Breaking Changes
	Heads up! Knex.js just released an update to their library and contains a 	breaking change (breaking changes are major updates that libraries do, that 	will mean that something that may have worked in the past with the older 	version of the library WILL NOT work with a newer version).
	In the next video you will see us working on Updating Entries. The only 	change you will need to make to your code from the video (in case you are 	using version 1.0.0 or higher of Knex.js) is to add .entries to the end of  	entries[0].
	You can see the code snippet and explanation of this change below:
	.then(entries => {
	    // If you are using knex.js version 1.0.0 or higher this now 
	    // returns an array of objects. Therefore, the code goes from:
	    // entries[0] --> this used to return the entries
	    // TO
	    // entries[0].entries --> this now returns the entries
	    res.json(entries[0].entries);
	  })
352. Updating Entries
	We can navigate to our /image endpoint and delete everything but our id const.
	Our image endpoint updates the entries and increases the count. So all we have to do is update, and if we look at the KNEX docs, they have .update().
	db.where('id', '=', 2000)
	.update({
		entries: 
	We want to say that where the ID equals, and it's not 3 equals because this is 	SQL, where the ID equals the ID that we received in the body, we want to 	update the entries to be higher than the entries that is currently there.
	How can we grab the entries from the database and increase it. Again this is 	a problem that's very common and is said of selecting the ID grabbing 	whatever the entries is and updating it. Well we can use something called 	.increment().
	Increment takes the column and the amount we want to increment by. Instead 	of .update, let's use .increment.
	db.where('id', '=', 2000)
	.increment('entries', 1)
	.returning('entries')
	.then(entries => {
		console.log(entries[0].entries)
	})
	If we save and head over to postman, and send a put request to /image, 	containing an object with an id property and a value, we get an error, 	because we need to do:
	db('users').where
	Prior to that edit, it did not know which database we were talking to. If we 	resend our request now, we will get 1 in our console.
	Now that we know this works, we can do a res instead of a console.log.
		res.json(entries[0].entries);
	})
	.catch(err => res.status(400).json('Unable to get entries.'))
	If we remove 'users' from our db() we can test when it errors, and it errors 	properly.
	We got our /image endpoint working! Now onto /signin.
353. Quick Note: Knex.js Breaking Changes 2
	In the next video you will see us working on Sign In. The only change you will need to make to your code from the video (in case you are using version 1.0.0 or higher of Knex.js) is to add .email to the end of loginEmail[0].
You can see the code snippet and explanation of this change below:
.insert({
	  // If you are using Knex.js version 1.0.0 or higher this 
	  // now returns an array of objects. Therefore, the code goes from:
	  // loginEmail[0] --> this used to return the email
	  // TO
	  // loginEmail[0].email --> this now returns the email
	     email: loginEmail[0].email, // <-- this is the only change!
	     name: name,
	     joined: new Date()
	})
354. Sign In
	We have our final end point, sign in, where we're finally able to sign in users.
	We want to create something every time a new user logs in and actually 	capture their passwords or the hash of their passwords. 
	First we need to register a user and grab their passwords. Now for us to do 	this we need to insert into another database. But first let's use bcrypt to hash 	out a password that we get.
	We're going to do the synchronous method of bcrypt because it's simpler.
	var hash = bcrypt.hashSync("bacon");
	bcrypt.compareSync("bacon", hash); // true
	bcrypt.compareSync("veggies", hash); // false
	Keep in mind that with synchronous it means that as this is working 	JavaScript is not going to get executed on the next line.
	We can copy and past the hash declaration into our /register. We want to 	bcrypt the password.
	const hash = bcrypt.hashSync(password);
	Now that we have the password hash we need to make sure we update the 	users as well as the login tables. 
	To avoid this issue of only populating one table, we can use something called 	transactions, which comes with a method transactions().
	Transactions are these code blocks that we can add to make sure that when 	we're doing multiple operations on a database, if one fails, they ALL fail.
	If for some reason, I can't enter something in the users table, but I can in 	login, as long as it's wrapped around a .transaction they both fail so that I 	never have these inconsistencies.
	To implement this we can do something like this:
	db.transaction(trx => {
		trx.insert({
			hash: hash,
			email: email
		})
	})
	Db.transaction and now we have a transaction in KNEX and if we go to the 	KNEX they have transactions in the docs and you can read about them, and 	SQL has transaction as well. 
	If we go back this transaction gets a trx parameter that we can now use 	instead of 'db' to make sure that whatever we do is a transaction. 
	The first transaction we want to do is that we want to insert into the users or 	into the login, the hash and the email, so we can say hash: hash that we just 	received from bcrypt, and then email which we just got from request dot 	body.
	Instead of doing the return db('users') syntax, we can do something 	called .into().
	.into('login')
	.returning('email')
	.then(loginEmail => {
	})
	We can do into login and then returning the email of the user. Now that we 	have this returned we can say dot then email because we're returning the 	email. And just to keep it separate from the request or body we'll say that this 	is the loginEmail and the login email will now be used in here.
	Now we can copy and pase our return db('users') block into the .then that 	takes loginEmail.
	.into('login')
	.returning('email')
	.then(loginEmail => {
		return db('users')
			.returning('*')
			.insert({
				email: loginEmail[0].email,
				name: name,
				joined: new Date()
			})
		})
	})
	We can use the loginEmail after we've updated the login table to 	update/insert the users email
	In order to make sure that these are both part of the transaction instead of 	doing DB here we use the TRX object.
	return trx('users')
	If we save and register a new user, we will get their user object returned in 	the req.body, however they are not added to our database login or users 	table, if we check.
	The last thing we want to do is at the end, and you can read about this in the 	KNEX section, we can do dot then TRX commit.
	.then(trx.commit)
	We weren't able to send these because we didn't say that if all these pass 	then commit send this transaction through.
	If we do dot catch, we can do TRX roll back.
	.catch(trx.rollback)
	So now let's give this a go. If we click send, we get a response, and we 	get the user registered to both the login and the users tables.
	So this transaction is probably the trickiest part to get used to. But once 	you get the syntax it becomes very easy.
	You create a transaction when you have to do more than two things at 	once, and you use this TRX object instead of the DB now, to do these 	operations. In our case we insert it into login, it returned the email, and 	then we use the login e-mail to also return another TRX transaction to 	insert into the 'users' and responded with JSON.
	Then in order for this to get added we have to make sure that we commit 	and then in case anything fails we roll back the changes. If I enter the 	same user again I get unable to register. If I send the wrong information I'll 	get unable to register.
	Now we can go to our signin endpoint, and remove everything inside it.
	db.select('email', 'hash').from('login')
		.then(data => {
			console.log(data);
		})
	We can then do a db.select to select the email and hash from login. Then 	we will take the data and console log it. If we send a sign in, we get the 	emails and hashes of each user in an object. This is because we haven't 	selected specific criteria using where.
	.where('email', '=', 'req.body.email')
	Now we get only the object of the user we sign in as. Now we need to 	check the password by using the bcrypt compareSync function.
	.then(data => {
		const isValid = bcrypt.compareSync(req.body.password.
		data[0].hash);
			if (isValid) {
			db.select('*').from('users').where('email', '=', req.body.email)
			.then(user => {
				res.json(user[0])
			)}
			.catch(err => res.status(400).json('Unable to get user.'))
		}
	})
	.catch(err => res.status(400).json('Wrong credentials.'))
	Within data here we can grab the array which is the first one and use 	bcrypt. We can do bcrypt.compareSync with whatever the user entered. In 	this case, req.body.password. With the hash, in our case we can just say 	data zero dot hash. If this returns true well let's just have a const isValid to 	represent it as true or false.
	We'll do a res.JSON with the user information. So we'll have to do a 	db.select star from 'users' dot where email equals to req.body.email then 	we'll get the user.
	Finally within here we can do res.JSON user array first item. And if there's 	some issue getting that then we'll say catch error res.JSON we'll do status 	again 400 .JSON unable to get user, and also down here because we're 	still doing the database search right here, and this doesn't need to be a 	transaction because, well we're just checking, we're not modifying any of 	the database items, in here we can say dot catch if there's an error we 	can just res.status 400 and this time we can say wrong credentials.
	All right let's give that a go. We get 200 OK, but we didn't recieve a user. 	Lets console log user, and we also can fix a common but critical error, 	which is return db.select('*') so we are actually returning something when 	the database is queried.
	Now if we send a valid user through our signin endpoint we get the user 	returned and everything working properly. However if we enter something 	incorrectly, we get hanging. This is because we never entered a false 	condition statement.
	else {
		res.status(400).json('Wrong credentials')
	}
	Now everything works and we have our entire signin flow working. In the 	next video we're finally going to test all this out with our Front-End.

357. What's Next?
Section 29: Production + Deployment
358. Deploying Our Files
	Up until now in this course, we've learned about front-end code, back-end 	code, about databases. Now we have to put something online, not just on 	our computers, but to the whole world. 
	How do we go about actually deploying something to production properly 	so that our apps can become real businesses? 
	Well, let's review what we know. 
	We know that we can right front-end code, that is we use HTML for text, 	we use CSS for the visuals and we use JavaScript to create logic and add 	behavior to our front end apps.
	With our Smart Brain app we use to create react app that allowed us to 	use JavaScript to manipulate the DOM, HTML to add different information 	on the screen, and use CSS to style the app.
	Then we learned about NodeJS. We used Express to create our server. 	And what does the server do? The server allows us to interact with our 	front-end.
	So whenever a user logs in or a user submits a picture, our JavaScript 	code on the front end, our react app, is going to send an HTP request to 	our server, to our node app.
	Hey, the user just signed in.
	Hey, the user just submitted an image.
	Our node server, either logs us into our app or sends the image 	information to Clarifai to get the response back, and then send it to the 	front end.
	We also created our database, right? We needed a place to persist, our 		information, if a user comes back after five days, we need to have a way 	for us to store that information, for them to sign in with their password and 	email.
	We couldn't just leave that on our node server because if the node server 	ever crashed or turned off or stopped, well, that information would be 	gone. We needed a database to store that information and to access that 	information in an efficient way.
	So we've created these three things ourselves, our Front-End code, our 	Back-End code, and we also wrote some SQL statements to create our 	database.
	Which we can connect using the Knex library to our database and use 	those commands to store information into our Postgres server.
	But here's the thing, how do we deploy these things?
	Up until now, we saw GitHub pages, right? We're able to put our websites, 	such as our startup landing page on GitHub pages for free and GitHub 	pages allowed us to just simply serve the HTML and CSS and JavaScript 	code.
	We also saw how to deploy our react apps using create react. So we had 	to have a bit more configuration with react to serve our JavaScript and 	CSS files, but we're able to deploy. But our Smart Brain app now has 	more than just front end code. It has server code. It has database code.
	So how do we deploy that?
	What we're going to do is, well, we're going to use Hiroku to have another 	server and this is what you'll see in most apps.
	You'll usually have your server code, that is your backend code like node 	on a computer dedicated as the server.
	You can use things like Hiroku, you can use AWS, Google Cloud, 	Microsoft Azure, Digital Ocean, LiNode, there's lots of services out there 	that essentially give you computers. 
	Remember, a server is simply a computer. And we put our node files on 	this computer. We run the node file and the server is going to be running 	the entire time, just waiting, waiting to hear from the outside world until 	somebody pings us, makes an HTTP request and we do something with 	it.
	Hiroku is going to give us for free a computer somewhere in the world that 	we can put our node JS files to, our backend code, and it's going to wait 	for us to be able to ping it whenever we want.
	Now we could leave our front-end code, our Smart Brain app, on GitHub 	pages. That totally works. But we're also going to put our front end code 	on another Heroku server as well, just to practice, just to show you how it 	works, even though you could leave it on GitHub pages.
	Finally, we're also going to show you how to create a database, not just 	on your computer, but for production and deployment. 
	Our goal is to move our code from our computers to computers that are 	dedicated to just being alive, staying alive, so that whenever users go on 	our app, whether it's three o'clock in the morning, whether it's noon, 	they can always access the app.
	On Heroku, we're also going to create a dedicated database. Now, a 	database is again, just like a server, is just a computer. But usually when 	you deploy, you want a dedicated database server that focuses on just 	running the database software. In our case, our PostgreSQL software is 	going to be running on a Heroku server.
	I use Hiroku just because it's a simple one to use. But you can do different 	things. 
	You can have your database on Amazon's AWS.
	You can have your front-end code on GitHub pages.
	You can have your node server on Hiroku or Digital Ocean. 
	When it comes to production and deployment, it's a whole nother 	ballgame. It's usually called Dev Ops and where you want to deploy 	things 	is really dependent on the situation.
359. Code Review
	A code review is something that you want to do every once in a while 	when you're working with your codebase because it can always be 	improved.
	One bug that we have is that if we create a new user, and submit an 	image under their profile, then log out, and create another user, when they 	login to the homepage, the image is not cleared, the state is not cleared 	between sessions for our app. To fix this, we can open up app.js.
	We have onRouteChange that gets called when when we sign out. When 	we sign out, all we do is set state to isSignedIn: false. If we look at our 	state, we never clean out the user object. The user object which at the 	beginning was the first user, stays the same, and when the second user 	logs in, it has the first's information.
	One thing we can do to fix this is create an initial state. We will do this 	above our app. And this initial state will contain the entire state object.
	const initialState = {
		input: '',
		imageURL: '',
		box: {},
		route: 'signin',
		isSignedIn: false,
		user: {
			id: '', 
			name: '',
			email: '',
			entries: 0,
			joined: ''
		}
	}
	Then we can simply assign this.state with the initialState object.
	constructor() {
		super();
		this.state = initialState;
	}
	Now simply use this variable to change onRouteChange. When we sign 	out, we want to remove all the information. So instead of doing setState 	isSignedIn to false we can just say that we want to set state to initialState.
	if (route === 'signout') {
		this.setState(initialState)
	} else if (route === 'home') {
		this.setState({isSignedIn: true})
	}
	What else can we do with this code base? There's a lot of things that I 	think we can clean up, for example, it's always important to have a catch 	statement every time you do some sort of a fetch.
	If we go to our Clarifai API call, we see that we're using fetch but we never 	catch if for some reason this fetch response to localhost image ever fails.
	The only thing we catch is we catch if the clarifai API fails.
	A good practice is to always have a dot catch after you do any .then. 	Then we can do something simple like a catch console.log to log out any 	errors we have.
	That's something that will improve our what we call error handling to 	make sure that you don't have errors that happen without us knowing.
	.then(count => {
		this.setState(Object.assign(this.state.user, { entries: count }))
	})
	.catch(console.log)
	That's one thing we can improve, there's also our signin and register 	components. They are very very similar since we copy pasted most of it, 	and the only real difference was the input values and the register form has 	an extra field. We won't fully get into it, but it is pretty simple and there's 	many ways of doing it.
	We can imagine maybe having a shared component, maybe called 'form' 	and this form accepts different props like input types, so that we can set 	and share that component between signin and register.
	So that's something that we can do with our front end just to improve 	some of our errors and some of the duplication of code. We can probably 	even put this into a container component so that App.js App.css and 	App.test.js are all in a container component. But overall the front end 	looks pretty nice and clean.
	As we expand the app we can always move components and make them 	smaller and smaller if we want so we want to reuse them.
	We talked about how we can break down the sign-in and register 	component.
	Maybe inside the image link form, if we go to that component, we can 	divide up the input and the button so that we can reuse it somewhere.
	But for an app of this size I think this is a good enough place to leave this 	application.
	So let's move on to the back-end and see how we can improve the back-	end. You remember here our API, which is connected to a database, and 	we have our four simple routes, and we've kept this a little bit messy.
	We've had everything in just one file server.js. But ideally in any large 	project you don't have just one single file that contains everything.
	Although this is just four routes, you can imagine this getting bigger and 	bigger.
	One thing I like to do in the back-end is create something called a 	controllers folder. And what controllers do controllers are things that 	control whatever happens when an endpoint gets hit.
	I'm going create a new file and this file will say register.js and within 	register.js we can copy this entire function, this entire transaction that we 	use, so that now we have app.post register and we can call the function 	here instead of having that entire block. So let's say that we're going to 	call it register.handleRegister.
	app.post('/register', register.handleRegister)
	So how is this going to work?
	Let's go into the register file and I'm going to paste the function that I just 	copied out of there. Looking at server.js we see that 	Register.handleRegister will get the request response so that when 	register endpoint gets hit I'll receive the request and response and get 	called with it. Like:
	app.post('/register', register.handleRegister(req, res))
	So all we will be doing here is module.exports. If you remember this 	syntax and within here we'll just have a handle register function that we'll 	create. 
	module.exports = {
		handleRegister: handleRegister
	};
	And we're just exporting it and this handle register function will just 	be this 	block of function that we are creating. So we'll call it const 	handleRegister.
	const handleRegister = (req, res) => {...}
	We have the request and response but we don't have DB or bcrypt.
	If I run this function in this file I'll get an error saying I have no idea what 	bcrypt is and I have no idea what DB is and we have two options.
	We can either within this file place the bcrypt and KNEX imports and 	connect to the database, or we can pass these to the function.
	Within the register, we can just do req, res. And this request response will 	receive the request response as well as the database knex and bcrypt.
	app.post('/register', (req, res) => { register.handleRegister(req, res, db, 	bcrypt) })
	This what we called dependency injection we're injecting whatever 	dependencies this handle register function needs.
	If we go back and now change our parameters to include the ones that 	we've stated and save, let's go and see that, yeah, we have modular 	exports, everything looks fine on this end.
	const handleRegister = (req, res, db, bcrypt) => {...}
	If I go back to the server and now within here I do a const the register 	require and we give it a path. In our case it will be controllers and register. 
	const register = require('./controllers/register');
	So let's see if this works. If we register a new , it works. If I go to my 	database and make sure that we have it in there, I can select star from 	users, it's in there, and if I go to the login table I have the email as well.
	So we haven't changed any of our functionality. Everything is still working 	but the only difference is that we've now made register just have one line 	and separated out the register function to just be in this file.
	So that if we 	wanted to have more things added specifically to register we 	separate these concerns of the register logic to be in this file.
	Let's follow suit and do that to the rest of the functions as well.
